P R K F A C E. 

\ THOUGH opposed by a great variety of character, ways, ami 

^ means, and though opposing the defections and apostacies of this 

age, the Christian Baptist continues to extend its circulation and 

to augment the number of its patrons. From llie first number to 

le liist published it has progressively advanced in public reg^aid 

id esteem, if a continued increase of friends and advocates may 

►e considered a good omen. The fifih Vi)lume we are permitted 

to commence under circumstances bt'dl more propitious than those 

inider which we commenced the last. 

When I say that there is no periodical work of the same charac- 
'ter amongst the scores of the day; none conducted on the same 
principles; none directed to objects perfectly similar; none ex- 
hibiting with equal fulness both sides (>f every subject discussed — I 
0nly say what hundreds have alicaily said, and \\ hat all who read it 
\iid other publications of the day, do kiiow. liut when 1 say, I am 
inlkrested and disinterested in the larther progress and'success of 
the work, I must offer an explanation. I am interert' d, because 
I am more and more confidently assured of the truth and import- 
ance at' the general views which it exhibits, and bec:mse I cannot 
doubt but that a state of things, such as it contemplates, roust 
suppl|int the carnal, worldly, and superstitious establishments, so 
popular in this day; each of which owes its origin to an ecclesias- 
tical council and its creed. With these views I cannot look around^ 
even as a spectator, without feeling a high and intense interest in 
its success. I am also disinterestedC inasmuch as I have committed 

^ it to the patronage of Heaven* and am satisfied to await the re- 
sult. And I cannot but think it very unbecoming, when we coao- 
jnit any thing to the guidance, control, or blessing of the Lord of 
All, to feel solicitous about the issue. As to my worldly interest in 
the work, which the weakness and ill will of some have magnified 
into a primum mobile^ I feel no concern. Sixteen years ago tbe 

f devil whispered into my ear that I might get a good benefice in one 
of the honorable sects of tbe day; or if I would prefer a seat in the 
bar or in tbe temple of legal science, I might promise myself « 

Sood little fortune in wealth and fisne. \ Vi^ iV^NV^% ^v^^^ 
mt, poor and inexperienced as I tYieii iKw^lYkaji^icwi^g^^.^'^**^^

contents of the volumes already published will not attest my Inde- 
pendence of roindy singleness of object and aim, and disregard of 
numan applause, except that of doing good, I should fear that 
Teason and argument would be offered in vain. 

The policy, and the measures adopted both by my open, avowec^^ 
and determined opponents; and by the masked, double-minded 
and double-tongued, faltering and wavering adversaries, have in- 
spired me with more confidence in my means and resources — with 
more assurance of the truth and triumphing pretensions of th« 
cause I espouse — with more disdain for error itself, and the low 
cunning, pusilanimous intrigues, and cowardly artifice by which 
it strives to creep into notice, or to hold fast its unauthorized hold 
upon the passions and prejudices of those who will not think, and g 
therefore cannot act for, or from themselves. f 

Amongst all the combatants who have appeared in their proper 
name, or under a mask, in^the "Baptist Recorder,*' the **Western ^ 
Luminary," the "Pittsburgh Recorder,*' and the other ^Uighta^* 
of the day, who has made good a single position, a charge, accu- 
sation, or specification, either against the New Translation or any 
leading point in this work, by any thing like argument, reason, «rj 
testimony? The history of my friend SkUlman^ with his frieiM^ 
Steel, alias "Friend of Truth," alias "Vindex," &c. &c. U the*^ 
history of them all. The winking and hoodwinking, the insinua 
ting and criminating, the masking and unmasking, the fearing aji'd 
doubting, and J wish I had no reason to add, the equivocating an^ 
misrepresenting, of such opponents, only beget doubts in th^ 
bosoms of their own friends, deepen the convictions, and confirm 
the confidence of those who cannot unite nor fraternize with them. 
Let my opponents name the man who has not retreated from tlite 
ground so soon as the troops were marshalled. Let them coujf^ 

how many have even stood till the battle was set in order. Kow 

powerful is truth, and how bold too!! How imbecile is error, and 
how dastardly too!! 

I wish to state it again most distinctly^ that not one of th% essays 
on the **JRe8toratUn of the Ancient Order of Things*' has been 
impugned or seriously objected to from any quarter wh^itever. 
The same, indeed, might be said of almost every leading position 
in the whole work. The truth is, light is increasing. Many of 
those who have opposed us in one way or another, have been con- 
vinced; and some )f those yet opposing would much rather wear 
• the mask than Tisk-their persons. The truth is, they have not 
confidence in themselves. Many too who have been for years 
teaching things which they now know they ought not to have 
taught, are extremely hard pressed between conscience and rab- 
binical pride. Conscience says. Confess your error and reform, j 
Btit the pride that cometh from the sacred desk saya. No; the ' 
people will honor me no more. There is a volume here in one ]| 
sentenee. And since the days of John the Immerser until nov^, •; 
the kingdom of heaven is invaded, and invaders take possession by 
/brce. The laity hsLve had to invade it, and to raise up .captains*

EDITOR. 

— :oS»go:— 

REVIEW OF DOCTOR NOEL'S CIRCULAR. 

A ClIiCULAR letter written for the Fraoklin Asso- 
ciation, Ky. ld'2G, by the reverend Silas M. Noel, 
D. D. on the creed question, was republished in the 
Baptist Recorder, and lately republished in Cincinnati. 
On my late tour I was often told that it was represent- 
ed and held, by the advocates of human creeds, as an 
unanswerable performance; as the best thing ever writ- 
ten on the subject; that it settled the controversy for- 
ever, &c. &c. Hearing it so highly extolled, and 
being so well acquainted with the versatile genius of 
its author, I read it with great attention, and whether it 
was owing to my expectation being too much elated, or 
to some other cause, I vouch not; but, in truth, it ap- 
p^^ared to me much below the ordinary talent oi tne 
writer, and extremely imbecile. It is, indeed, as 
sti'ong in assertion and as weak in argument as any 
piece I have tean on the subject written in the current 
century. It is a condensed view of the Princeton pam- 
phlet, in some of its stronges- positions; but wnen ap- 
parelled in a new dress it is still more awkward and 
unsightly than in the full uniform of Doctor Miller. 
A Baptist Doctor caparisoned capa-pie in a Paido 
Doctor^s regimentals, always appears as unseemly to 
mey as a damsel attired in a soldier^s uniform. 

rhe Doctor's starting point is this — ''Creeds formed 
or entorced by the civil authority, are usurpations lead- 
ing to persecution and to despotism, while those formed 
by voluntary associations of christians, enforced by no 
higber penalty or sanction than exclusion fco\!cv \£l^\^ 
jnemhershjp in the society are not ouV^ X^'wVviX^V^ 
necessary ia the present stale o£ tti^ i^Vv^vonx^ viovV^^ 



4 THE CHRISTIAN 

This is a mere assertion and a distinction without a dif- 
ference. Creeds formed by "voluntary associations'' 
wbetiier convened by tbe state or tbe cburch are alike 
voluntary; alike in their tendency and results. And 
while the Doctor gravely makes the sanctions of those 
voluntary civil deeds greater than the sanctions of the 
voluntary ecclesiastical creeds, they are, in fact, and in 
effect, the same. Tbe sanction of such creeds as the 
Doctor advocates, he kindly and politely calls ^^rnere 
exclusion from membership*' in the kingdom of heaven, 
while the sanction of the creeds he condemns is worse 
than mere exclusion from that kingdom; that is, civil 
pains; such as confiscation of goods, exile, imprison* 
meat, or death. So that exclusion from temporal ad- 
vantages is much greater in the Doctor's view than 
exclusion from the kingdom of heaven. The Doctor, I 
admits does not speak out so explicitly as he would do 
in a belter cause. He does not like to appear, on this 
occasion, with Peter's girdle and the keys of the king- 
dom of heaven dangling on his loins. This he knew 
would illy comport with the leathern girdle of the Har- 
bmger, and would not suit the spirit or taste of the age. 
But when we draw aside the Doctor's surplice we shall, 
under the leathern girdle, see the mighty keys, some- 
what rusted it is true; but cast in the good old Roman 
}nould, with the sublime initials of P. M. V. I. C. with 
the good old motto, "i^rocw/i^rq/anc*"— Hence yc pro- 
fane. 

The short metre of the Doctor's music is this: Our 
church is the church of Jesus Christ called, in tire N. 
Testament, "the kingdom of heaven," and all who are 
worthy members of it, shall be worthy members in the 
kingdom of glory; all who are justly excluded fiom it, 
are justly excluded from the kingdom of glory — be- 
cause we act by the authority of the great Kii»g> J^nd we 
all allow that the great King will not exclude, nor allow 
to be excluded, from his kingdom on earth, such as be 
will receive into his kingdom of glory. 

I know how the Doctor would try to save himself here. 

He would tell us that he does not consider his church as 

r^^ff oa^jr church of Christ, and he will very courteously 

and Jcj'ndljr teU all his orthodox neighboriBf; cVivxTeW^

liis own; and that by bis sanctions to his creed, he means 
no more than to teli the excluded that he is not quite 
8o good company as he could wish, but that he can be 
accommodated equally well with a place in some other 
good natured church of Christ; and that he hopes to 
meet him in the heavenly kingdom though he has some 
objections to fraternizing with him 'Sn the present stai6 
of the religious world y Here Doctor Miller and Doe- 
tor Noel politely and graciously shake hands, and bid 
each other good bye. And in parting say: Tou, dear 
Doctor, keep your church pure from me by your creed, 
and I will keep my church pure from you by my creed; 
but, God bless you, dear brother Doctor, for although 
^4ij the present state of the religious world" it is fitting 
that you should commune under your creed, and I under 
the banners of mine, I do believe we shall commune in 
heaven together and be both welcomed there by the 
great King as good and faithful servants; /for excluding 

iyoUy and you for excluding me. The Princeton Doctor 
says, O dear Doctor NoeJf I will receive you into my 
' pure communion, will you not receive me!!^ The Doc- 
tor of Oakley rejoins, Farewell, Doctor Miller; I thank 
you for your assistance in the creed question; but while 
you rantize these little puklings I do not like to sit by 
your sidc'^excuse me, dear Doctor, I love you and we 
will both feast at the same table above. 

In this pithy, polite, and good natured way, our 
Baptist Doctor excuses himself for all the sanctions of 
his creed^which has neither proscription^ nor persecu- 
tion; tyranny, nor. usurpation; but a little good natured 
chicanery. 

But to quote Horace once more, as I know one Aleph 
in Kentucky, who has a dictionary of quotations. 

^^8ed tamen amoto queramus seria ludo\^^ 
let us come to the starting point again. The Doc« 
tor begins this puissant circular with a petltio princxpii^ 
and ends with an argwnentum ad vevecundiam But for 
the present we shall canvass his Alpha or his Aleph, and 
leave Omega till another day. To tell the naked truth 
with that candor and simplicity which I desire always to 
be characteristic of my pen — il\si\\dLO\^\m^\.%"«^J^\^- 
rj from Grst to last. And 1 d\4 ^\^\i n^N^^ V>\i^ ^^^*

tr notice this letter, because of my personal regard for 
its author. But when solemnly called to the task, we 
must know no man after the flesh. I will then, as far as 
in me lies, repress this pen of mine from all irony or 
saMre, and with the utmost gravity examine the capital 
assumptions of the writer. 

It i^ assumed that mere exclusion from membership in 
a society claiming the high title and character of a church 
of Jesus Christ, is a sanction to a human creed of no 
such great moment as the persecutions and proscriptions 
which sanction human creeds framed by civil power. 
This is obviously a fundamental error. The excluded 
are generally proscribed to the utmost extent of the ex« 
cluders. If it be so that the excluded from any church 
in the government, are not injured in their political 
character and standing, we have reason to thank the 
liberality and indep/cndence of those who brought about 
such a state of civil society, and not the creed nor the ^ 
priest which excludes. But I do most sincerely think j 
that it is no small matter, no *'wicf«" little thing to be ^ 
' solemnly proscribed the kingdom of heaven by those 
little idols which the sects worship, whether authorized 
by letiers patent from the Sceptre or from the Mitre- — 
And however we maj choose to word it, when we de- 
sire to carry our point with guile, to exclude a man 
from "mere membership'' in the church is an act of the 
snost awful import, and unless sanctioned by the great 
King and head of all authority and power, it is an usur- 
pation and a tyranny, than which there is not any mor^ 
heinous. 

Again the Doctor assumes thaf creeds formed and 
enforced*'* by a voluntary association are laufnL But 
he has forgotten to lay before us the law and the testimo* 
ny. To assert that such are lawful is not enough — we 
want to see the law. But this cannot be shown, and 
therefore we cannot see it It may be lawful in the 
civil code of Kentucky or of Scotland; but we are not 
to be satisfied with civil statutes in matters of this sort. 
Xiet 128 have a Divine law authorizing a voluntary asso- 
cJatjon to farm and «n/orc6 any religious creed, and we 
trj/j jrie/d the point at issue. But uatil V.V\s Vs ^oti^ v^«k 
ous^ n'eit the assumption as perfectly graluWous. 



BAPTIST, 7 

In the next place the Doctor assumes that churches 
are ^^voluntary associations^^ These terms ought not to 
pass current until tried. Human establishments of a 
sectarian character, maj, perhaps, be called "voluntary 
associations j'^^ because begotten and born of the will of 
man. But I am far, Tery far, from granting that the 
church of Jesus Christ is a ^^voluntary associationV 
Men and women, it is true, ought to become members 
of it with their own consent. But the constitution and 
laws and institutes of this society are not at our option 
nor rejection. No man can reject, or new modify, or 
refuse them obedience, and be guiltless. "So man is 
4dlowed of his own will and free consent to make a 
church covenant, to decree church laws, or institute 
any religious observance . I wish for a definition of the 
terms voluntary association when applied to the church 
of Christ. I promise to show that if the Doctor attempts 
this he either refutes his own circular or directly assails 
Ithe New Covenant or constitution of the kingdom of 
keaven. 

I offer these remarks upon the Doctor's starting point 
alone. Ills letter wants method, be ought first to have 
given his definition of a creed, and then to have given 
the law and the testimony. But he begins as I have 
noticed and then gives his definition. His definition I 
tvill attend to in my next. 

EDITOR. 

DEISM AND THE SOCIAL SYSTEM.— JVO. ///. 
KOKE of the gentlemen Free Thinkers, none of the Deisticd 
Philosophers of the city of **Mental Independence^*' nor any where 
else, as far as I have seen, hare, as yet, either deigned or ventured 
to meet me on the premises submitted in my last. Gentlemen, 
this will not do. This will neither comport with the artificial 
d^gni^ of your profession, with the ground you have assumed, 
nor with the awful magnitude of the subject. You have erected 
a temple, in which you have constructed a throne, and on it you

If I may judge fVom all the samples I have erer seen of the whole 
of your resources, gentlemen, I must think your cause the most 
desperate of all causes, ever plead at midnight or at noon. You 
liave no premises, and how can you have any conclusicn?^ If ^ou 
have premises, let us see them explicitly and definitely laid down. 
"Your friend, **The Inquirer " of whcm I have made D.ention, in 
all the pieces I have seen from his pen, has given us not so much 
as an axiom, postulatum, or proposition. The sum of his first 
number is, that he was once a true behever in revelation, and that 
he is now a true unbeliever; and the reason he gives for being an 
unbeliever is, that **he could not help finding traces of ignorance 
in the scriptures.'* The only conclusion 1 can draw from his first 
essay is, that he was once a true believer without evidence, and 
he is now a true unbeliever without reason. My conclusion I 
contend is perfectly logical, fur he g^ves no reason why he once 
believed, and I will shew he gives us no reason why he now disbe* 
lieves. A person who tells us that he was a true believer of a liCf 
mean5, 1 suppose, that he was a sincere believer of a lie, and intends 
that we should consider him at that time as a dupe cf others. 
Indeed he p-ublicly professes himself to have been once the dupe 
of others; for he sincerely believed what he now acknowledges to 
be a lie. I'his will not prove, either on Aristotle's or Lord Bacon's 
plai\, that he is not. now the dupe of others or of himself. For if 
he once sincerely believed a lie, it is neither absurd nor impossiblal 
to suppose that he now sincerely believes a lie He would hav« 
fiaved himself of many a blunder, ami us of a little trouble, if he 
bad told us all the evidences on which he once believed the lie, 
and had contrasted them with all the evidences on which he now 
believes what he calls truth. The fact, however, is, that he has 
now no faith at all, either true or false; for faith •withont testimony . 
cannot exist, ^ind as he has no testimony that the Bible history is 
iintrtie^ he cannot believe that it is not true. For a man to say that 
be believes the Bible is not true, is just as incongruous as for a 
snan to say, / see without tight. Now as there is no opposiko 
testimony to that of the Jewish or Christian historians, no man caa 
say ihat he believes the Bible history to be false. And here Iqt 
mj ask in passing- this quaq mire, Is there any cotemporary historiak 
wi h I^Ioses, Matthew, Mark, Luke, or John, who contradicts their 
tes imony? If so, produce it.

now as he admits he once was. He was once a true behever wiXhJt

responsible person, has now presented himself before the public, _

assertions, or to recal the remarks he has made. There are

deeply interested in the calm and able discussion of those point! 
which now cause so much emotion. ^ '

LETTER I. 

Moon^eld, Ky, May, 1^27. 
Brotheti Campbell, 

BEING desirous to see, in our denomination, urdty of hearty 
of sentiment and exertion, I have thought proper to address you 
in the loose style of epistolary writing, as one who is eminently 
qualified to do good. As I have but little leisure for either read- 
ing or writing, and withal, labor under continual infirmities of body, ^ 
you will please excuse my inacuracies of style or expression; and 
ret^ard me as a friend who approaches you unmasked, undisguised^ 
open and free. 

The church of Christ is compared to a human body. If one 
member suffer, they all suffer — if one be honored, all are honored. 
Of this body we all are members; we all f»ave the same ru/e, the 
New Testamtnt; the same master, Jesus Christ; the same hope '^ 
and calling; and we all should be of one mind, and speak the same 
things. But this is not the case; every one has his "doctrine and 
his psalm," schism exists, divsions are fomented, and party feeling 
aroused. I allude to the effect produced by your writings, oratioiii^ 
and lectures. To this fact, your Christian Baptist bears testimony. 
Some are for you, others agmintt you; some beheve, others rejeoli 
some approve, others censure and condemn. Such is the state iof 
»fikirs; such the effect prodaced by your writings. But let ne 
ask, What is the great good which such divisions will achieve? 
Will the disciples become better christians, love each other mofe 
fervently, be more'humble and fiuthful? I fear not. A bouse dirt 
ded agamst itself cannot stand; if we bite and devour each other* 
shall we be more prosperous, more happy, or exhibit a brighter 
ex&mple of christian forbearance, brotheti^ love, and charity? 
Y( u will Sky no. What then is to be done? m what manner shall 
ve fulfil the law of Christ? Vave we no bowels of compassion, no 
sympathies for the church in the wilderness? I hope you have« 
tltatjrou nould rejoice to see vihat 1 desirt, and what cimBtitutcs 
the bLTden of my messBge at a throne of grace. Come then, ngr

1 have written either of you or your opinions. Except on the sub- 
ject of experimental relig^ion, 1 have neither censured, condemn-

correspondents; and even on that subject I spoke with caution; I

queifted an explanation. This you did not thinrlc proper to give; 
and hence, our correspondence was closed. I now resume it 
under a dilferent form, and on my own personal responsibility. 

You object to creeds and confessions; and for the very same rea-

nave, in part, formed a new creed; not a lifeless inefHcient one- 
no, not BO; but one which as effectually influences the conduct of 
your abettors as any confession of faith. Your creed, I 'mean your 
vritingi, is not the Bible — is not the rule of conduct prescribed by 
Jesus Christ and his apostles; and yet it is manifest that those who 
embrace your view^ of divine truth and conduct, are governed by 
them. Ob this subject I shall enlarge in my next; in the meafk 
time thmk on what 1 say. Though I may not possess your talents, 
leisure, or acquisitions^ yet 1 hope to show you in the sequel that 
your breUiren who reject your opinioni^ deserve your love and 
respect. Consider me not an enemy, but a fiiend, a brother. 

Observe, between you and your Baptist brethren there Is no 
diflTerence of opinion as to the rule of fkith and practice. On this 
aubject we all speak the same language; we all acknowledge the 
same authority; all profess to be goFemed by it What, then, is 
the diflTerence between us? Simply tkis: We cannot agree as to 
wiiat the Bible teachet. The Baptists think the Bible teaches the 
doctrine contuned in their creeds; you Uiink it teaches what you 
have written and published, and what you will hereafter write and

REPLY TO THE ABOVE. 

JSrother Clack, 

I CANNOT but express ray astonishment at the greatness 
of your charity in saluting me "brother.** Having been for more 
than one year the constant object of vituperation and detraction, 
of obloquy and misrepresentation in your paper; to be addressed 
by you as brother, sounded as wild in my ear as did cousin in the 
ears of the fox when seized by the dog. *Tis true your editorial 
articles were extremely mild; but while you gave free and full 
scope to every anonymous reviler, while your columns were sur- 
charged with the very lowest scurrility and personal abuse, and by 
those too who dare not shew their face; your editorial moderation 
only served as a little seasoning to the dish; and your dexterity in 
selecting and extracting from every source such matter as would 
amalgamate on the doctrine of affinities with your original cavaliers, 
only served to evince the sincerity of j^our intentions and the 
firmness of your efforts to put me down and the cause which I 
arlvocate in the estimation of your readers. You have certainly 
learned that I am extremely good-natured, or else you have sin- 
cerely repented of the error of your way. If the latter be the fact, 
and you are determined to reform; and as you seem determined to 
pray for the forgiveness of the evils you have committed against 
the cause of God and truth, my religion teaches me to forgive; 
and therefore, so long as you evince sorrow for the past, and 
promise to do better for the future, I will call you Brother Clack. 
Well, then, brother Clack» what is all the evil I have done in 
my "writings, orations, and lectures," for which you would have 
xne join you in your prayers for remission? You tell me "divisions 
and schisms" exist. This is true; but whether my ''writings, 
orations, and lectures," are to praise or blame, or neither, for 
these divisions and schisms, is a question not so easily decided. 
The gospel of the Lord Jesus, his preachings and teacliings, or 
his orations and lectures, together with those of his apostles, 
caused much division, schism, and persecution. But whether 
they who proclaimed liberty to the captives, the opening of prisons 
to them in chains, the recovering of sight to the blind, and the 
year of acceptance with the Lord-— were to blame for these evils; 
or whether the opposing party who contradicted and blasphemed, 
who slandered and persecuted the Lord and his apostles, is a 
question that would not, I think, puzzle you a long time to de- 
cide. And if any of the Pharisees or other praying people of that 
age had requested the apostles to join them in prayer for the for- 
^Jvenes3 of the erih they had done* referring to schisms and 
aivjsions, itia a question whether 2^y of tViem wo\AdVivi^ U^^ed 
€ae kaec, which would not require me loag to deQ^%

tiro to be more general, whether they are to be permanent, or 
whetlier jfood or evil, are questions on which every man will think 
for himself accordinj*' to the bent of his feeUngs, prejudices^

What good effects are to result to society from the many religiou?i

As to what you say concerning the evils of division amon;2^st

tional grounds, I would travel on foot a hundred miles to see him 
and to confess my faults to him. 

The intelligence, ptirity, and union of all who acknowledge the 
mission of Jesus our Loi-d, and the conversion of sinners to him, 
are, with me, the mtignum bonum, the grand ultimatum of all my 
»« writing:'), orations, lectures;" and social prayers. On this ground 
J object to all your little human creed books, which yourself and

ftdraire the frivolous, IS^ht, and fantastic style, which clKiraclcrizea 
the incubations of your «*Aleph.** Wit and humor have their 
tdmirers— satire, and even dechnnation will not always disgust; 
but there is a style which is destitute of all these, and pleases none 
but the vitiated taste of those who never had, or have lost a true 
standard of appreciation. I reserve my remarks upon your, anil 
the Doctor's definition of creeds, until my next on his circular;^ m 
which I will shew that you both have abandoned the cause which 
you think and profess to advocate. 

I have advanced many arguments in this work against creeds^ 
which none of your writers have even noticed, ana which I am 
sure none of them can set aside. And I have solid and substantial 
objections to them, which, I presume, no man livmg can remove. 
But it is creeds, in the legitimate and established sense of the word 
in ecclesiastic usage; and your not defending them, but changing 
the use and acceptation of the term, proves to the intelligent and 
discerning reader your embarrassment and impotency; but amongst 
those who cannot distinguish arg^ument from declamation, whose 
passions and prejudices are strong, and whose judgment and 
powcFS of reflexion are imbecile, any thing that pleases their taste 
passes for logic profound and unanswerable. 

I do attribute to creeds, in the proper acceptation of the term, 
all the divisions and strifes, partyism and sectarian feeling, of the 
present day; all the persecutions and proscription, all the navoc of 
human life, and all the horrors of the inquisition in the cause of 
religion^ during many centuries before we were born. I attribute 
to them and the councils which gave birth to them, the greater 
part of the ignorance and superstition, enthusiasm and debates, 
and even the schisms and divisions of which you lament in the 
present day. I have yet to meet with the first church which holds 
a human creed with inflexible rigidity, and which is enlightened 
in the Holy Scriptures. The stronger the faith in human creeds 
the weaker the attachment to the Bible, and tlie greater the 
ignorance of its contents. This is, at least, in truth and fact, the 
result of my experience and- observation. 

But the peace, the harmony, the union, and love of christian^ 
the purity and jo^ of the household of faith, can only be pro:noted 
by a devout, spiritual, and unwearied attention to the lively oracles 
-~no dry bones, no lifeless skeleton, no abstract miniature of 
doctrine, no cold formula of discipline, ever, brother Clack, pro- 
moted peace with God, conversion to God, harmony, union, and 
love amongst christians. Search the records of time and you will 
£nd ijg^norance, superstition, tyranny, division, and schism, on the 
one Side. Humility and christian affoction, spirituality and true 
charity amongst the leaders, expired in the Council of Nice, when 
ttie first creed received the imperial subscription. 

You will find the Lord Jesus at the head of those who have opposed 

human creeds. Bver since the da^ that he lifted up his voice and

commandments of God. From that day to this, creeds and creed- 
makers are anathematized from Heaven. Innocent and harmless as 
you suppose them, they are a root of bitterness, and justly con« 
demned by all in Heaven The prayers of the martyrs under the 
altafy the l^lood and tears of those who refused subscription to 
pagan, Jewish, papal, and protestant creeds, cry aloud for ven- 
geance on those who framed them and on those who executed 
them. Many thought they did God service when they made them, 
and that they were necessary for tlie unity and purity of their 
church; yea, they thought they did God service when they killed 
them that opposed them, and stoned and gibbeted them who 
would not subscribe them. But you see their error, and cannot 
see your own, brother Clack. 1 would not be found in your ranks, 
neither as a commander nor a private, for all the fertile soil of your 
state — for all the honor which all ypur population could bestow. 
I would rather be in the ranks of the martyrs, at the head of which 
stands the illustrious chief who was crucified rather than subscribe. 
Yes, I desire to be with them living and dying. And when the 
hour of his indignation comes, when the awtulday comes when he 
will answer the prayer from under the altar, may the thoughtless 
and inconsiderate advocates and ubctters of a system essentially the 
same, find pardon and refuge in him. 

I have only to request you to reciprocate the favor or the act of

me to be most sincerely attached to every one who loves my Lord 
and Master, whether Baptist or- Paido-Baptist, New Light'or Old

A RESTORATION OF THE ANCIENT ORDER OF THINGS, 

.VO. XX, 
THERE is no trait in the character of the Saviour more clearly 
marked, more forcibly exhibited in the memoirs of his life, than 
liis unreserved devotion to the will of- his Father and his God. 
Mow often do we hear him say, *'I came not to do my own will, but 
the will of him that sent me." "It is my meat and my drink to do 
the will of him that sent me, and to accomplish his work." The 
mottD of his life was sung^by David in these words: <<To do thy will, 
O God, i delight." An unfeigned and unreserved submission to, 
a perfect acquiescence in, and a fixed unalterable determination to 
do, the will of the Most High, is the standard of true devotion, 
and the rule and measure of true happiness. Hence, let me ask, 
arose this devotion to the will of the Father in our Lord and Sa^ 
viour? We answer. Because he knew the Father. He knew that 
iSod is, and was, and ever shall be love, and he received every ex- 
pression of his will, whether pleasing or displeasing to flesh and 
blood, as an exhibition of God's love. He kivevr loo t\\^kt. i\\^^^ 
was no love like the love of God, either inixaVvrctw ^e\f\^^, "W^- 
love of God 19 a love emanating from^ iucot^«w\Ji5k \C\^^ "W^"^ 

2S V. 5. 



18 THB CHRISTIAN 

measured by, an infinite wisdom, and omniscience. Human affect' 
ion is often misplaced and misdirected, because of human ignorance 
and human weakness. The love of some men is much greater 
titan thttt of others, because of the strength of their natural en- 
tlo\v:nents. But as the wisdom and knowledge of God are unsearch- 
able, so his love never can be misplaced, misdirected, never can be 
measured, nor circumscribed. It is perfect in nature, and in 
nature it is wisdom, power, and goodness combined. In degree it 
cannot be conceived of by a finite mind, nor expressed ia our im- 
perfect vehicles of thought. It passeth all created understanding. 
It has a height without top, and a depth without bottom. Eveiy 
oracle of God, is a manifestation of it. As the electric fluid per- 
vades the earth and ail bodies upon it, but is invisible to the eye 
and imperceptible ta the touch; but when drawn to a focus in a 
cloud by its Jaw of attraction, and when it is discharged to another 
body which requires more of it than the point from which it ema- 
nated, it assumes a new form, and a new name, and becomes via- 
ble to the eye, and its voice is heard. Every expression of the 
will of God, every commandment of God, is only drawing to a 
certain point, and giving form and efficacy to his love. It then 
becomes visible — it is then audible— We sec it — wc hear it — we 
feel it. 

The very term devotioii has respect to the will of another. A 
devoted or devout man is a man wlio has respect to the will of God. . 
When a person is given up to the will of any person, or to his own 
will, he is devoted to that person or to himself. Ikit as the term 
devout is used in religion, we may say that every man is more or 
less devout, according to his regard to the will of God expressed 
in his holy oracles. The Saviour was peifcctly so, and he is and 
ever shall be the standard of perfect devotion. Kot an item of the 
-will of Goil found in the volume of the old book Mi'iticn concern- 
ing him, that he did not do, or fcubmit t<»; not a single command-

arc therefore dc\out, or devoted to the will of God, and the un- 
I'cgencrate care nothing about it. Now e\ery one that is devout, 
or devcted to the will of (iod, will continually be inquiring into 
the will of God. Hence his oracles will ahvays be their meditation. 
I'very regenerated man will liicrefore be devout, devoted to the 
revealed will of God, will seek to know, and understand, and prac- 
tise them, therefore every regenerated man will be a fnend and 
idvocute of the ancient order of things, in the church of the Living 
God, uec:iu3ethat order was according to the will of Gjd, andev-

en to this similitude-* 

A householder who had one son and many servants was about to 
depart on a long* journey to a distant country; he called his son 
into his presence, and said to him. My son, I am about to be ab- 
sent for a long time; yon know I have a vineyard, and an olive 
yard, and an orchard of various kinds of fruit. These I have cul- 
tivated with great care, and have kept my servants employed in 
fencing, and in cultivating each of them with equal labor and care. 
I now give them and my servants into your care and management 
until my return, and I now command you to have each of them 
fenced, and pruned, and cultivated as you have seen me do, and 
at my return I will reward you for your fidelity. He departed. His 
son calls all the servants together, and having a predilection to the 
g^pe above every other fruit, he assembles them all in the vine* 
yai'd. He improves the fences, he erects his wine vat, and be- 
stows great labor and attention on the pruning and cultivating the 
vines. They bring forth abundantly; but his attention and the 
labor of the servants is so much engrossed in the vineyard, that the 
oliveyard and orchard are forgotten and neglected. In process of 
time his father returns. He finds his vineyard well enclosed, 
highly cultivated, and richly laden with the choicest grapes. But 
on visiting bisorchardand oliveyard be finds the enclosures broken 
down, the trees undressed and browsed upon by all the beasts of 
the field. He calls his son. He hangs his head in his presence. 
His father asks. Why is it, my son, that my oliveyard and orchard 
are so neglected, and destroyed, while my vineyard flourishes, and 
is laden with fruit? Father, said he, I have always thought the 
^rape was the most delicious of all fruit, the most salutary, as it 
cheered the heart of God and man, and therefore the most wor- 
thy of constant care and cultivation — I therefore bestowed all my 
attention upon it. His father rejoined. Unfaithful child, it was 
not my pleasure, my mind, nor my will, then which guided you; 
but your own inclination. Had you preferred any thing else to 
the vineyard, for the same reason that you neglected my orchard and 
your oliveyard, you would have neglected it. I thank you not for 
cultivation of the vine, because, in doing this, you consulted not 
my pleasure, but your own. Undutiful son, depart from my prc- 
sence^I will disinherit you> and give my possessions to a stmn- 
ger. 

So is it with every one who is zealous for keeping up one institu- 
tion of the King of kings, v/hile he is regardless of the others. 

Some Baptists are extremely devoted to immersion. They have 
read all the baptisms on record in the New Testament, and begin- 
ning at the Jordan they end at the city of Philippi, in the bath in 
the Roman prison. The ancient mode and nothing else will please 
their taste. Away with your sprinkling and pouring, and babyism! 
The authority of the Great King is desctvbed. Sxv ^Vqi^yw^ ^Ovw^. 
The importance of implicit obedience \i^ eiLVcuW^^, wv^ >^\^ >K««:'«^ 
utility of keeping Ilia commands is set ^otX\\ \\\ \wi^vs»^^ "^^^^^ 
csnnotbe mistaken. But when the auci«xv\. mwi^ q1 vi>a^'t^NVNi^'^

Lord's day or of breakin^f bread is called up to their attention, ihej 
fall asleep. The authority of the Great King will scarcely mako 
them raise their heads or open their eyes. Implicit obedience now 
has no charms, and the utility of keeping his commands has no 
attractions for them. Such Baptists are not regenerated, that isi 
tiiey are not devout — not devoted to the will of God. They seek 
to please themselves. Let such compare themselves with the son 
of the householder in the preceding parable. They have got a 
Baptiit cotucience, and not the conscience of the regenerate. < A 
Baptist conscience hears the voice of God and regards his 
authority only where there is much water. But a regenerated 
mind and a christian conscience hears the voice of God and regards 
his authority as much on every Lord's day, or at the Lord's table, 
as on the monthly meeting, as at Enon or in the desert of Gaza. 
Many, we fear, think they are pleaang and serving God, while they 
are pleasing and serving themselves. They think they are devout, 
but they are devoted to their own will. So is every one who &c- 
kiiowlcdges any thing to be the will of God, and yet refuses to do it« 
Ah! remember, my friends, that all flesh is as grass, and all the 
glory of man; rabbinical, clerical, reg^l, is as the flower of the. 
grass: the grass withereth, and the flower falleth down, but he that 
DOETii the will of God abideth forever. Ye Doctors of Divinity, who 
are doting about questions, and fighting about straws; ye editors of 
relii^ious journals, who are surfeiting the religious mind with your 
fulsome panegyrics upon those who second your views, and direct* 
ingthe public mind to objects lighter than vanity-^reroember that 
the will of Jehovah will stand forever, and that when <*gems and 
monuments and crowns are mouldered down to dust," he that does 
the wil) of God shall flourish in immortal youth. Go to work, then, 
and us^ your influence to restore the ancient order of things.

case himself? I speak as unto wise men. Judge ye what I say. 
IVaa the business of the Saviour into the world to bear witness to

bear witness of myself, my witness is not true." John v. 31. And 
again, in the 10th verse he says, '^Because he beUeveth not the 
reerd that God gave of his Son." Here the same record is said to 
be given by God himself. Now admitting that the Son bears a 
part of tins record^ can we speak the language of Canaan with 
reason, and say, This is the record God ^ve of his Son? From 
these and many other considerations of a similar nature, I am led to 
believe that the Son is not implicated by the term **Word** in this 
▼erse. Now you would ask me. What composed said record? To

that sent me to baptize with water, said unto me. Upon whom 
thou dialt see the Spirit descending and remaining on him," &c. 
Throu^ these scriptures, or in this manner, the Father bore record 
of his Son. 

Second manner— The Holy Spirit descended upon Christ when 
he came up out of the water; or, in the language of verse first, 
<*Tbis is he that came by water." '*And John saw and bear record 
that this is the Son of God." John i. 34. 

Third manner-^yUXU iii. 17. "And lo! a voice (the Word) from 
heaven, saying. This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well 
pleased." Thus we see these three are one as to their origin and 
design, being given by one being, who, by these three manners 
of attestation, designed to prove the heavenly, the heart -reviving, 
and the soul-saving truth, that Jesus Christ is his well-beloved 
Son." 

Dear Brother^ 

1 CAN neither admit the genuineness of the reading of

blood, and it is the Spirit which attested this, because tho Spirit is 
the truth. Farther, there are three that testify this — the Spirit, 
and the Water, and the Blood— and these three are one," or to 
one amount. Thus I Jiterally translate the Greek text of Gries- 
bach, which reading is moreover approved and confirmed by 
Michaelis, and other great critics and colUtprs of ancient MSS. 

That the common rtading, if genuine, makes nothing in favor of 
the Trinitarians is admitted by both Calvin, Beza, Mucknight, &c. 
^c. That it is Hot genuine was admitted at the era of the l^et'or* 
znation by I.uther, Zuinglius, Bullinger, and Erasmus, and by 
many eminent critics since that time. That it is wanting in all the 
uncient manuscripts, save one, and that of doubtful authority, is 
generally admitted; and that it is not found in any of the very 
iincicnt versions is indisputable, such as the old Syriac, the Coptic, 
Arabic, and Ethiopic. That it is not quoted by any of the primitive 
fathers, and scarcely referred to before the era of the Council of 
Nice, is also admitted. It was by Robert Stephens introduced 
into the common Greek text from some of the most ancient of tlie 
Vatican Greek Testaments, from which the Spanish theologians 
formed the Complutensian edition of the Greek Testament, and 
which Pope Leo X. gave them. Mill, in his note on the common 
readmg, lays considerable stress upon its having been quoted by 
Tei tulJian and Cyprian before the middle of the third century; but 
the objections against these quotations render them of very doubt- ^ 
ful authority; and it is most worthy of note that in the fierce con- ^ 
troversies about the Trinity immediately subsequent to the Nicene 
Cnuncil and Creed, it is not once quoted by any writer, whieh 
shews it not to have been in the copies then generally read. 

As in the judgment of Calvin, Beza, and the most learned 
Trinitarians, it makes nothing in favor of three pemons in one God/ 
and as neither the adoption of it as genuine, nor the rejection of it 
as spurious, favors the conceits of the AriMis: neither sect should 
contend about it beyond the evidence which antiquity and the 
scope of the passage furnishes. 

'i'hc translation I have above given of Griesbach is in the spirit 
and scope of the context; and as I understand the passage, it 
imports that Jesus was proved to be the Messiah t)r the ChH»t^ 
supereminently at his baptism and death. He -was, according to 
ancient t^pe and prophecy, to come by water and blond — and ac- 
cording to these he did come fully attested at his baptism and 
death. Now there are three evidences of this truth that Jesus is the 
C/ttist, and that all who believe in him have eternal life. These 
three concur in one and the same thing. These are the spirit, not 
the Holy Spirit particularly, but the doctrine which Jesus taught. 
Thus John defines it in the passage itself: "The spirit is tue tbcth." 
The article is overlooked in the common version. The truth, 
tlicn, or the spirit, or the doctrine 'which Jesus taught, pioves his 
mission and his claims. The water, or his baptism, and the baptism 
of the first christians, which was generally acct mpanied by some 
sph'jtual gift, is /inother proof of the same. His death inseparably 
oonner-tcd with Ilia resurrection, consummates tbe "«\vo\e, uixd \\\e 
<vdjnance that commcmonUs it is a standing moi^atat^x. o^ \v\'i

death of Jesus, all attested and accompanied by the most signal 
demonstrations of the Holy Spirit, constitute a summary view of 
the infallible evidence of the Messiah'ship of Jesus, and of the 
truth of God's promise of eternal life to all who believe in and

Ordination of the Rev. John Montgomery , 

ON Tuesday, the 6th instant, the presbytery of Ballymena 
oifdained the Reverend John Mont|3fomery, A M. to thf pastoral 
charge of the newly erected congregation of Glenwhirrj'. The

The multitude who came to witness the solemnities of the day was

tented field, in tlie immediate vicinity of the newly erected meeting

ly nena. 5. James Owens, Esq. who so liberally gave a free grant of

A number of other toasts, all expressive of cordial good will and

are spr«»ading with unparalleled rapidity— that every revolvmg 
year adds to the number of their con^rregations in Ireland; and that 
the voice of their preachers, speaking in the Saviour's eternal name« 
is heard over the land, from **Carnckfergu8 to Cape Clear,'*

believing when our will is on the other side. But still it is a truth 
capable of the fullest demonstration that your assertion is false; or, 
in other words, that the will has an immense control over our 
belief. You see, then, we are at issue here. And as this is your 
main fort and citadel, do examine its bulwarks and towei». They 
are most certainly built upon the sand. You assert that the will 
has no power over our belief. I assert that it has an immense power 
over it. My adage is. What men will to believe, they most gener- 
ally, if not liniversally, believe I assert that the understanding is 
iK>t independent of the will, nor the will of the understanding.' 
But I only call this subject up to your reflection at present. The 
design of my present paper is to offer some thoughts upon the 
nature of the evidence of Christianity. 

The evidences of Christianity, or the proof that it is of divine 
origin and authority, are usually classified under two heads — tb^ 
Internal and the External. The Internal are those which appear 
in the volume itself, or the proofs which the religion itself, objec- 
tively considered, presents to the mind of a reasoner or student. 
The external are those attestations which accompanied the pro- 
mulgation of the religion, and those arguments derived from, not 
^he nature of the religion itself, but from the accompaniments oC 
/f;; tbese are usoaMy denominated the miracles and the prophe- 
'^*^A ' ^^ ^^o§e who were the cotemporar'iea oC the promulgation 
^r tills reiiffioa, the exterauil eyidenccs ftrs^ atteaXfta x\v«:\t ^xx^w- 



BAPTIST. 27 

t^on, and were, in a certain sense, to them the stronger evidences;^ 
but to us who have the w^ole on record, both the religion itself 
and the mirasles and prophecies, the internal are the stronger, and 
hrst arrest our attention. It is, perhaps, improper to separate 
them, for the one is not without the other, either in the design 
or execution of this stupendous scheme, nor in the import of it. 
1 am not about to adopt this trite metlfod, nor to occupy the atten- 
tion of my readers in the investigation of cither distinctively! but 
in the mean time, would offer a few reflections upon the adjustmeiil 
of the evidences to the condition of mankind in general. 

1 will, without hesitation, admit that the evidences of the truth 

of Christianity might have been easily augmented if it had pleased 

the founder uf it, or hud it been compatible with the whole plan 

of things. From analogy 1 have reasoned thus: Tke Sun mig^t 

have been made to have produced a thousand times more light and 

lieat. Animals necessary to our comfart might have been greatly 

multiplied, or those given us might have been endowed with a 

higher degree of instinctive knowledge. But again, if the Sun 

had been made to alford greater light, the human eye would have 

been rendered useless, or to have been made differently. If the 

heat whicli we attribute to the Sun had been greatly augmented, 

our bodies could not have endured it. If domestic animals had 

been augmented, their support would have been more oppressive; 

Kff if tliose made for our convenience had been endued with more 

instinct or more extensive knowledge, they would not have served 

us at all, but have become our masters. And if the evidences of 

Christianity had been augmented, it wouid not have been adapted 

to the condition of man. The adjustment of hght to the eye and 

of the eye to light; of heat to animals and of animals to heat; of 

instinct to brutes, and of brutes to our service, is all graduated 

upon a divine scale; or, in other words, is perfectly adapted means 

t(i end, and end to means. Precisely so the evidences of the 

christian religion to the present condition of men, and of the re- 

Jigion itself to man. The christian religion is made for man, and 

absolutely and indispensably necessary to his comfort, as food is to 

the body. And the evidences of this religion taken together are 

as precisely adapted to the condition of man in this stage of his 

existence, as light is to the human eye, or sound to the human ear. 

Amongst the thousand ways in which the evidences of the christian 

religion might have been, and might now be augmented, 1 will 

mention but two or three. For instance, God might have spoken 

aloud to the Jews and Romans in their own language, in such a way 

as could not be misunderstood, and have attested the pretensions 

and claims of his Son. The Son himself might have^ by the same 

power, given more general and conspicuous proofs of his mission. 

He might have gone to Rome, as to Jerusalem, and summoned alt 

the heads of departments, magistvates, legislators, and priests, 

snd given such proofs of his person and mission as would have 

revolutionized Rome and the world in a few days. At this time 

also God might speak in all the languages oC^Vv^ hi Q»\Vd.\wV5v^vwsv^ 

instant of time, and inform a\\ natious, Tiua T<»c.e^>^'aX.^^ ^^x^ks^

EDITOR. 

A FAliMER once had i.horse, which his son, a lad often years 
old, could ride with pleasure and safety. But no fence could keep 
this horse out of his master's corn field. The consequenac was, 
he was confined to the stable and secluded from good pasture. 
The lad said to his fathA* one day when riding out, 'Father, what 
a pity it is that this horse has not a little more wisdom— how much 
better he might live in the pasture than in the stable, if only he 
could learn from his firstiong conhnement to avoid going into the 
cornfield. If he had only a little more sense how much better it 
would be for him and for us.^ Stop, my son, replied his father — if 
he had a little more sense, just as much as you now wish him to 
have, he would not let you nor me ride him at all. Those who 
never think upon the adjustment of things to their respective ends 
and uses^will find an admonition here. Ed.

To begin with the last mentioned, namely, religion; 
that we may not mistake the meaning of this leading and 
important term, let us begin at the root;*- it is derived 
from the Latin word, reli^io^ and that from religo^ to 
bind tJiorougbly, or strictly; that is, to all intents and 
purposes; hence the noun in the Latin language is fre- 
quently used to signify an oathj more commonly piety, 
the worship of God, or the rites and ceremonies of his 
worship . Hence, a man of religion, of piety ; or a pious 
and religious man, are phrases of equivalent import; 
expressive of the possession and exerci^^e of an inward 
principle of love, adoration, and reverence towards 
God. In this sense, indeed, religion is the same in 
in all true worshippers, both men and angels. In this 
sense, therefore, neither Christ nor JVIqses, officially 
considered, were the authors nor introducers of it. 
Our author therefore must needs understand it in the 
external exhibition of it, consisting in a devout and 
referential observance of certain rites. and ceremonies, 
or ordinances of divine worship, divinely appointed; for 
in no other sense can religion be properly the subject of 
a divine institution. Now our author has told u^, that, 
in all these respects^ the patriarchal, Jewish, and christian 
re;igiui:s are distinct and different. How then can they

this is the meaning of our author; for he says, the Jew«i 
ish and christian religions, ^^are in substance and design 
the son*/;'* "/or a sinner teas justified by faith., and saved 
then^ just as he now is,'^ If by the term, justified, we 
are to understand a person's being sustained as righteous 
before God, as approved and accepted in his sight, we 
migbt argue in a similar way, that the religion of our 
first parents in the state of innocency^ and of the father 
of the faithful was essentially the same; yea of all 
true believers to the end of the world; for who k lows 
not that the very essence, or prime constituent princi- 
ples and essential qualities of atl acceptable worhip, of 
all true religion, are faith and obedience; that by the^e 
Abraham, and true believers with him, are and have 
been justified, and ever shall be; and that by departing 
ftrom these, our first parents sinned, and fell into con- 
demnation — even by their disbelief, and consequent 
transgression. But, after all, our author may perhaps 
be exculpated from the unpleasant charge of self con- 
tradiction by the just import of the term, dispensation^ 
which he always annexes to the word, religion, in the 
premises before us. He does not say that either Christ 
or Moses introduced anew religion; but only a nevv dis* 
pensatiom of religion. What may be the difference 
between a neto religion^ and a new dispensation of reli^ 
gion^ seems difficult to define- The term dispensation 
strictly and properly implies a weighing or parcelling 
out of something, as a task or portion for present use 
or ocupancy. Hence, in certain cases, there may be a 
new or repeated dispensation of the satn^ Voatv^^ . \W^ 
rummer aad wiaterj spring and auluxRa^ ^"a."^ ^\Axiv^^i 



^4 THE CHRISTIAN 

are, and hsive been dispensed to the world, and shaH 
continue so to be to the end of time. Yet no man con- 
siders any of these a new dispensation. The word cov- 
enant, which our author uses in this connexion, and 
vhichhas the advantage of being a more scriptural epi- 
thet, goes to afford no assistance towards solving the 
difficulty; for a neto covenant of religion^ which signifies, 
a new constitution or establishment of religion, neces- 
sarily implies and designates the newness or novelty of 
the religion established; especially when the people for 
and amongst whom it is established, are already in pos- 
session of a religion or form of worship which the new 
religion goes to supercede, as our author acknowledges 
the christian did the Jewish, to all intents and purposes 
demolishing its whole fabric 

Upon the whole investigation of this subject of appa- 
rent self contradiction, there appears no means in the 
compass of the common use of language, and of com- 
mon sense, to exculpate our author; I mean, of recon- 
ciling him with himself. This, however, would appear a 
matter of small moment, were it not for the importance 
of the subject, and the connexion in which it stands. 
But what a pity, that so strenuous and able an advocate 
for reformation should have so committed himself, for 
the sake of maintaining an antiscriptural hypothesis, viz* 
that the christian church or kingdom of Christ is but a 
continuance and improvement of the old; and this not 
for the sake of priestly honors, and the tithing system, 
like the high pretentioned Episcopalians; but merely 
for the sake of infant sprinkling, founded upon the hy- 
pothesis of church membership, deduced from the rite 
of circumcision, the fleshly seal of the covenant of pe- 
'Culiarity, with the select seed of Abraham according to 
the flesh. PHILALETHES. .

REPLICATION NO. II. TO SPENCER CLACK. 
JDrotfier Clach, 

WHEN you have read my No. 2. on Dr. Noel's circular, you 
will no doubt have observed that I represent you as having changed 
the subject of investigation on the creed question, and that you 
are considered as fighting with a phantom of your own creationi 
You have defined a creed to be all that a man -aritcs on the subject 
of religion — a definition however true and correct you may consider 
it, is at war with all the creed systems in Christendom. On your 

. definition, the creed of the Presbyterian church is the writings of 
all the commentators, all the bodies of divinity, sermon books, ani 
religious magazines, written by the orthodox clergy of that church, 
equally with the Westminster productions. On your definition, all 
writings of Dr. Gill, Andrew Fuller, and a hundred others, regular 
and orthodox Baptists, constitute the creed of the regular Baptist 
church. And so it comes to pass, that all the writings of every 
man is his creed, and all who adopt him as a brother or a member 
af their community, adopt his writings as their creed. I know you 
have not said so in so many words, but your definition of a creed 
most certainly represents the matter thus. For you call my whole 
writings my creed, and make them the creed of all who read them 
with approbation. This is not that question Dr. Noel, Dr. Miller, 
or I was discussing; and by introducing this view of the matter, you 
have changed the whole ground oicontroversy. For instance, 
when I commence a defence of myself from your imputations, I 
have only to shew that I am making not a creed for myself or others, 
no test of religious character, no term of communion; and when I 
have done this the question at issue is never glanced at, which is 
proof positive that the question at issue is abandoned by you. 

If yoli aim either at my conviction or that of others, you must 
not reason in this way; for to see you driven into this plan, ostab- 
lishes us in our views more and mom; and weakens your cause 
irreparnbly. I now bag your indulgence while I attempt to shew 
you that you have mistaken the subject altogether. I say mistaken^ 
for I would rather believe that you have mistaken, thau that you 
have knowingly misrepresented it. You say in Letter 1. "You 
(meaniag myself) ''object to creeds and confessions, and for the

TO THE EDITOR OF THE CHRISTIAN BAPTIST. 
T^ear Sir, 

OS reading^ your essay in one of the late numbers of the 
ChritUan Baptist, on the **purity of speech," or being cast into 
the mould of the New Testament, or Covenant, my mind was 
involuntary led to the following train of reflections, which I have 
concluded to pen down and transmit to you for publication, if you 
think them worthy of insertion. 

The subject of my meditations was the first idolatry, or image wor< 
ship, the worshipping of the molten calf as gods, a particular account 
of which may be read in Ex. xxxii. The Israelites said to Aaron 
while Moses was upon the Mount receiving the law, **Up, make us 
gods which shal) go before us," &c. "And Aaroti said unto them. 
Break off the golden ear rings which are in the ears of your wives, 
of your sons, and of yoMr daughters, and bring them unto me." 
•*And all the people brake off the golden ear rings which were in 
their ears, and brought them unto Aaron." Men wore these orna- 
ments in the eastern countries as well as women, as we find in the 
story of the Israelitish and Mldian soldiers. Judges viii. 24, 25. 26. 
and Pliny, speaking of their ear rings, says, ''In the east it is 
esteemed an ornament for men to wear gold in that place." (See 
l$c anchart's History, chapter 34.) "And they said. These be thy 
gods, O IsraeH" &c. "And Aaron built an altar before it, and 
made proclamation. To-morrow is a feast to the Lord." Now, 
wiiat in this history struck me so forcibly, was, first they changed 
Hieir glory into the similitude of an ox, a fat filthy ox that grazetU 
on the green meadow, and then transferred to this beautiful ox 
with white and black spots intermingled, the fearful name, the 
character, the attributes, tlie perfections, the works and llie wor- 
sliip of tte J am that law. For they rose up early on the morrow 
and brought burnt offerings and peace o€erings. How easy the 
transition! This may appear a small, matter to some who believe 
that whatever a man thinks to be right, ia right to him; and tu 
others who say that it is no matter what we call fhings, so that we 
mean the same filing; and by othera it will doubtless be viewed in 
the light of a non-essential, as it was at most only a departure 
from one of the statutes. But let us trace this one act of the high 
priest through all it& meandering^ as far as we have the facilities 
9f doing so, and see if the ultimatum will support these persons 
in their indifference about celestial names and things. 

It i&. generally supposed.that they learned this idolatry or abomi- 
nation from the Egyptians, among whom they had. sojourned, and 
who were notorious for their love and use of hiertiglyphics, and 
who accordingly \yorshipped Joseph, (who interpreted the dream 
of Pharaoh's seven fat and lean kine,) under the emblem' of an ox 
with a budiel turned over his head. This is the foundation of all 
idolatry. This is the Apis, or Serapis, of the Egyptians; the Bel, 
or Belus, of the Canaanites, Chaldeans, or Babylonians; the Meli- 
anthus of the i'/.ccnlclans; the Mokch, ^uVocXv^ "V^Ac^tcv, >^^V%\«^^ 
MUJsom, Rcpbair, Itemphan, Ch\un, o? lYit KmtMi^\\»&\ >^\^^5»v^^

a male deity, of the Israelites; the Chemosli, Baalim, aiul Ahstarolf?, 
feminine deities, of the Moabitef ; the Adonis of the Syrians; or

the aulhor of the original) referred to me as tlie teacher who 
refused to have the New Translation read at a public meeting, be- 
cause it is not the word of God, &c. Candiaus has done great 
injustice to himself and to me by his uncandid statement. At our 
April meeting, on a question before submitted, Which was the 
most scriptural for the church to have a Pastor or a Bishop? on the 
point of order I judged it improper for it to be read (not because 
*Ht it nat the -word of God") but because the church had not re- 
ceived it as the standard by which questions of that nature were 
determined; that when references were made by the church to 
the scriptures, they had always referred to the common version. 
But lest a mistaken idea should have been received, I added, I 
wished it to be distinctly understood that I gave the opinion on the 
point of order, not from any prejudice I had against the book; 
that in the general I was pleased with it; that I had it, and used it 
at home; that nO person need be afraid of it; that the reason why 
I did not use it in the pulpit, was owing to the prejudices that 
existed against it, as I was unwilling to assail the feelings of any 
when it could be avoided; and declared that if the church was 
then willing, it should be introduce$l— I had on my part not the 
least ol)jeclion. 

The reasons that made the above decision necessary, were: the 
cliurch was under considerable excitement, inflammation running 
high; many of the members had never seen nor examined the 
new version, a number were«highly prejudiced against it; and as 
there was no precedent of any other except the common version 
ever having bsen Introduced, it became necessary that the churcii 
s!ioi/Id have been consulted and consented to it before its introduc- 
tion. Abruptly bringiii^ it in would have endangered the peace 
of t^e church the more; to guard against which was my particular 
object. 

You will please to publish this in the Chriatian Baptist as 
promptly as you did the one it is designed to correct. As 1 am net 
pleased with fiction, you will give my name. 
Uespectfully, your brother in Christ,

» Richmond, Va. July 25. 

'•TH £ revival in Richmond has measurably subsided. la NorfoUc 
and Portsmouth the revival has been very great, and many have 
been added to the cong'regations of Baptists, Methodists, and Pres- 
byterians. In the Isle of Wight county the excitement has beeii

oldest christian^ in tba^ section. They meet at private houses for

"Itidianopohs, Indiana, July 23. 
*'AVHEN I moved here last October, there was but one Baptist 
church in the county, and that was in town, constituted on the arti- 
c'es of faith of the Long Run Association. Three months ago they 
were laid aside. We now take the New Testament for our faith 
and practice, and no difficulty has yet arisen from this course of 
procedure, nor do we expect any. Two other churches have been 
constituted in this county (Marion) last winter and this spring with 
S members eacti. One has now 60 members in fellowship — the 
other, twenty.*'

FROM the perusal of your Christian Baptist, and known 

talents upon theological subjects, I would come* nearer to the 

truth of a question I would presume to propound. Before I lay 

flown the 9uestion, [ will state the history of the case which 

g-ave r/se to it. J am a member of the Methodist Episcopal Church,

the Mutual Rights^ in which are discussed the principles of church 
government. We, the Reformers, wish the church to be modelled 
upon primitive usag^e, ». e, tlie people to be identified in the church 
with the ministers in the law-making department. Now it is con- 
tended for in a pamphlet versus Reform, that, as the church origin 
noted from the preachers^ that is, in the formation of the discipline 
to govern the Methodist Episcopal Church, as the people did not 
originate the discipline, they have not any right in the administra- 
tion of the church. And again, that a man virtually surrendered 
his inherent rights in the church, or, in one word, that a member 
of the Methodist Episcopal Church has lost his liberty as Christ's 
freeman when a member of said church. You must recollect that 
the discipline of our church originated from Coke and Asbury, and 
that the present polity was surreptitiously introduced, being con- 
trary to the desire of our founder, the episcopacy of the c/iurch. 
^ow for (he question: Can it be possible for any church to exclude 
its members from a participation in the law-making department of 
the church, merely and solely because the church government origina- 
ted from the ministry? Was not the church for 150 years governed 
according to the manner it is laid down in the New Testament, and 
as soon as the clergy debarred the people their legitimate rights it 
sufik or merged to popexy, with its concomitant evils? Now it is 
also contended in the pamphlet, that, as the Methodist polity is 
missionary, lay representation will bring it down to the congrega- 
tionalist form, and naturally destroy the design of the missionary 
character.^ Now we have nothing to warrant that assumption from 

Siimitive times. I am certain that there were churches planted in 
liferent parts of Asia, Europe, Africa, and that under that econ- 
omy the spirit of the missionary character was glorious. Now how 
lay representation in the councils of the clergy can destroy the mis- 
sionaiy effect, is an enigma to my mind, maugre the spareness of 
the number of the people and the extent of country the preachers 
have to travel, even if they have the world for a diocese. 

Your attention to these will command the gra^ful recollection of 
your sincere friend and well wisher, 8. 1. M.

more Conference, for reading the "Mutual Rights," and recoin- 
mending them to others — his moral character unimpeachable. 
These thin^ have roused the members so in many parts, that they 
are determined no longer tacitly to submit to this Methodist 
Popery."

appear in this country so illy to comport with the spirit and genius

I do hope that you will succeed in defacing one mark of the 
Beast from your system. But I do not understand so well what 
you mean by the laity participating in the law-making department. 
Neither the teachers nor the taught, as I understand the New 
Testament, have any Iaw>making authority at all. Jesus Christ, 
ilie New Testament teaches me, is the one only lawgiver, and he 
>s able to save them who obey his laws, and to destroy them that do 
not. "^'ou have no need of any other lawgiver, nor laws, as far as 
1 can judge. I would ask those who wish to have a'legislating 
powciy to inform me how, and upon what subjects, they would 
exercise it. 

I do not wonder at ifee logic used by the anti-reform good people. 
Men never like to part with power; and those in power will always 
ilnd matiy tools by which to carry their projects into effect, by any 
jfReans, sense or nonsense. 

I answer your c^uestion with a capital NAY. But lam unwilling 
to put out ene class of lawgivers and to put in another, when I 
know that every law they make for the church will be an attempt 
^o usurp the throne aad government of the Great King. You want 
iess law-making and more law -keeping. If I were to set up a 
human religion, that is, a reFig^on of human contrivance, I would 
.ordain that all the law-making should be in the hands of the laityy 
and that the priesthood should have no part in it at all; but let them 
execute the law of the laity. Then you might expect something 
like ycur rights — your mutual rights— but if you let the clergy 
help you to make laws and execute them too, you will be dup^ 
at last. For were you to send two laymen for every priest, the 
pr ests would make the laws at last; and your reformation, likethA 
of Luther, wodldneed to be reformed again and again. 

"With the best wishes for your success in destro}ing idols and 
them that worship them, by the power of Iruth, I subscribe myself 
your friend and the friend of every man who loves truth and liberty. 

A. C.

IN reply to the queries of the Christian Baptist^ published in 
our Gazette of the 11th ultimo, we have received the following 
communication from our correspondent W. R. 

To the Editor of the JSTe-^o Jffarmony Gazeit0i 
II A VI NG In vain looked for a reply from some of your co»- 



BAPTIST. 47 

' The questions proposed for our consideration are, Is thcr* a Gcti 
who created all things? Is there a spirit in man which will survive 
the hody? Is there a future state of reward or torment? I answer. 
We can reply to these propositions neither in the affirmative nov 
in the negative, for we possess no positive knowledge on any o\' 
these subjects. 

A Gnd, the Soul, Heaven and Hell, if sucli existences and 
places de really exist, can never, from their nature, become cog- 
nizable by the senses of man. I therefore cannot conceive how 
we shall ever be able to acquire information regarding their nature 
or existence. W. K. 

Can the editor of the Chrislian Baptist, or some of the ••enlight- 
ened Deists" from wnom he expected a reply, afford us any pos^ 
five information on this subject? If' so, we simll be pleased to hear 
from them, and shall insert their communications, reserving to 
ourselves our editorial privilege of clos-ing the discussion, should 
it become too lengthy for our columns, or uninteresting to our 
readers. 

I HAVE only room for the present to remark, tha*, with all the 
improvements in philosophy ftp 18 centuries, the world is no wiser 
with respect to God, than it was when Paul lived. He tlien de- 
dared that neither Greece, nor Rome, nor Egyp^ by all their phi- 
losophy, knew God. Even to tnis day, the God that was wihnow.i 
in •Athens, is unknown in New Harmony, and to all who have no 
other lights than what philosophy affords. And here is another 
and a striking^ proof: the people of the city of ••Mental Indepen^ 
denceV are said to have the best library on this continent, and witii 
«ll the advantages of social converse in the best improved condi- 
tion of human nature, having voluntarily extinguished the lights 
of supernatural revelation, have now candidly and honestly avowed 
that whether there is a God at all, a spirit in man that will survive 
his mortal body, a heaven or hell, is to them unknown and utikno-ioa- 
ble. This is .the identical conclusion to which I knew most certain- 
ly» by all ihe knowledge of phdosophy which I possess, they would 
be constrained to come. For, as 1 have frequently said, there is no 
stopping place between Deism and AtbeisoB; and they are lame 

Ebilosophers who, taking philosophy for their guide, profess to 
old with Herbert, Hume, Gibbons, and Payn#, that there is a 
God, an immortal soul, a heaven, or a hell. I give great praise 
to the New Harmony philosophers for their candor and their hones- 
ty in frankly avowing the conclusion which all the lights tbey have 
authorize them to maintain. I say the^ are ^ood philosophers. 
They have reasoned well. I tliank them for their polite and minute 
attention to the queries I proposed to them; and in the mean time» 
promise them a continuance of my essays on this most interesting 
subject. Ed. C. £,

IN looking over some of your late nqmbers of the Christian 
Jiaptis!, I found a series of essays addressed to Mr. D, whom you 
call a sceptic. Though I am not fond of useless •'replication," yet 
when controversy is instructive, I have no objection to give ear to 
it, and learn what may be learnt from it. This being the case, I 
feel somewhat inclined to investigate some positions laid down (( 
will not say a<«sumed) by you in the above essays; but at the same 
time I will observe that I wield a young, untutored pen — one ia 
which it would be the height of presumption to undertake to vie 
with the masterly quill of the erudite A. Campbell. 

In thefiraiand second numbers of your Replication, you deny 
the possibility of the existence of a God bemg known without 
deriving that knowledge from the Bible. Strange, in^fsed, is it, 
that the all-wise Creator of the universe should make the most 
faltiblekind of evidence, viz. testimony, the only possible vehicle 
through which he can be known to his creatures! It is strange that 
he should make the frail inventions of men, such as empty sounds^ 
'fiaper, &c. the archives of his name and character, in exclusion to 
the more du^^e work of his own hand — the Book of Nature, ^a 

I think th^Mie evidence of the scriptures is of tl^ moiifi|lliPif 
cUss; becauiB^ is to us history^ hearsay, or evideifte resting on 
the tes%Don^^i others. There are but three kinds of evidence by 
which we assent to the truth of a proposition; »nd of these but one 
"is infallible, and that is where the principles on which the evidence 
18 founded are intuitive. Sucl) is the evidence on which mathema- 
tical truths^are founded. The next highest class of evidence is that 
which I call experience; and is that which is received immediately 
by the senses. It is on this kind of evidence that the truths of 
jiatural and experimental philosophy stand. This, though a very 
Ittfh kind of evidence, is still fallible: for we are liable to be de- 
ceived by our senses, since, to a man having the jaundice, evei^ 
thing appears yellow. The next and last class of evidence is testi* 
mony, woerein we give our assent or Assent to^ijvji^^i\VMycv oti. 
.ifre renoitjr of others. This kind of eTtd^act \ft <^^^ ^aiSfi^^v V^x^

the witness mty either wilfully deceire by ppcyarication or lying; 
or though he wish to give correct testimony, his senses illlly have 
deceived him; and he being deceived, those who receive bis testi' 
snony cannot but be deceived also. 

The truths of the Bible are with us, founded on this kind of 
evidence. For though at the promulgation of the gospel, its truth 
was attested by miracles; yet we believe that it was attested in such 
a manner, on the evidence of testimony. It is possible fur the 
Bible to be all a fable or romance produced by priestcrafV. And 
as it is possible for it to be so, you see that the vehicle which you 
would make us believe is the only one by which we can come at a 
knowledge of our Creator, may deceive us, and we may spend our 
whole lives in controversial bickering about fables. 

Having sadd this much to shew you that there is not so much 
credence necessarily attached to the scripture account of the 
Creator and his character as you would have us believe, 1 shall now 
undertake to she^ you that, notwithstanding you could not, by 
your senses, discover but that the Creator *<was either not almigh. 
ty; that the winds and rain were stronger than he, or that he was 
the most notionate, irrational, and whimsical being in the universe;" 
we can, by cor senses, and reasoning faculties, be as imperatively 
convincea of the existence of a God, as we can by the scriptures. 
. I would here observe that this is the main point in which I disagree 
■• with you in your "replication." 

To shew that we are capable of knowing that there is a God, and 
how it is we came by this knowledge, I think we need go no farther 
than ourselves. Man, beyond doubt, has a clear perception, and 
certfun knowledge that he exists and is something, jjf any oiy» is 
80 sceptical as to deny this, he may enjoy his opinion; for me, till 
hunger or pun convince him of the contrary. For such are beyond 
the poweif of reason or demonstration to touch, if it were possible 
for such to be. But it is impossible for such rational creatures to 
exist; therefore, rational creatures that do exist, are certainly 
assured of their existence. 

<'ln the next place, man knows, by an intuitive certainty, thai 

re nothing can no more produce reial being, thaiMt can be equal 
^glrtangles. If a man knows not that nonhtity, or the 
I OT anbeing, cannot be equal to two right ^Bes, it is im- 
possible he should know any demonstration in I^ vRl* V there- 
fore, we know there is some real being, and that nm^entii^ cannot 
produce any real being, it is an evident demonstration that, from 
eternity, there has been something; since what was not from eter- 
nity, ha4 a beginnings and what had a beginning, must be produced 
by something else. 

**Nex^ it is evident^ that what had ita being and beginning from 
another, must also have all that whiqh is in, and belongs to its 
being from another too'. All the powers, it has must be owing to> 
and received from the same source. Ir^is eternal source, then, of 
all being, must alto be the source and original of all power; and so
