have, then, p^ot one step farther; and we are certain now, tbat 
there is not only some being, but sonte knowing intelligent being 
in the world. 

•*There was a time, then, when there was no knowing being, 
And when knowledge began to be; or else there has been also 
<i knoviti^ bein^ from eternify. If it be said there was a time when 
DO being had any knowledge, when that eternal Being was void 
of all understanding — I reply, that then it was impossible there 
ever should have been any knowledge; it being as impossible that 
things wholly void of knowledge, and operating blindly, and with> 
out any perception, should produce a knowing being, as it is im- 
possible that a trianglo should make to itself three angles bigger 
than two right ones. For it is as repugnant to the idea of senseles;) 
matter thut it should put into itself sense, perception, and know- 
ledge, as it is repugnant to the idea of a triangle, that it should 
j»ui into itself greater angles than two right ones. 

"Thus, from the consideration of ourselves, and what we infalli- 
blv find in our own constitutions, our reason leads us to the know- 
Jedjje of this certain and evident truth, that there is an eternal^ moat 
po-wet'ful, and moat knoioin^ being; which, whether any one will 
]>lease to call God, it matters not. The thing is evident, and from 
this idea duly considered, will easily be deduced all those other 
Sittributes, which we ought to ascribe to this eternal Reing. If, 
nevertheless, any one should be found so senselessly arrogant, as 
to suppose man alone knowing and wise, but yet tlie product of 
rner* ignorance; and that all the rest of the universe acted only by 
that blind hap-hazard: I shall leave with him that very rational and 
empha^cal rtbuke of Tully, ('. 2. deleg. to be considered at his 
leisure. What can be more sillily arrogant and misbecoming than 
for a man to think t||at he has a mind and understanding in him, 
but yet in all the umVenK beiside there is no such thing? Or that 
those things whiclt, wij[n^e utmost stretch of his reason he can

REPLY TO THE ABOVE. 
Dear SSr, 

TO the classification of evidence which you adopf, I 

. offer no objection. But more has been said on the superiority of 
jntuitive evidence than the subject deserves. Its superiority, in 
Hie estimation of philosophers, is greater than either in fact or 
utility. For the sake of argument, I am willing to admit that it 
produces infallible certainly. But this infallible certainty is of no 
greater importance in actual life than is the certainty, fallible or 
infallible, which results from the evidence of our senses or of testi- 
mony. I am ititvitivety^ certain that a whole is greater than a part. 
I am experimentally certain that fire will burn. I am, by testimony, 
certain that George Washington once lived. I doubt no more the 
truth or certainty of the last mentioned than of either of the former. 
You, in theory, place intuitive evidence above all other, as respects 
Ofertainty; but, ih fiat, you plaoe the evidence of your senses or 
eipenence above it. Take an instance in the close of your letter. 
You attempt to prove that there is a God from intuitive principles; 
and after reasonmg for some^time on these principles, you conclude 
your syllogisms by saying, "From what hasJ)^eQ said« it is plain to 
me we have a more certain knowledge ^ tne existence of a God, 
fhan of any thing our senses have not mmecliafelx discovered unto 
us." But, what follows? Not so certflR^or not more certain, than 
we are of any thing which our senses discover to us. In this wajr 
the philosopher often forgets his theory when he comes in contact 
with fact. I attribute Locke's words to you, as you have adopted 
them. 

But as men do not feel themselves certain upon, nor according 
tor the principles gi'aduated by philosophers in their schools, it id 
a matter of no importance with me to' spend many minutes in ob- 
jecting to your remarks upon evidence in general. The Revelation 
of God was not first communicated by testimony: he did not choose 
to reveal himself in this way; but to us now it is all matter of histo- 
ry or testimony: but not merely so, as you represent it. The Reve- 
liktion is addressed to the whole man, and it hus within it its intui- 
tive principles, which it presents to the honest student as Euclid 
does to bis students. When the terms are understood, it is as in- 
tuitively evident that good men differ from bad men, as that 3 and 
3 are not one and the same. There is no proposition in Euclid 
more capable of lucid and conclusive demonstration than this one.

through priestcraft or kingcraft. To those acquainted with its 
contents, it is an axiom as evident in morals, as any respecting 
quantities in mathematics, that good men could not surreptitiously 
introduce this volume. Neither could bad men. But, without 
particularizing on a subject so plain, I proceed to remark that the 
evidence which supports the claims of this volume is not confined 
to any one species, but embraces the whole* Its truth becomes the 
subject of expei^ence, properly so called. Jesus tbe Messiah puts 
it in the power of every person whom he addresses experimentally 
to prove the truth of his pretensions. He says, "Come unto me, 
all ye that labor and are heavy laden, and I will give you rest. If 
any man put himself under my guidance he shall know the truth, 
and the truth shall make him free. Thus we have the means of 
deciding experimentally on the reality of his pretensions. AVhether 
he were an impostor, or the Messenger of the Great God, is submit- 
ted thus to be tested by our experience. Where is the man who 
has proved these promises false? Myriads have experienced their 
truth. Thus you see it is doing injustice to the wisdom of the 
author of this volume to say, that he has made it a matter of testi- 
mony otily, properly so caJsd. For its cla.ms are supported by 
intuitive evidence, experience, and testimony. 

Bat there is a shorter, and, to the bulk of mankind, a more 
cogent way ot deciding the question. Whether the Book of Crea- 
tion, or that called the Bible, is better adapted to communicate to 
the human mind the knowledge of God? 7'his is by furnishing an

Bible, or they who read nothing else but the Book of Nature, know- 
most of ^od, or know him bett? Which of them possess the more 
dear, consistent, and rational v e^va of Deity? The progress of the 
students is the better proof of tiie qualifications of the pie^eptor. 

But the principal point is that which is yet to be noticed. You 
object in strong terms to my "denying the possibility of the exist* 
tence of ('od being known, without deriving that knowledge from 
the Bible." Permit me distinctly to state the difference between 
my views ^^^ those of natural religionists, deists, and sceptics in 
^neral, on this subject. 

1. 1 contend that no man, by all the senses, and powers of reason 
which he possesses, with all the data before him which the material 
universe affords, can originate or beget in his own mind the idea of 
aGod, in the true sense of that word. 

2. But I contend so soon as the idea of Deity is suggested to the 
mind, every thing within us and without us, attests, bears testimony 
to, and demonstrates the existence and attributes of such a being. 

If the firat position can be established, it will follow that there 
cannot be a rational deist on earth. If the second position be 
established, there cannot be an atheist amongst itll the compos mentis 
of the human race. 1 think both of these positions can be triumph-

monthly paper, the same size, execution, and price of this work

have often thought that such a work is much needed in the present

But I must not conclude this paper without pointing out the 
grand error in your and the philosophers* reasoning upon intuitive 
principles to originate the idea of a Creator or first cause. You 
begin to work wilh the idea in your own mind, and fondly imagine 
you have acquired it by your reasonings Your effort should have 
been to shew how a person without such an idea is to originate in 
his own mind the whole idea of a God. You suppose him in pos- 
session of a part of the idea before he begins to reason at all. 

All that the Book of Nature teaches is that every animal and 
vegetable is dependant on one of its own kind for its production. 
The whole volume does not afford a model or archetype for an idea 
of any animal or plant being dependant on any other of a different 
nature and kind for its production. You leap over the distance 
from Earth to Heaven in your reasoning, or rather, you fledge 
yourself with the wings of feitii, and find in the Bible the idea of 
all things being dependant on a Being unlike every other, who 
produces no being like himself, contrary to your analogy from the 
Book of Nature, and who produces all beings both unlike himself 
and one another. You flew so nimbly and so easily over this 
mighty gulf, that you were not conscious that you had got out of the 
region of earth-born ideas altogether, and were farther than all 
^pace from the volume of Nature which you sat down to read. 
Ask Locke and Hume, and they will tell you that you cannot have 
a single idea— a simple uncompounded idea, the pattern of which 
or the thing of which it is the idea, is not first presented to some 
one of your senses. Ideas are images, and before the image is 
seen in the glass, or exist in the mind, an object mus< be presented. 
And when have you seen any things creating ot producing something 
out of nothing, or forming any thing essentially unlike itself? And 
if such an object is no where presented you, how can you have the 
image of it!!! 

A natural man might see, and have an idea, that every animal 
and vegetable is dependant upon one of the same kind fop its ex- 
istence; but by what steps he could arrive at an idea that an invisi- 
ble bei' g made one or all animals and vegetables, I think no man 
living can shew. And that any roan could logically infer that there 
is a first cause, which is the effect of no antecedent cause from any 
thing he ever saw or heard outside of the Bible, no philosopher 
h.'S yet shewn, nor can it ever be shewn until man gets six senses 
instead of fi\e. Locke and other philosophers who have rejected 
the doctrine of innate ideas and who have traced all our simple 
ideas to sensation and reflection, have departed from their own 
reasoning-8 when they attempted to shew that, independent of 
supernattiral revelation, « man could know that there is an eternal 
^^st cause uncaused. You have 8 lever, bvLlUke ikKJak\mtde%> ^oa

rest ybur fulcrum. And outside of the ttibie the universe does

But I have a few facts which, on your principles^ are inexplicit 
blc^on mine they are easily understood^— 

1. Not one of the terms peculiarly expressive of the idea of a God, 
such as spirit, eternity, immortality, &c. are to be found amongst 
any people antecedent to their being possessed of oral or written 
tevelation. 

2. No nation or individual, without oral or written revelation, 
can be found who has a single idea of any item in the deists* creed. 

3. All the deaf and dumb that have been made to hear and 
speak, or who have been taught to communicate their ideas, have 
uniformly and universally declared that an idea of a God, or any 
thing under that name, never entered their mind. This is decisive 
proof that the knowledge of God enters the human mind by the 
ear, or by communication, verbal or written. 

4. Not one of the idolatrous nations pretend to have derived 
their religion from reason. These are facts which I can only state 
at present; but which, when developed, contain volumes of invin- 
cible argument on this subject. 

My dear sir, all your philosophy ends in doubts. And you may 
see from the philosophers of Harmony, that so soon as the Bible 
words and ideas are proscribed, man is left in total darkness, both 
as respects his origfin and destiny, the two grandest stnd most sub» 
lime points ever imagined or expressed. While they boast of light, 
they make a man more ignorant than an ass which knows its master's 
crib. They divest him of all his majesty, and make him of no more 
consequence than a sntul or a mushroom. Sic transit gloria phu

A PROBLEM 

For the "Editor of the Harmony Gazette and his doubting Brethren. 
YOU think that reason cannot originate the idea of an eternal 
. (ir^ cause, or that no man could acquire such an idea by the em- 
ployment of his senses and reason — and you think correctly. You 
ttvink also, that the Bible is not a supernatural revelation~*not a 
revelation from a Deity in any sense. These things premised, 
gentlemen, 1 present my problem in the form of a query again. 
i^The christian idea of an eternal first cause uncaused, or of a Godj^ 
ia now in the world, and has been for ages immemorial. You say it 
could not enter into the world by reason, and it did not enter by 
revelation. Now, as you are philosophers and historians, and have 
all the means of knowing, how did it enter into the -world? Ed.

TO THE CHRISTIAN BAPTIST. 
Brother Campbell^ 

YOUR talents and leamiog we have highly respected: your 
course we have generally approved; your religious views, in many 
points, accord with our own; and to one point we have hoped 
we both were directing our efforts, which point Is to unite the 
^ock of Christ, scattered in the dark and cUmdy day. We have 
seen you, with the arm of a Sampson, and the courage of a Davui, 
tearing away the long establishea foundations of partyism, human 
authoritative creeds and confessions; we have seen you successfully 
attacking many false notions and speculations in religion— and 
against every substitute for the Bible and its simplicity, we have 
fieen you exerting all your mighty powers. Human edifices begin 
to totter, and their builders to tremble. Every means is tried to . 
prevent their ruin, and to crush the man who dares attempt it* 
We confess our fears that in some of your well intended aims at ' 
error you have unintentionally wounded the truth. Not as uncon- 
cerned spectators have we looked on the mighty war between you 
and your opposers — a war in which many of us had been engaged 
for many years before you entered the field. You have made a 
diversion in our favor, and to you is turned the attention of creeds 
makers and party spirits, and on you is hurled their ghostly tbun»f| 
der. We enjoy a temporary peace and respite frwn wir where 
you are known. 

From you we have learned more fully the evil of speculating oa 
religion, and have made considerable proficiency in eorrecting

TO TIIE CHRISTIAN MESSENGER. 

Mi'other Stone, 

1 WILL call you brother because you once told me that 
you could conscientiously and devoutly pray to the Lord Jesus 
Christ as though there was no other God in the universe than he, 
1 then asked you of what import and consequence was all the long^ 
controversy you had waged with the Calvinists on the trinitariaii 
questions. They did practically no more than pray to Jesusi and 
you could consistently and conscientiously do no less. Theoreti- 
cally you diftered, but practically you agreed. I think you told me 
that you were forced into this controversy, and that you regretted 
it. Some weak heads amongst my Baptist brethrea have been 
scandalized at me because I called you brother Stone. V»*hat! say 
they, call an *\^rianf heretic" a brother// 1 know nothit^g of his 
Arianism, said I, nor of his Calvinism. I never seriously i*ead one 
entire pamphlet of the whole controversy, and I fraternize with 
liim as I do with the Calvinists. Neither oV their theories are worth 
one hour} and they who tell me that they supremely venerate, and 
unequivocally worship the King my Lord and Master, and are 
Willing to obey him in ;;jl things, I call my brethren. But more 
than this, brother Stone, 1 have to say unto thee. Your enemies, 
and they are not a few, have, to a man, as far as 1 have heard them 
speak, said your christian character, your moral deportment, was 
iinblemished. Would to Heaven that this could have been said of 
all who opposed you! I do not think it strange that, in running post 
haste out of B.ibylon, you should have, in some angles of } our 
course, run past Jerusalem. Nuy, verily, I have been astonished 
that you should have made so few aberrations in. so many eiForts. 

But, brother Stone, I exceedingly regret that you have said and 

written so much on txoo topics, neitucr of which you, nor myself, nor 

any man living, can fully understand. One of these is the burthen of

aenting it I was not about to contetid for it, nor to maintain any 
theory upon the subject. ^ My words are, **J^or would I dispute ov 
cvntvnd for thit as a theory or speculatiofi with any body" "Why, 
then, call me into the fieldf I have received many letters on the 
subject of that essay, not one of which confines itself to the thin^ 
] have said, nor to the ^rand object 1 l)ad in view, viz. to examine 
into the ideas attached to the term employed by the Holy Spint to

and sent into tlie world, and him who sent him. 

I have uniformly found that all writers for the trinity and against 
it, have much to say upon the rationale of the doctrine. Reason is 
either proscribed or enthroned. Those that one while proscribe 
ber, at anothcar appeal to her; and those who make her sovereign 
will not always do her homage. 80 that the controversy is from 
Reason to Revelation and from Revelation to Reason^ as the parties 
are pressed. I will take the liberty of laying down a few positions 
on this snbject, not for the sake of demonstmting them, but for the 
sake of deciding on a proper course of conduct. 

1. Tl:e pretensions of the B^ble to a divine authority or origin, 
are to be examined by our reason alone. Its evidences are address- 
ed to our reason, and by our reasoning powers the question is to be 
answered, "/« the Bible of divine or Luman origin?" So soon as 
reason has decided this question, then 

2. The truths of the Bible are to be received as first principles^ 
not to be tried by our reason, one by one, but to be received as new 
principles, from which we are to reason as from intuitive princi- 
ples in any human science. 

3. The terms found in the Bible are to be interpreted and under- 
stood in the common aoceptation, as reason or use suggests their 
meaning; but the thin^ taught are to be received, not because we 
have proved them by our reason to be truths, but because God has 
iavght them to us. 

4. The strongest objections urged against the Trinitarians by 
their opponents are derived from what is called the unreasonable- 
ness, or the absurdity of three persons being but one God, and that 
each of these three is the Supreme <iod. Now as you know I am 
i)pt at all disposed either to adopt the style nor to contend for the 
%iews of the Trinitarians, any more than I am the views of the 
Socinians or Unitarians of any grade; you will bear with me 
when I tell you that no man as a philosopher, or as a reasoner, can 
object to the Trinitarian hypothesis, even should it say that the 
Father, the W«rd, aiid the Spirit, are three distinct beings, and 
yet but one God, There Is nothing unreasonable in it. 1 will, 
indeed, in one sense, say, that it is unreasonable there can be a' God 
at all, or an Eternal First Cause/ because in all the dominions of 
reason there is nothing could suggest the idea; and because it is 
contrary to all the facts before us in the whole world that any cause 
can be the cause of itself, or not the effect of some other cause. 
No man, from analogy, can reason farther than every cause is the 
effect of another, ad infinitum. Here reason shuts the door. Here 
analogy puts up her rule, and shuts her case of instrnments. Now

'd thU sense, the Unitarian and the Trinitarian are afike nnphi1o» 
sophic— alike unreasonable. But here is the sophism: the Bible 
originates, or still keeps up the idea 'of a God — both the name 
andthetV^a. We see it is proved by every thing* within and with- 
out us. The Bible teaches us sometliing concerning three beings, 
(I shall call them) the Father, the Word, and the Holy Spirit, ft 
teaches us that there is but one God. From what the Bible teaches 
A supposes that these three beings are each and together one God, 
the same in substance, equal in power and glory. Ji says it is 
inconsistent— it is absurd. JIoiv caii three peraom or beings be one? 
How can one of these th'rec be the Deity, and yet the three be no 
more than the Deity? C says, This is not more unreasonable than 
that there should have been from all eternity one First Cause un- 
caused; and add% Your error is this: you know nothing of the 
existence of spirits at all. All bodies you know any thing of occur 
py both time and place; consequently, it would be absurd to 
suppose that three beings whose modes of existence are such as to 
be governed by time and space, could be one being. ButinasmucU 
as we do know nothing ab )ut the mode of existence of spirits, we 
cannot say that it would be incompatible with their nature, or modes 
of existence, that three might be one, and that one being might 
exist in three beings. Now, as no man can rationally oppose the 
CaWinistic hypothesis on principles of n ason, so neither can he 
prove it to be correct by any analogy, or principle of reason what- 
soever. Why, then, wage this warfiire? We n^ay disprove a theory 
by what the Bible declarea, but not by nur re asoning on such topics. 
Why not, then, abide in the itse of Bible terms alone? [See Essay

side of the Trinitarian as on the side of the Unitarian; and neither 
of them can, without a gross «Jireliction of their grand positions, 
iiccuse the other of being unreasonable in their reasoning or coR' 
illusions. 

Hut I adopt neither system, and will fight for none. I believe 
that God so loved the world that he sent bis only beprotten Son; 
that Jesus wasti.e Son of God, in the true, fall, and proper import 
of.thcse words; that the Holy Spirit is the Spirit of God. the Spirit 
of Christ, which was sent by the concurrence of the Father and 
the Son to attest and establish the truth, and remain a comforter^ 
an advocate on earth, when Jesus entered the heajttns. If any 
man's faith in this matter is stronger or greater -.hm fniie, I have 
no objection. I only request Jiim not to despise my weakness, and 
1 will not eondemn his strength. 

I am truly sorry to find that certain opinions, called Arian or 
Unitarian, or something else, are about becoming the sectarian 
badge of a people who have assumc<l the sacred nume Chris. ian; 
and that some pecuhar views of atonement or reconciliation are 
likely to become characteristic of a people who have claimed the

EDITOR^ 

MISCELLANEOUS LETTERS.— ^o, /. 

A HUNDRED letters, many of them of much consequence 
too, are on our files, and unless I should enlarge this work to 
double its present skfce, most of them must remain there until 
moth-eaten. 1 have concluded to attempt a sort of general an- 
swer in a series of letters called miscellaneous. The greatest in- 
convenience the reader may find in these letters, will be that he 
can seldom tell^ when reading one period or paragraph, what he 
may expect in the next. If he have a taste for variety, this may 
compensate for the many disappointments and sudden vicissitudes 
he may in one single letter have to experience.— We proceed. 

Common Sense, J^o. I. — A Baptist preacher of considerable 
standing, a few weeks since, did "preach a sermon on Eph. ii. 10." 
The divinity he taught was — 1. That m&n \osi "^ood disposition" 
before he lost Eden, and that he must get a good disposition be- 
fore Paradise can be regained. That regeneration consisted 
wholly and solely in getting a good disposition, and was a mere 
change of disposition. The parable of the sower was alleged as 
proof: and he concluded that a man would be judged and rewarded 
according to his disposition in the last judgment. That being 
created anew in Christ Jesus was simply to have a good disposition 
infused.— Now another Baptist preacher of still greater name and 
authority preaches thus: ** Regeneration consists not in the creation 
nor infusion of new faculties, senses, perception, taste, disposition^ 
or subjective light. But is a strong and lasting impression made 
on all our faculties by the almighty force of divine faithfulness 
and truth. It is an unshaken purpose and piu'suit formed in the 
mind by a full view of the government of God as explained by

Elder John Secrest told me, at the me^tin^ of the Mahoning 
association, Ohio, on the 27th ult. that he had immersed three Aun- 
4lred persons within the last three months. I asked him, Into lohat 
did he immerse them? He replied, he immersed them into the faith 
of Christ, for the remission of Uieir sins. Many of them were the 
decendants of Quakers, and those who had formerly waited for *the 
baptism of the Holy Spirit'' in the Quaker sense of those words — 
But brother Secrest bad succeeded in convincing them that the 
one baptism was not that of Pentecosti nor that repeated in Cesariat 
but a.i immersion into the faith of Jesus for the remission of their 
sins. He labors in the word and doctrine principally in the coun- 
t'es of BelofiGntand Monroe, state of Ohio. Thus while my friend 
Common Sense, and bis two Baptist doctors, are speculating on 
what regeneration is, brother Secrest "has, by the proclamation of 
repentance towards God and faith in the Lord Jesus Christ and 
immersion for the remission of sins, been the means of regenera- 
ting three hundred, in three months, in the proper import of the 
term. He thinks that a thousand persons have been immersed this 
season in the bounds of his labors, by himself and those laboring 
' with him. Immense have been the crowds attending, and great the 
€Xcifement produced by the simple proclamation of the gospel in 
the good old fashioned simplicity of unlettered and untaugh elo» 
quence.

Jesus Christ belongs to no religious party. All the sects them-

nanters, and High Church folks never have any revivals among 
them, because they do not believe in them. Be this as it may, one 
Ihipg is certain, that there is nothing special indicating that God 
is a party in any sectarian acheme.

, Bi'otljer Thomas Jiullock, of Kentucky, suggested to me a goot4 
idea last winter concerning the present condition of the Baptist 
churches in that state. As respects the tour churches and one 
pastor, or the monthly rotation, or "the horse-mill plan," as sonne 
call it; that is, in plain Scotch, one preacher coming once-u-month 
to preach to one church in a rep;tilar round as many times as there 
are months in a year. Just as a blind horse when he has gone once 
round, beg'nsa second tour in the same track. The preachers, as 
he judiciously observed, had been so long accustomed to gtiing 
round in tl.is way, that they could not now walk straight forward^ 
and therefore never would make bishops of a particular flock. He 
thoug'ht tney could do better at catching or gathering sheep in this 
circuitous way, than in feeding one flock; and suggested the follow- 
ing idea: Let every particular congregation elect one or more 
bishops who had never been spoiled by the preaching plan, and 
loose all the cords which bind these present preachers to four 
congregations, and let them go in circuits in rotation as often and 
as extensively as they could, and preach and teach; but let the 
congregations meet every Lord's day with their own bishops, and 
iittend upon the ancient order of things; and when any of these 
circuit preachers made them a visit, let them exercise all the gif\s 
they had frr the edi^calion of the brotherhood and the conversion 
of all aroui d; but by no means to interfere with the stated worship 
of the day. In this way ttie '*ongrcgation8 would have as much, if 
not more, of the labors of all these public men, and their own 
enjoyments and edification would be greatly enhanced by their 
constant attention to all the ordinances of the Lord's house. Brother 
Bullock suggested this merely as a preparatory or preliminary step 
towards a foil restoration of. the ancient order of things, and not as 
a fixed system of procedure in all time coming. He would have 
the congregations to contribute weekly, and these contributions 
in the hands of the trea^irers or deacons of the congregation, 
to be, at the discretion of the commnnitv, apportioned to such of 
the public brethren as visited them, according as they had need. 
This idea I think is a good one, and worthy the examination of the 
brethren.

I AM obliged to request thee to explain a small tnorai 
impropriety. The Minutes of the Franklin Association were pub-

circular could have reached FrankfWrt. My first notice of it' was 
published at Bethany on the 6lh of August. The Franklin Asso- 
ciation met the 4th of the same month. The August number could 
npt have been received by you before the middle of August, about 
the time the Minutes were in pre^s at Frankfort. How, then. 
could you have stated to the public, in a notice prefixed to said 
Minutes, that my fruitless assault on your circular had created a 
demand for it unprecedented and surprising, and it was implied 
that my assault had helped to sell some editions of your circular. 

This needs some explanation from you. It is understood that it 
was through you, if not bif you, this notice was prefixed to the 
Minutes, ft is well known that my *'assault" on your circular could 
not have been more than read by yourself and a few others in 
Frankfort when the Minutes were published. The question, then, 
is. How in one, or tvfo days at most, my remarks could create a 
demand for your circular unprecedented and surprising, and con- 
tribute to sell three editions of it.^ This unprecedented and surpri- 
mng fact, that two days at most after the arrival of my Jirtt notice 
of your circular, it should have created such an enormous demand 
as compelled you to announce the fact on the frontispiece of the 
Minutes, without leave or license from the Association* requires a 
word or two of explanation from yourself. That charity which 
hopeth all things, induces me to hope that you will find some way 
of explaining this thing to diyest id at ICMt, tf «skx tiMroi vc&y^*

A true story. — TJIEKFi lived, some years ago, in the town or 

. , in Connecticut, a man who was much addicted to the practice 

of coiivertinpr his neij^hbor's property to his own use and benefit, 
without if or ami — The clergyman of the town, suspecting him 
of making too free with his hay, bad one night concealed hi rose IF 
in his barn with his dark lantern. The thief soon appeared, and' 
tying up a large bundle, had just left the premises, when the Ilev. 
Ovvner, instead of bawling oat, "You scoundrel you! what do you 
mean by stealing my hay!" disengaged the candle from the lantern 
and dexterously applied it to the combustible load. The bundio 
was soon in a bright blaze, and the unlucky fellow, suspecting that 
he was pursued by some person with a light, laid his feet to the 
grotmd with uncommon agility. But it was in vain to escape the 
pursuing fire. The blaze, increasing in brightness as he ran, seem- 
ed to his terrified imagination to come nearer, tUl venturing to 
look around to discover the extent of his danger, he perceived, to 
h s astonishment, that the stolen hay was on fire. How it came so, 
puzzled him not a little. But, as conscious guilt assisted bis natu- 
ral credulity, he settled down upon the conclusion that the fire was 
sent from heaven to admonish him of his transgression. Full of 
this alarming notion, he gave himself no rest until he had gone to 
the parson, and made a confession of his crime, and related the 
supposed extraordinary and terrible warning from heaven. The 
Kev. gentleman humored his credulity, under the idea that it might 
reform bis life. He was not mistaken; ibr the blazing bay had made 
so deep an impression on the poor fellow's mind, that from thence- 
forth he forsook his evil courses, became a valuable member of so- 
ciety, and was united to the flock of the judicious clergyman, who 
had assisted so materially in his reformation. He finally died an 
honest man, in the firm belief of the interposition of Providence 
in setting fire to the stolen hay. The parson kept the secret till 
the poor man was laid in the dust; but then, even the clerical ton|^e 
could no longer resist the desite of communicating so serious an 
incident. ^ JNT. T. Baptut HegUter. 

Many conversions attributed to heavenly influences, are of the 
same kind with the above. JIfora/.— Let every man estimate his 
feith by the influence it has in purifying hb heart and reforming^ bis 
life. Ed. C. B.

REVIEW OF DIJ. NOEL'S CIRCULAR— vVo. IIL 

T SHOULD much regret If my plaMincss in this review should 
incur the displeasure of my friend and brother. Dr. Noll. Had he 
not piven such a direction tf» his circular as lo impose it on me to 
nT>tice it, I would fiave let it die a natural drath, or if It prefen*ed 
jniicide I should not have disturbed its manes t»r its ashes. I hope 
the Doctor will remember that he first took up the tomahawk, (to 
8peak in his own style) and that !)e fired twenty times at me from 
i^chind a tree, while I stood in t^e open field, and before I beg-an to 
pick my flint. He built his vamparts, entrenched himself, and tried 
his cannon before he ever proclaimed hostilii'es. Nay, he placed 
the white flag, the flag* of peace, at my side, and caused it to 
wave over my head, while his castles were building and his mu- 
nitions of war were preparing. These hieroglyphics the Doctor 
will xinderstand, and as for my other readers it is not necessary 
that they should understand them. 

I can still fraternize with the Doctor. I make it a ride td enu- 
merate (if I could) how many great and glorious things there are 
in which we agree. Besides a great many things in the Doctor's 
creed« we agree in many other things of equal importance which 
he left out of it. For example, we agp-ee in the hope and belief 
of the resurrection of the dead — and of a future state of happiness 
and misery, which capital points, we before saw, were not to be 
found in the Doctor's summary view of what the Bible teaches.— 
Indeed, we agree in nine hundred and ninety-nine things in every 
tbousand,and why should our difference in the thousandth have such 
a repulsive power as to burst through almost a thousand attractions. 
Nay, the Doctor will himself confess, and, were he dead, there are 
most unexceptionable witnesses to prove that he has repeatedly de- 
clared his hearty concurrence with me in almost every single point; 
Ihat he would travel many miles every week to enjoy membership in 
such t church or cbristiaa society as I would construct on my views 
<tf the kingdom of Jeaus. He has gone even farther than this. For 
he has declared to me, viva voce, that the time would come, and 
at no very distant day, when those views which I inculcated, 
would universally prevail amongst att chTu^ioA. K\A t;Nt.Ti ivvw^s^

Brother Camphell^VT is only by a free, unreserved corrcspou'I- 
cnce, either xiva voce or by writing, that we can come to a proper 
understanding on subjects of a supposed difference. You arc aware 
that it is foreign from my design to enter into a polemical cocnbat. 
I wish by the help of the Lord, to lead you to a serious conaiilera- 
tion of the importance of cultivating love and union with your 
brethren. From the many communications over diiferent and 
anonymous signatures published in our paper, you see that many 
of our brethren are exceedingl)? opposed to what they suppose to 
be your sentiments. Have they mibtaken your real views? are 
they fighting against shadows? Do you indeed hold the sentiments 
and opinions which your brethren have charged upon you? 1 have 
210 doubt you will answer in the negative; you will say they were 
mistaken. If so, what can be the cause of so many erroneous con- 
ceptions of your real views? Why is it that your brethren do not 
understand precisely what you mean? Can you ascribe it to the 
dulness of their intellection, to malignity of heart, or to an unjusti- 
iiable and illiberal prejudice? Surely not. Some who oppose your 
\iews are men of strong intellectual power — of fervent piety, and 
who* are very justly esteemed your best friends. You remember a 
worthy bishop ot Virginia, whom some time .since you bad occasion 
tojpraise; he is your friend; he loves you, but does not approve of 
your opinions. Ht ar what he says. Of Paulinus he speaks thus:— 

"He wrote something last year in which he certainly went too 
far. He is row convinced (I am persuaded) and is guarded against 
our friend Campbell's chimeras." 

Concerning your self he remarks: — "What shall we do with 
Campbell? He is certainly wise, but not with the wisdonn of God, 
at least not often. He seems to be misled by an ambition to be 
thought a reformer; but he wdl fail, or I shall miss my guess (as the 
Y'ankees say.) He may be as learned as Luther, or Calvin, or 
Melancthon, but they fell on other days than our friend Alexander. 
It is one thing tc reform Popery, and another to reform the Refor- 
mation." And though be cannot approve of your opinions, "yet, 
after all," sa} s he, **1 can't throw him away as a good man, nor am 
I without hope of his veering about until he gets to the right point 
of the compass and his last days be his best days." Such is the 
4fpinion of this excellent bishop. Consider what he says. 
Oi/r beloved "J^/wUnna*' will not, though he much loves voiu, be 
ibuijcJ an advocate for your opinioRS. He \a boX ^\w^o^td to rend

— That you may be better infonned concerning those who thus

to the capacity of the plain and simple followers of the Lamb— a'^ 
'tite have riot much of this world's leanting', and are disposed to admit 
that alone as obligatory, which can be clearly adduced from the 
New Testament, without the aid of sophistry or allusion to the 
practices of man. And we trust it may be given us from above, to 
receive with meekness whatever of this nature your love and con- 
cern for our welfare may dispose you to communicaf e. 

'f he order, which we derive from the law of Christ, i« as follows: 

Wo require that all whom we receive into fellowship should 

believe in their heart, and confess with their mouth, that Jesus is the 

Christ; that he died for our sins, according to the scriptures; and 

that upon such confession, and such alone, they should be baptized 

We hold it to be the duty and privilege of the disciples of Jesus 

to come together into one place, on every first day of the week, 

rejoicing in the recollections which that day revives — whereon the 

Lord Jesus destroyed the power both of hell and death, by his 

resurrection from the dead, and gave sure hope to his people of 

being raised also. When thus assembled, we proceed to attend 

to all the ordinances which we can discover to be enjoined by the 

practice of the first churches, and the commandments of the Lord 

and his spostles, 

jft. Our eJtIers presid'mf^, and the brethren all together, (having

the faith,) in obedience to the command, 1 Tim. ii. 1, &c. — we 
commence our public worship by kneeling down and offering the 
supplications, prayers, 8cc. directed in that passage — the elders by 
themselves, or one of the brethren selected by themselves, or one 
of the brethreii selected by them as competent, speaking as the 
mouth of the body. 

2d. One of the elders selects a suitable hymn or psalm, expres- 
nve of praise; in the singing of which all the members stand up 
and join, 

Sd. A portion of the word of God is read by one of the elders 
relative to the subject or institution of the Lord's supper; upon 
which thanks are given, by one of the elders or brethren, for the 
bi e :d; — and af\cr the breaking of bread — thanks for the cup;— 
and after taking the cup a suitable hymn or psalm is sung. 

4th. A passage relative to the feilowship or contribution for the 
poor saints is read; then prayer for suitable dispositions, and 
thanksgivinp^ for ability and privilege to contribute in this way. 
The collection for the saints follows. 

' 5th. Previons to reading the holy scriptures; prayer for the 
Holy Spirit to open the understanding of all present, to understand 
and receive the sacred word. The reading consists of a chapter in 
the I*aw, one in the Prophets, and one in the New Testament. 
After each, a pnuse is m^de to allow opportunity to any of the 
brethren to make remarks by way of illustration as the subject 
night require. 

6ih. Exhortation from the word of God, by the elders or bre- 
thren. 

7t I. Praise. 

8th. Prayer and separate. 

In the evening, the church assembles for worship; after which 
the elders in their turn, and some other of the brethren, approved 
by the church, declare the gospel to those without. 

A love feast is also attended to — and a meeting on a week even- 
ing — but those not appearing to be of the same strict obligation 
wiih the duties of the Lord's day, are sometimes made to give 
vay to circumstances. 

The kiss of charity, the washing of the feet, and the entertain- 
ment of the disciples, being things the performance of which 
arises from special oocasic^s exemplihed in the New Testament^ 
we deem of importance to be attended to on such occasions. 

Discipline is also a duty which will sometimes fall to the lot of 
the disciples on the Lord's day. 

It may be necessary to observe, that our elders labor at their re- 
ipective callings, for their support, and are not burdensome to the 
enurcb; but in case of need, or that the duties of their office ren- 
der aid unnecessary, the church deem it their duty and privilege to 
communicate liberally to them, as '*the laborer is worthy of his 
hire." 

As to our intercourse with the world, we require strict upright- 
ness in walk and in dealing, sobriety in spirit and behaviour — kmd-^ 
ness toWlird all, even enemies— no evil speaking of any— but zeal

Your epistle of March the Ist came duly to us, and our joy and 
gratitude to the Father of mercies have been excited by this in- 
stance of a society of believers in Christ, meeting together among 
themselves, and separating from the world and from false profes- 
sors, in order to walk aacording to the dictates of the kingdom of 
Zion, directed by his word and spirit in the exhibition of on king- 
dom. We are glad to observe also your zeal for ancient brodherly 
intereourA between churches holding the same faith and observing 
the same pr4pces— an attainment too much neglected in our days. 
In apostolic^mes, a member of one christian church had access to 
fellowship in another, on the footing of his membership in the 
farmer alone. Thus Phebe is commended to the church at Rome, 
as being a member of the church at Cenchrea, Rom. xvi. 1. 2. and 
it appears that such recommendations were usual in those times. 
2 Cor. iii. 1, 2, 3. 

To maintain such brotherly intercourse, both in a chnrch and be- 
tween different churches, it is ne/pessarjr to guard both against too 
much and too littlt: forbearance; and especially in respect to the 
external onler of the society. Accordingly, any shades of differ- 
ence from your pi'actices which are among us, we think should not 
affect or mar our relation as sister churches. 

We, as well as y«u, require such as we receive into our fellowship 
to believe in their hearts, and confess with their mouths, that *<Je8(is 
is the Christ, the Son of God." We think the scriptural meaning 
of this expression includes the belief of the character of God 
manifest in the flesh, and of the all-perfect and all-ef!icacious atone- 
ment which he hath made by shedding his blood: it includes also 
th^ belief of the promise, that whosoever believes the testimony 
of -God respecting the efficacy of the atonement, shall be save J. 
The profession that Jesus is the Christ, includes also the acknoW' 
ledgmcnt of the dominion of the Redeemer, and the authority of 
his laws; that he is both •*Lord and Christ." With regard to bolli 
faith and practice, we hold the vieaning of a passage to be the word 
of Gml, rather than any form of speech. Hence, when a persou 
professes to believe that **Jesus is the Christ," we satisfy ourselves 
that he understands and believes those words in the scriptural sense; 
for whilst we know that **no man can call Jesus Lord, but by th^ 
Spirit of God," we know also that many say to Jesus, "Lord, Lord," 
who have no part in his kingdom. The gospel contains the testi- 
moTiy of God respecting the Saviour and the salvation, the dignity 
of the Saviour's character and the efficacy and satisfactory nature 
of the atonement, and the completeness of the glorious redemp- 
tion. It contains also the divine promise, that whosoever is illumi- 
nated to believe the divine testimony shall be saved, and is by this 
faith justified. Such as nuike a credible profession of this faith we 
baptize and receive into fellowship with the church. 

On the first day of the week we count it our duty and privilege 
to meet, and joyfully commemorate the death of Christ as an atone- 
ment for sin, and bis resurrection as the pledge of ovu: v3&v.\fiRaJi«K?v,

as that by vir*\ie of «'hich we are raised to the "newness of life," 
nnd as the sample and th« earnest of our deliverance front the power 
of the grteve at the last day. In these exercises we think it our 
duy to promote the glory of God and our mutual edification. 

I« our social observances on the Lord's da^, we judge worship- 
ping "in spirit and in truth" to be chiefly important, rather than 
any particular arrangement of observances, or any particular bodily 
exercises in them. 

Except that we begin with praise and prayer, and interpose these 
exercises between the other observances, our general arrangement 
is tuken from the words, Acts ii. "thev continued u tne apostles' 
doctrine and fellowship, and in the breaking of %^ad, and in 
prayers." In the forenoon we commence with praise and prayer, 
cadi twice; the first referring to the Lord's day, the second before 
reading the word. Then we read in the historical part of the 0)3 
Testament, from the beginning to the end of either. ^Xe read also 
in those books caHed Ilagiographa, i. e. Job, and to the end of 
Canticles; and we read also in the historical part of the New Testa- 
ment, i. e. from the beginning to the end of the Acts of the Apostles. 
IVenext sing and pray with a view to the exercise, and attend to 
mutual exhortation and instruction; and then conclude with prayer, 
praise, and the dismission. In the middle of the day, as many of the 
members as find it convenient meet to a temporal repast, where we 
think it right to inquire after each other's welfare, and to cultivate 
familiarity with each other. This Is our love feast. In the after- 
noon we commence, as in the forenoon, with praise and prayer, 
each twice. Then we again attend to the apostle's doctrine by 
reading the prophets and apostles. We next praise and pray, with 
a view to the collection, or fellowship. We next attend to the 
Lord's supper, the observance being preceded by praise, and a 
reference to the institution, and thanksgiving preceding both the 
brea - and the cup. After the supper a hymn is sung, and then (for 
6ome time past) prayer, and a discourse by one of the pastors or 
preachers, and conclude as in the forenoon. In the forenoon the 
membe s of the church sit generally apart from others; in the 
afternoon, almost universally so. And we are advancing more and 
more in this, whilst we still forbear on it. Such is our ordinary 
procedu • ; but we dont think it essential. Until lately the Lord's 
supper Wis our last observance, except prrise and prayer. We 
stand at prayer and praise. In our ''measures and decisions," the 
voice (.f the church is fairly taken, and the minority generally fall 
in With the majority. The difftrence generally arises from ft mis- 
understanding, and is removed by explanation; or the difference 
may r late to a point not settled by the scriptures, and then it ought 
to be matter of forbearance. Tn exact a greater unanimity than 
this, leads, we think, to tyranny on one part and hypocrisy on the 
other, and n endless divisions of churches. Such is our mode, and 
we think ir warranted by the word of God. But we do not blame 
you fo- coinmencing with praj-er, though we think the words ''first

Regarding brotherly intercourse, and our conduct in the world

Such ehurches as ours have existed in Scotland, at Edinburgh

took place on the question of small societies, without pastorc, having 
a right to use the Lerd's supper. We took the affirmative of this

onltf baptized belitYert^ whilst tkey plead for admitting all true be-

brotherly exhortations on the Lord's day in the pubtic meetings of 
the church. Our members are about one hundred and eighty. 
Those of our sister church at Paisley about the same. There arc 
besides a number of churches, as at Perth, London, Uverpool,. &c. 
<cc. and many societies without pastors, with whom we are in (be 
habit of christian intercourse. 

We are, on behalf of the church here, who wish you grace^ 
mercy, and peacej in Christ Jesus, yours for the gospel's sake,

reformation, we dismiss them all with this general notice.

OBITUARY NOTICE. 

AFTER the first form of this number wai in type, on Monday, 
Oie 22d ult. at nearly 11 o'clock A. M. after a odious and painful 
illness of a consumptive character, which she bore with the utmost 
fortitude, patience, and resignation — departed this life, MRS. 
MARGARET CAMPBELL, consort of the editor of this paper, 
aged 36 years. The deceased was a christian in profession and 
practice, and did in her life and deportment for many rears recom'* 
mend the excellency of the christian profession to aU her acquaint- 
ance; and during her long illness, and in her death, she did exhibit 
to her numerous connexions and friends how tranquilly and cheer* 
hilly a christian can meet death and resign the spirit into the hands *% 
of a gracious and dirine Redeemer. *i die,*' she said, **withou4 
an anxiety about any thing upon the earth, having committed all 
that interests me into the hands of my faithful and gracious Heavenly 
Father — and in the confident expectation, of a glorious resurrection 
wheo the Lord Jesus appears unto the salvation of sd\ who trust HI 
him." Without an effort towards a eulogy or an encomium — with* 
out a single bias from the most endearing^ relation — we simply 
announce the above event for the information of a numerous ac- 
quaintance, widely extended, and as an apology for the delay of the \ 
present number beyond the usual time. Her dying address to her ' 
live burriving little daughters, we may, for their benefit and that 
of others, shortly lay before our readers in a subsequent number. 
"The Lord gave, and the Lord hath taken away. Blessed be the 
name of the Lord."

"WE ha'.c been much refreshefl, and ediiit;cl, by the com-

In compliance with your wish, we shall now proceed to give ycu 

.a brief sketch of our history as a church, and inform you of the 

manner in which we ccuiuct cur worship. In most respects it 

agrees with your practice, and where it differs, we shall mention 

to you the reasons of our conduct. 

^ It is about twenty years since we were first associated together. 
At that time, we observed the Lord's supper once a montn; and 
although we had a pistor, we also procured a succession of preach- 

^ ers from a distance, whose discourses were inoi'e addressed to those 
vho were without than to the church. 

Our first step towards scriptural order, was our beginning to 
break bread every Lord's day. In examining this subject we learn- 
ed, that the churches of Christ, to the end of the world, ought in 

; all things to be guided by the apostolic traditions. 

The subject of mutual exhortation and discipline on the Lord's 
day was next agitated. These had formerly been attended to at 
our weekly evenmg meeting, but wd became convinced, that what- 
ever is enjoined on the churches, should be oh&«r«^d c^w \\i^ ^^^^

came under our considc nation; and in consequence of many being 
biiptizcd, and mutual exhortation and discipline on the Lord's day 
being introduced, a considerable number left us, who still continue 
to assemble as an Independent church. This took place about ten 
yeai-s ago, since which time we have observed our present «rderr 

Our number is about two hundred and fifty. M'e have three 
elders and four deacons: we had four elders; but one of them 
(brother Thompson) has for many years been desirous of preaching 
Christ in foreign lands, and has left us with this intention. He was 
commended to the Lo^d for the work, by prayer, with fusting.and 
laying on of hands. lie sailed on the 12th instant from Liverpool 
for Buenos Ayrcs, as he considered the southern part of your con- 
tinent to be more neglected thSin any other missionary field. We 
request your constant prayers on his behalf. 

^ye meet at half past 10 on the Lord's day morning. After 
prayer by the presiding elder, (in which 1 Tim. ii. 1, 2. is partic- 
ularly attended to,) any case of discipline which requires to be men- 
tioned, is laid before the church; the names of those who have 
applied for fellowship are also read, and the result of the conver- 
sation which the elders and two or more of the brethren have had 
with them, is stated. If the church be satisfied they are baptized 
in the course of the week, and received next Lord's day. On 
their udmission they arc saluted with a kiss by the presiding elder, 
while the church stands up in token of approbation. We consider 
it necessity, not only to inquire into the views of the gospel which 
those \vI»o npply to the church entertain; but we endeavor to 
ascertain whether they are acting under its influence. We know 
from the testimony of God that the truth ^'oiks eflectually in all 
w ho'believe; but we sec muny who make a scriptural profession of 
faith without bringing forth the fruits of righteousness, and con- 
brqnently show that they are not standing in the true grace 
of God. Hence the necessity of inquiring into the conduct of 
men, since they have professed to know the truth. The example 
of scripture is clear on this subject. Paul's confession must have 
been unexceptionable; yet the disciples did not receive him till 
th.ey heard the testimony of Barnabas respecting his conduct. The 
piesiding elder then gives out a psalm or hymn, in singing which 
\\:c brethren juin, standing. A chapter is readfrom the Old Testa- 
Tj.ent, and a corresponding one from the New. (We go regularly 
through the Old Testament in the morning, and through the New 
in the afternoon.) One of the brethren is called on by the elder 
to engage in prayer, and at the conclusion of this and all our 
prayers the chu'. ch says JJmen, Praise. The elder, after a few 
observations on the Lord's supper, gives thanks, or calls on one of 
the brethren to do so. The bread is then handed about by the

On Wednesday evening we meet for an hour; when, after praise

and praise. On Friday we do the same, only the time is occupied 
by the exhortations of the brethren. 

Having thus, beloved, given you a full account of our order, we 
shall now make a few remarks on some points in which a siigli*: 
tlifferencc appears to subsist between you and us In doing so, we 
address you with all aiFection, and entreat you to bear with us. 
"We have all much to learn, and none of us imagine we have already 
attained, or are already perfect. 

1st. We do not kneel in prayer, we stand both in prayer and 
singing. This does not arise from thinking that kneeling is un- 
scriptural, but because our seats are not so constructed as to renucv 
it convenient, and we find both postures recognised in the word of 
God. 

2d, We are not quite sure whether we understand you, when you 
say, "Having no fellowship in sacred things with those who confess 
and obey not the faith." We have no idea of a believer having 
fellowship In worship with an unbeliever. "How shall they call on 
him in whom they have not believed?" But we have known per- 
sons who entertain ideas on this subject which we judge to be 
unscriptural; Not only do they maintain that the church should 
flit together, (which we approve and practise,) but they entertain 
fl dread of others teeming to join with them, of which we cannot 
perceive a trace in the word of God. Under the influence of this 
Apprehension, some intimate to those who are present that they 
are not to stand up when the church prays or praises; others do 
not read the hymns lest any but the church should sing. There U 
not a shadow of any such thing in the apostolic history. We find 

Srayer employed by the apostles in order to bring men to the 
nowledge of the truth. Acts zxvii. 35. xxviii S. and no appre- 
hensions are ever expressed of unbelievers appearing to join in 
worship by putting themselves in the same posture with believers. 
Besides, where any number attendthere generally are some disci- 
pies of Christ not connected with thechuTcKwi^^Xv^^WNaR-^i^^^^*

phy any man should fotbid them. We know it has been said w6 
might as well admit unbelievers to the Lord's supper as suflTef

by receiving them to break bread, we acknowleg-e them to be disci-

gives a false view of the encouragement given by Jesus to sinners; 
and while it has a ^Aow of faithfulness, it is calculated to foster a 
temper towards those who are without very different from what

dd. We observe that you attend to a love-feasf, but do not eoa»

church ought not to come together to eat and drink. We do not 
approve of liolding any religious service as not being of "strict 
oblij^ation." Every part of our worship is either commanded or 
not; if commanded, we are bound to obey; if not, it is ia fact 
proliibited. As to the church meeting on week days, it is not en- 
^ined; but social prayer, &c. is enjoined, and always proper when 
^rcumstances permit. 

4th. As to washing the feet, it was a piece of hospitality which 
was general In the east; the neglect of it was an evidence of wunl 
of respect, Luke vii. 44. but we do not consider ourselves bound

that it was intended as a reproof to his disciples, who, during sup*

at the time, but after supper rose and washed their feet, thus point- 
ing out to them the way to true greatness in his kingdom. C-jmpare

5th. The kiss of charity we consider to be very different. From 
the earliest ages a kiss has been the highest token of affection. It 
is not confined to any particular country, but being a natural ex- 
pression of love, is universally practised. Customs may change as 
to the ordinary expressions of good will to an acquaintance; but if 
a son bad been lost and were found, his father and mother would 
be impelled by nature to kiss him. The Lord does not interfere 
with civil customs, and in these his disciples ought not to affec*: 
singularity. As it is improper in believers to dress in a different 
manner from others, so when meeting on the street, they ought not 
to distinguish themselves by any peculiarity of address. But in the 
churches of the saints there is neither European nor Asiatic. 
Every distinction is lost in the character of disciples of Jesus, and 
to him alone all are to be subject. When he directs such a society 
to observe any thing, they are not at liberty to suppose that their 
obedience may be suspended on the local customs of the country 
in which they sojourn. Now the precept to salute one another 
with a holy kiss, is expressly given to the churches at Corinth and 
Thessalonica, 1 Cor. xvi. 20. 2 Cor. xiii. 12. 1 Thess. v. 26 But 
this, it is supposed by some, is only to be done on <*spccial occa> 
sions." We should be ^lad to know what these occasions are, for, 
respecting them the scripture is silent. The commandment does 
not refer to the occasional meetings of individuals, for it is given to 
the churphes, and includes all the brethren. If it be alleged, that 
although given to the church, it is to be observed by the brethren, 
not collectively, but individually— we reply, this is the very argu- 
ment adduced against mutual exhortation in the church; and those 
who do not practise salutation cannot, with any consistency, disap- 
prove of the sentiments of those who afRrm that the precepts to 
exhoit each other do not refer to the church when assembled, but 
to our intercourse as ndividuals. We believe, however, the true 
reason of the prejudices of some disciples against salutation in the 
church, is, that it appears to them formal and unnatural. No doubt 
all the oidinances may degenerate into form; for instance, our 
meeting on every £rst day of the week, and proceeding in the same 
manner, may be nothi.ig better than a form; we may draw scar to 
God wit)) our mouths, and honor him with our lips, while our 
hearts are far from him; and some have objected to the weekly 
observance of the Lord's supper on this very |;round. It is cer- 
tainly our duty to watch and pray against formality in our religious 
duties; but we do not see that we are moore V\^\A^ \Q\i^fiawv^\vi\\»aX

The question whether such ])ersonR should be received into tlie

BAPTIST. 103 

l9t. Tbere is no example in the New Testament, of any disciple 
being refused fellowship with the churches of the saints, altho' vari- 
ous differences of sentiment prevailed. A church of Christ is a 
school for training up his disciples, and we conceive the only terms 
of admission are, that they give evidence of belonging to him. 
Hence we dare not refuse to receive a believer, although unbap- 
tized. 

2d. We are expressly commanded to receive those who are weak 
in the faith, Rom. xiv. 1. **to receive one another, as Christ also 
hath received us to the glory of God," Rom zv. 7. Now, a disci- 
ple who holds infant baptism is, in this respect weak, yet he is in 
the faith; and, therefore, we think ourselves bound to receive him. 
AVe know it has been said, that the 14th and 15th chapters of the 
Uomans refer to things in the nselves indifferent, and that the pre- 
cepts above quoted, respect only such matters;' but those who 
nrgue thus, have not diiSy considered the. subject. Meats and 
drinks are doubtless indifferent in themselves; but their introduc- 
tion into religion is not a matter of indifference. The whole system 
of antichrist is founded on an attempt to introduce Jewish observ- 
ances into the kingdom of Christ. The observance of da^s is 
bpoken of in connexion with the precept to receive the weak be- 
liever, Horn. xiv. 5. and yet the apostle elsewhere declares, that 
the observance of days and times led liim to fear that the Galatians 
had never received the truth, Gal. iv. 10, 11. We, therefore, 
understand the precepts, to receive him that is weak in the faith, 
ill tliCir plain and obvious meaning, and consider them as referring 
■o any cn-or iRto whic^ a real disciple of Christ may fall. Of this 
v/e have a striking proof, 1 Cor. viii. where great ignorance on 
most important subjects, is declared to. be compatible with true 
.'lisripleship. 

od. We shall just refer to one more passage on this subject, 
I'hil. iii. 15, 16. "Let us, therefore, as many as be perfect, be 
:1ms minded: and if, in any thing, ye be otherwise minded, God 
hhall reveal even this unto you. Nevertheless, whereto we have 
alretd) attained, let us walk by the same rule, let us mind the same 
thing." This appears conclusive on this question, and, therefore, 
while Me pray that grace may be with all those who love our Lord 
.lesus Chiisl in sincerity, we cannot refuse to receive any of them 
who desire to observe the institutions on which we are agreed; and 
this v»e are convinced is the scripture way to unity of sentiment, 
which ougl ft constantly to be the subject of our fervent prayers. 

Such, beloved, are the observations which have occurred to us 
on the perusal of your letter. We regret that in consequence of 
the ccrrupt'ions introduced into the kingdom of Jesus, it is necessa- 
ry for his disciples to say so much about the external order of his 
churches. On this there ou^ht to be no difference, and the time is ap« 
proaching, when the existing differences shall be dona away, it 
would have been far more agreeable to us^ and we are assured also 
to >ou, to have written each other of the glory and dignity of the 
person of Immanuel; of the height, and depths and h^«.'%n^!ok^ vcv*^ 
'-njrth^ of his Jove; of the ftilness of Yuft 9Xotk«nkft.\i\\ q^^^V«,«^^'^'5» 



10* The christian 

of his salvation; oF the powerful obliga*ions under which we are 
kid to live devoted to Him who purchased us with his blood. We 
should have preferred dwelhng on the delight which we ought to 
feel in his service, the care we ought to take to adorn his doctrine^ 
walking before our houses with a perfect heart, worshipping God 
in our families, manifesting our delight in the meeting wiih our 
brethren, and meditating on it at home— -in short, whetiier we 
eat or drink, or whatsoever we do, doing all to the glory of God^ 

But we are very sensible, that it is necessary, diligently to search 
for the footsteps of Christ,s flock in regard to his institutions; for 
they are all calculated to promote that holiness, withou t which no 
man shall see the Lord, and all the contrivances and commandments 
of men in religion turn us from the truth. 

It is, however, highly important that we should be on our guard 
against the wiles of the devil. He is transformed into an angel of 
light, and, through the deceitfulness of our hearts, may divert our 
attention from that righteousness, and peace, and joy in the Holy 
Ghost, in which the kmgdom of God consists; while we are zealous- 
ly contending for those institutions, the real object of which is to 
f>romote every holy temper of mind. 

In the course of our experience, we have seen not a few, who, 
while they appeared to be advancing in the knowledge of the na- 
ture of the kingdom of Christ, were evidently loosing spiritu- 
ality of mind, and becoming much less exemplary in their con- 
duct than formerly. We have seen such make shipwreck of 
faith and a good conscience; while others, whose views they 
despised as being nearer Judaism than christianitv* have lived hon- 
oi*ah!y, and died triumphing in the hope of eternal life through 
Christ. 

Do we, therefore, account the ordinances of Jesus to be of little 
importance? Do we adopt the sentiments of those, who seem to 
think that the churches of Christ may do what seemeth good in 
their own eyes, according to their views of expediency? By no 
means, any more than we neglect the scriptures, because those 
who are unleared and unstable, wrest them to their own destruct- 
ion. But we wish to approve ourselves the servants of God, by the 
armour of righteousness on the right hand and on the left. 

It is our earnest prayer for you, beloved brethren, that ye may 
stand complete in all the will of God; that your light may so shine 
before men, that others, seeing your good works, may glorify our 
heavenly Father; that ye may be blameless and harmless, the sons 
of God,' without rebuke, in the midst of a crooked and perverse 
nation, shining among them as lights in the world. — Now the God 
of peace, that brought again from the dead our Lord Jesus, that 
Great Shepherd of the sheep, through the blood of the everlasting 
covenant, make you perfect in every good work to cJo his will, 
troi'kinff in yolk that which is well-pleasing in his sight, through 
Jcaus Christ; to whom be glory for ever and ever. Amen,

not only contends for a few dry abstract opinions, nicknamed

How loud and how long does the Arminian sing his frfie graces

answer, When a person derives more pleasure from the contem* 
plation of a tenet because of the opposition made to it, than he 
would, did nQ such opposition exist; or when he is more opposed^ 
to a tenet because of the system to which it belongs or the people

cy, he acts the sectary and not the christian: and so of all predilee- 
tions and antipathies, when they are created, guided, or controlled 
by any thing extrinsic of the subject matter itself.

REVIEW OF DR. NPELS CIRCULAR—JVo. /F. 
^'BEFORE the adversaries of creeds can boast of having* gained 
ftny thing in this controversy, it devolves upon them to do, wliat 
we apprehend cannot easily be done. They must exhibit some 
method, scriptural and practicable, of excluding corruption from 
the church without a creed." Thus speaks the Doctor's circular. 
"When my criticisms upon the Doctc^s use of the term creed, and 
bis definition of it, i^ion the term churchy and his application of it, 
•re remembered, the thin vail which conceals the sophism in the 
above period is removed. But should any person inquire for any 
other exposure, I will answer thus: Many churches are commended 
in the New Testament for detecting and excluding cori'uptions and 
oorruptors; for example, the church in Ephesus, A. D. 97, is thus 
addressed by Jesus Christ — ^**Thou hast tried them which say they 
are apostles and are not, and hast found them liars." They had 
also ti ied, detected, and **hated" the views and practices of the 
Nicolaitans. Now the question is, By what creed? Not by that 
ordained by Constantine; for this was not made till A. D. 325. Not 
by that of Dr. Luther; for that was published first A D. 1529. 
Not by that ot Dr. Calvin; for that appeared only in 1537. Not by 
that decreed at Westminster; for that was perfected A. D. 1640. 
Not by that made by Dr. Geo. Fox; for that was not known till A. D. 
1655. Not by that adopted by the Philadelphia Association; for 
that was regenerated A. D. 1742. Not by that made by Doctor 
Erskines; for that was born A.«D. 1733. Not by that finished by 
Dr. John Wesley; for that was not baptized till 1729. And most 
assuredly not by that made by Dr. Noel; for it is not yet finished, 
neither can be completed before the demise of the Doctor, unless 
he says he is as wise now as he ever can be, la^ ^Vvax <«^^^^^'«:^^

the conclusion of this article, and if they have any thing better to

cannot be better laid out than in sustaining truth against error. If 
they are of no value, they can of course hurt nobody. 

If, however, Querens thinks that I am bound to enter into a 
contest upon the many points in which I differ from my friend 
Campbell I must beg leave to diifer with him. Mr. Campbell's 
views are not new, at least, not many of them — Sandeman, Glass, ' 
the Haldanes, were master spirits upon this system many years ago. 
And they were eiTectually answered by Fuller and others. Mr. 
Campbell said in his answer to me, some time past, that Sandeman, 
as a writer, compared to Fuller and his compeers, was like a giant 
among dwarfs. It may be so, or, as Dr. Doubty says, it may not be 
so. I can say this, however: If Sandeman is a giant, he has been 
as completely beaten by the dtoarf Fuller^ as ever Gohath was by 
David. If I am called upon, then, to establish my assertions as to 
Mr. Campbell's views, I refer Qnerem aiyi all such to Fuller's work 
against Sandeman, &c. I do not know a word in it that I would 
alter. On those on which brother Campbell differs from, or rather 
goes farther than these transatlantic writers, I am willing to adopt 
the defence of our principles, as they have been exhibited In the 
Jiecorder, so far as I have seen that defence, under different signa* 
tures. I do not say, by the by, that I may not, at some future day, 
atteifhpt something furthar upon this subject. I am, however, from 
some cause, not fond of controversy, and never have been At 
my age, therefore, I should be rather afraid to embark upon so 
stormy an ocean. If, however, I should be induced to become a 
controversialist, I believe I should as soon enter the Ksts with my 
friendCampbell as any other, for three reasons — one is: on the points 
on which we diflfer, I am persuaded he is palpably on the wrong 
side^ and it would not be a hard task to make it manifest. A second 
is, he is so much of a champion, that to be beaten by him would 
not be so discreditable as it might be with some other antagonists. 
A third is, I think him a generous combatant with one who wishes 
nothing but fair play. 1 believe, however, it is best to let Mr. 
Campbell's systein confute itself by its effects; It has beenpracti« 
cally tried somewhat in England, more in Scotland and in the 
United States. What has been the result? To say the least, it has 
been found like many other schemes, more plausible in theory than 
in practice. They advance the sentiment that the scriptures are so 
plain that every person ' may comprehend them, and therefore re- 
quires no comment, no confession of faith, no creed. Yet among 
themselves, they find it impossible to agree; and hence most of 
those who have left Scotland, &c. with these viewsy have so far 
relinquished them as to amalgamate with other denominations or 
have dwindled to nothing. When I think of Mr. Campbell's 
talents, conjoined witli pleasant manners, and apparently a pious 
spirit, I am exceedingly grieved that he has been heretofore, and 
is likely ito be hereafter, of so little advantage to the cause. I can- 
not but hope that he will be brought to a more scriptural and more 
ratiopal course, if you think the above remarks will subserve the 
cauie of truth, you are at liberty to furnish them for the Jiecordav^ 
and let thera take their chance. Yours «LW^t\I\^Y«\ft\>j^

A. CAMPBELL. 

CUSTOM, 
i KNOW of no professions more insincere, more hdllow, and 
deceitful, than the professions of those who call Jesus of Nazareth 
their Lord and Master, their King, Lawgiver, and Judge, and are 
wilfully ignorant of, and negligent about, doing his will. Flew 
many are they who make Utese professions and in works deny 
them. The genuine feelings of many who profess the cbrisnan 
religion appear to be, O, Custom! thou art my supreme object of 
veneration. Shall I call thee Fushion, or shall 1 invoke thee as the ~ 
arbitress of decency and polite devotion? I love thee dearly. 1 hy 
service is honorable, easy, and requires no self-denial. If thou 
sayest. Meet in the courts of the Lord once a month, 1 desire no 
more. If thou preachest to us from a monosyllable, 1 hear thee 
with delight, and I revere thee as orthodoxy itself. If thy good 
will and pleasure is that we should commemorate the Lord's death 
once in a quarter of a }'^ar, my soul is delighted and satiated there- 
with: or if thou sayest. Once in a \ear, it is just as delightful. If 
thou ordainest the sprinkling of an infant, or the keeping of a fast — 
if thou commandest tythes and offerings — if fhou makest us to '^ 
worship our teachers, our creeds, or our fathers, it is more pleasing 
to our taste than any or all of the requirements of the New Testa- 
ment. We will worship thee all our days.. We shall sing, pray, 
and praise, and do extry thing thou commandest, and what thou 
dost not enjoin we will not practise. For thou, O! Fashion, makest 
all things easy to be done, and art therefore worthy of our homage 
both now and forever. EDITOR.

EDITOR. 

BURKE'S OPINION OP REFORMATION. 
REFORMATION is one of those pieces which must be put at 
some distance in order to please. Its greatest favorers love it bet* 
ier in the abstract than in the substance. When any old prejudice 
of their own, or any interest that they value, is touched, they 
become scrupulous, they become captious, and every man has 
his separate exception. Some pluck out the black hairs, some the 
grays one pomt must be given up to one» another point must be 
yielded to another; nothing is suffered to prevail upon its own prin* 
dplcMi the whole is so frittered down, and disjointed, that scarcely 
m trace of the original scheme remains! Thus, between the resist* 
ance of power, and the unsystematical process of popularity, the 
undertaker and the undertaking are both exposed, and the poor 
reformer b hissed off the stage, both by friends and foes.

FAREWELL ADDUESS 
Of Ma». Mahgaext Campbeli., ro htr daughttrn^ spoken to thenh 
in the immediate prospect of death. 
My Dearly Belovtd Children. ^ 

IT appears to be the will of our Heavenly Father to 
separate me from you by death. The only desire I have had to 
live for some time past was for the good of my family^ For myself 
I could expect to enjoy nothing more on this earth than I 
have already enjoyed, and, therefore, for my own enjoyment, it 
is much better for me to be taken away than to continue with you. 
But 1 am reconciled to leave you, when I consider that if I con- 
tinued with you I could not preserve you from evil. I might, indeed, 
advise you and instruct you; but if you hear not Moses and the > 
prophets, Christ and the apostles, neither would you be persuaded 
by me. And as to natural evils^ 'tie God alone who can defend you 
from these. You are all able to read the oracles of God, and these 
are } our wisest and safest instructors iii every thing. But 1 am 
reconciled to leave you from another consideration. I was left 
without a mother when 1 was younger than any of you; and when 
I reflect how kindly and how mercifully our Heavenly Father has 
dealt by me; how be watched over my childhood, and guarded my 
youth, an4 guided me until now, I am taught to commit you with- \ 
out a fear or an anxiety into his hands. The experience 1 have had 
of his abundant goodness towards me emboldens me to commend 
you to him. But you must remember that you can only enjoy bis 
favor^ and I can hope for his blessing upon you, only so far as you 
believe in, and obey him. I have said you can all read the holy 
scriptures. This is what I much desired to be able ^o say of the 
youngest of you, and it is with great pl<:asure I repeat it, You can 
all read that blessed book, from which 1 have derived more hap pi. 
ness than from any other source under the skies. The happiest cir- 
cumstance in all my life 1 consider to be that which gave me a taste 
for reading and a desire for understanding the New Testament, 
This I have considered, and do now consider to be one of the 
greatest blessings which has resulted to me from my acquaintance 
with your father. Although 1 have had a religious educat'on from 
my father, and was early taught the necessity and importance of 
reJiffion, yet it was not until 1 became acquainted with the contents 
of this hook, which you have seen me so o\\«t\ re^Av'vVvi^x I cu.me " 
^ understand the character of God, and Xo t^jo'b' ^ ^^^^ '-i-'-^^ "^^-^
