bounded confidence in all bis promises. And now I tell you» my 
dear children, that all your comfort and happinoss in this life, and 
in that to come, must be deduced from an intimate acquaintance 
with the Lord Jesus Christ. I have found his character, as deli- 
neated by Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John, ih their testimonies, 
exceedingly precious; and the more familiarly I am acquainted 
with it the more confidence, lovet peace, and joy, I have; and the 
more I desire to be with hnn. I say to you, then, with all ths 
affection of a mother, and now about to leave you, I entreat yoa, 
as you love me, and your own lives, study and meditate upon the 
words and actions of the Lord Jesus Ghrist. Remember how kind- 
)y he has-spoken to, and of, little children; and that there is no 
};oo<l thin^ which he will withhold from them who love bim an^ 
walk uprightly. 

With regard to your father, I need only, I trust, tell you that in 
obeying him, you obey God. For God has commanded you to 
honor him, and in honoring your father, you honvr him tnat bade 
you so to do. It is my greatest joy in leaving ymi, that I leave 
you under the parental care of one who can instnjct you in all the 
important concerns of life, and who 1 know will teach you to 
choose the good part, and to place your affections upon the only 
object supremely worthy of them. Consider him as your best 
earthly friend, and, next to your Heavenly Fn^Uer, your wisest 
and most competent instructor, guardian, atid guide. While ^t is 

' over you, br you under him, never commence, -nor undertake, 
nor prosecute any important object without advising with him. 
Make him ymir counsellor, and still remember the first command-

AS' to your conversation with one another, when it is not upon 
the ordinary business of life, let it be on-stibjects of importance, 
improving to your minds, I beseech you tn avoid thatlight, foeiisbj 
and vain conversation about dress, and fashion, so common among 
females. Neither let the subject of apparel fill ynur hearts, nor 
dwell upon your tongues. Yeu have never heard me do so. Let 
your apparel be sober, clean, and modest; but every thing vaia 
iind fantastic avoid. If persons wish to recommend themselves to 
the vain and the giddy, they will dress and adorn themselves to 
please such persons; but asl would deplore the idea of your either 
choosing or approving such companions, I would caution you, and 
untreat you to avoid the conversation, manners, and apparel, which 
would attract the attention of such persons— They are poor 
companions in Mckness and death; they are no helpmeets in the 
toils and sorrows of life, and, therefore, we ought not to study to 

.^ please them in the days of youth and health. 1 never desired to 
please such persons; if I had, my lot might have been, and, no 
doubt, would have been far different, l^o, my dear children, I 
obose the course which I now approve, and which, when leaving 
the world; I recommend to you. And! am sure you can never be 
more happy in any other course, tlum I have been in that which I 
recommend to you. Persons of discernment, men and women of 
good understanding, and of good education, w\\l v^v^^^^ '^l^'^^.

ANCIENT GOSPEL.— .Yo. /. 

BAPTISM. 

IMMERSION in water into the name of the Father, 
Son, and Holy Spirit, the fruit of faith in the subject, 
is the most singular institution that ever appeared in the 
world. Although very common in practice, and trite in 
theory, although the subject of a good many volumes, 
and of many a conversation, it appears to me that this 
institution of divine origin, so singular iti its nature, and 
so grand and significant in its design, is understood by 
comparatively very few. In my debate with Mr. Mac- 
calla in Kentucky, 182S, on this topic, I contended 
that it was a divine institution designed for putting the 
legitimate subject of it in actual possession of the remis- 
sion of his sins — That to every believing subject it did 
fonnalli/y and in fact, convey to him the forgiveness of 
sins. It was with much hesitation I presented this view 
of the subject at that time, because of its perfect novel* 
tjr. I was then assured of its truth, and, I think, pre- 
sented sufficient evidence of its certainty. But having 
thought still more closely upon the subject, and having 
been necessarily called to consider it more fully as an 
essential part of the christian religion, I am still better 
prepared to develope its import, and to establish its utilty

EDITOR, 

THE POINTS AT ISSUE. 

WE argue that all christian sects are more or less apostatized 

from the institutions of the Saviour; that by all the obligations ot* 

the christian religion, they that fear and Iqvc the Lord are bound 

to return to the ancient order of things, in spirit and in truth. Ou<: 

opponents contend that the sects are not apostatized; or, if thty 

admit that they are apostatized, they say the lime is not yet conjc 

toreturn, but that they must await the millenium. Let this pleu 

^^ a resforafioji of the anciail order «/ thin^^'s ercvViSVife \;\Wv. ^.v>\u!r* 



185 

it nay, or let tbit eontroTeny occupy what grpund it may, tbis is 
the nuLed question at issue. 

We have the concurrence of the wise and good, in all parties^ 
when^we assert that the christian church is not now what it once 
was in its hale and undeg^nerate days; nor is it now what it will be 
in the ^lor^ of Christ's reign upon the earth, in the period called 
*the millenium/' While many are content with merely affirming 
as above, we are not satisfied, neither can we be, without attempt* 
ing something in a subserviency to this glorious Restoration. We 
wish all our readers never to lose si|^ht of the points at issue. If 
creeds and systems, telts and teztuaries, synods and councils, rites 
and ceremonies, cooqe in review before us, let our readers remem- 
ber that these are but a few of <Jie items to be discussed in 8uh« 
servience to the grand question. 

Logic of the Ids and Outs^ or of the Populan and 

Unpopulars. 

I have long since discovered that there are two systems of log^c^ 
or two modes of reasoning, that seem to be almost uniformly adopt- 
ed by two elasses in society, irrespective of their religious or 
Solitical views. The Ins adopt one system, and the Outt another. 
\y the Jtu we understand those in authority with the people; and 
by the OutSt those not in authority with the people. The former 
are the Populars, and the latter the Unpopulars, The logic of the 
Jfu has in it the following rules:— 

1. Never submit any of those points essential to^our good stand- 
ing with the people, to the hazard of investigation. Remember 
you have something to lose, but nothing to gain. 

S. When your system is attacked, always extol the wisdom, piety, 
fn virtue of its founders; descant upon its antiquity, and enumerate 
its votaries. 

3. Ridicule the pretensions and expose the an*ogance of those 
who would dare to oppose names so revered, usages so ancient, 
and authorities so numerous. 

4. If possible, as far as lieth in your power, arraign the motifs, 
and impeach the aims of your opposers. 

5. Calumniate their characters, if you csn, under any pretence, 
and defame them, but with apparent regret th^t you liiould be 
compelled to do so. 

6. And lastly, when you are conscious that you cannot carry 
your point, represent your opponent as unworthy of your notice; 
give his system or his arguments the name of some obsolete heresy, 
and tell how it was blasted and refuted centuries ago. 

The logic of the Outs is not so easily reduced to one system as 
that of the Ins, If in politics, one system is adopted; if in religioD* 
another. But the general points of coincidence are — 

1.^ To submit every thing to the test of reason; and if in religion^ 
to revelation. 

2. Neither to adopt nor to oppose any point becatljse •£ the 
names of the pertoai who embrace or reject itv 

U T. 5. 



129 THE CHRISTIAN 

3. Canvaas the opinions and arguments of those whb ^oppose, 
without invading^ their reputation, or attempting- to injure it. 
When- the cause of the Outs h a good one, such is the s>stem of 
logic adopted. And even when it is not so good, there must be an 
apparent respect to the above decisions. 

To make this matter still more intelligible and apparent, we shall 
present a few remarks on 

Moral Authority. 

Political and moral authority, though different in some respects, 
are, in others, the same. The president of these United Stales is 
possessed of much political authority. So is the king of England. 
The popes of Rome have had very' extensive political authority, 
and still have a good portion of it. They still possess a ver>' great 
ecclesiastical authority ; but this in church government is the same 
as political authority in the state. But besides this authority, and 
distinct from it, they are possessed of an authority over the miiuls 
of men affecting their understanding and consciences. This is 
purely vihat we mean by moral authority. The different sectarian 
teachers have each a certain amount of this authority over the 
minds of the religious community amongst whom they labor, and 
indirectly amongst others. Some of tlie sects know the value of 
this authority, and how to ase It to the best advantage, much better 
than others. Convert this moral authority over the people into 
arithmetical numbers, and some of the sects poasess it in the ratio 
of ten, twenty, thirty, and forty millions of actual stock. In 
managing this stock there is a greut diversity of talent exhibited. 
Some of them manage their capita! stock so wisely as to make at 
count twenty-five per cent, per annum; while others, not so prudent 
in their affairs, cannot make it tell more than eight or ten per cent, 
per annum. I see, or think I see, through all the Bn£chinery of 
the involutlnns and evolutions of these sects, a constant attention 
to increase the capital stock; and some of them have blabbed our 
the secret too soon in anticipation of \Yhat was to be achieved 
through the immensity of their resources. The Mammoth Bank 
of these United States is not more formidable to the liule county 
corporations, than is the moral authority, ov the capital slock of 
influence, of the leading sects, to the small patrimony of the 
Sabba'arian or the Covenanter. But there is one tkii g which, 
above every thing elsr, is v or thy of r^ mat k while on this topic, and 
I have felt and seen its truth very cfien exhibited. It is the ease, 
the uncommon ease, with u hicb a person possessed of much moral 
authority can support any point against a person who rests kis cause 
upon truth and evidence alone. A single assertion of such a per- 
son is worth at least ten ^ood a-'gumentt of the dfsciple wl o has 
nothing but reason and tiie Bible to support him. A notable proof 
of this we gave in our last number. All the arguments in four 
Tolumes of this work in favor of the restoration of the ancient order 
of things, were set aside and proved to be erroneous by a single 
assertion frv>m Mr. Spencer Clack of Kentucky, who announced 
tAat **Si'm/tle and Campbell -were at iesue!" Those, therefore, po3-

Revivals, 

Some rumors and some symptoms exhibited not a hundred mil£% 
from Boston, within the last year, indicated that a r«t;if a/ was got up 
by some distinguished preacher or preachers for the sake of cover- 
ing a defeat, or of carrying some favorite point. There was a great 
deal said in some of the eastern prints on this subject to which we 
did not attend closely, as it was no way new or interesting to us, 
believing that such things were not very uncommon. It seals the 
mission of a man to bo "the instrument" of, or the great actor in, 
a reinval; pretty much the same as miracles did the mission of the 
apostles. Many un4erstand (his topic full better than I do, and 
know how to gain one hundred per cent, per annum to their actual 
stock of moral autliority. Had it not been that some of the drama- 
ih persov^e^ or the chief actors in these mighty movements and 
qrand excitements, have afterwards fullen into some most scanda- 
lous crimes, or r a' her /a/Z^M /row ^race, and thereby have given a 
seal to their mission which annulled the former seal, 1 do not know 
to what extent the moral authority of some men might have been 
augmented. The. fact of some having fallen Thto these notorious 
scandals, after having been the agents in great revivals; and anothet 
fact that revivals are often gp*anted at the same time to the bellige- 
rents in the held, or to those who are engaged in giving one another 
over to Satan, or in some bitter opposition through strife and envy; 
1 am at a loss to say whether we should not now have had many 
apostles and prophets, even more than they had in ancient times. 
But when we see a revival got up by two men, about the same time, 
indifferent parts of the country, who are opposing each other, and 
the one saying to the other, See how the Lord is blessing U8;«- 
(**but look how he has blessed and is still blessing usi") I say, when 
such is the fact, (as it is this very time in some places to my know- 
ledge,) revivjtls are divested of those miraculous powers which 
otherwise thry would possess, and are incapable of being made 
seals or attestntions to the mission of any of our textuaries. 

I am fully convinced that there are real and genuine revivals of 
rel gion at different times and places, and that much good has 
resulted from them; but there are so many mock revivals, that any 
doctrine can be proved to be true by them, and any preacher can 
he proved to be sent of God by them, if a revival under his labors, 
or attendant on his doctrine, will be admitted as evidence. 

I therefore judge of no doctrine or cause by the revivals that 
attend it. If 1 did, I cannot tell whether I should be a Cumberland 
Presbyterian* a Congregationalist, a common Presbyterian, u Bap* 
tist, of t^e Gillitef FuUeritc—oi the crccd^ «t cinli-cTt^d. ^cVw^;

whether I should be of the ^Chrittian Church," or of the *'Church 
9f Christ" — a Methodist, a Calvinist, a Unitarian, or a Trinitarian;

Saviour and his apostles, what saith the law and the testimony, 
^HERXFoas, must turn the beam, or decide the point with me. 

Those who consider all the revivals announced in the sectarian

act of social worship wiCh those people amongst whom the Father,

Church*' and the "Church ef Christ," the old side and the new

EDITOR. 

Review of Dr. NoePs Circular. 

jvo. r. 
MAN being in a great measure a creature of experi- 
ence, he is incessantly making experiments in order to 
better his condition. All the great systems now admired 
or extolled, in church or state, are the results of expe- 
riments. We are either ignorant of, or we have for- 
gotten the movements, and changes, and experiments in 
society, (hat have given birth to the present order of 
things in the world. One system has gradually de- 
clined, and another arisen upon its ruins, just as men 
either felt disposed from information or passion, from 
inclination or aversion, to begin something new. As the 
mighty oak has sprung from the small acorn, as the 
majestic river can be traced to some small fountain, so 
most of the great systems have sprung- from small be- 
ginnings, or can be traced to some feeble origin. In 
every thing but in the true religion man was left to learn 
by experiment. Unfortunately, however, the love of 
experiment, and constant attention to it on all other 
subjects, led some bold adventurers into the department 
of religion*, and thus it became the subject of experi- 
oient, Tike the common concerns of worldly society. 



BAPTIST. «9 

Forgetting that religion, in subject and form, was alto- 
gether supernatural, some attempted its accommodation, 
or, as it was called, its ^improvement to circumstances." 
TSow from this principle, however apprehended, felt, or 
expressed, has arisen every human system of re/tgioti 
now in Christendom. 

£ven the man who contends for ^^a summary," or any

the individual or social happiness of mair; I say, while

of all the horrid deeds of cruelty and murder attendant 
on the creed side of the controversy. For my part, I 
incomparably prefer to fraternize with all these blood 
guiltless heretics, than to have to fraternize with all the 
popes and inquisitors who have gorged themselves with 
blood of human sacrifice in order to sanctify their creed. 

But to return to the question which was partially dis- 
cussed in my last: It appears to me a little strange that 
Dr. Noel should impose a human creed upon a church 
to keep corruption out of it, and that I should oppose 
his imposition of a human creed upon a church for 
the same purpose, viz. to keep corruption out of it. 
Thfs is just as strange as that Bishop Semple should have 
thought himself opposing me. and that he should have 
been represeof ed by the initing editor of the Baptist 
Hecorder as opposing me, as at issue with me, on the

complished by a human erred, which has equally held

knowledge of the Holy Scriptures. I say, then, that

Revivals, 
Revivals are usually followed up by great declensions, and appear 
ia be «nder the same law of nature which requires the animal 
^stem to come down as many degrees below par as it was elevated 
ftbove par by extrinsic stimuli. Hence the cold season which fol- 
lows the warm season is as melancholy as the former was joyous. 
We rejoice to know that there are some exceptions, but they are 
comparatively very few. Let him that thinketh he standeth firm 
<^ke heed lest ke fall. 

Immersion* 
The Dover Baptist Association In Virginia reported an additioii 
of two thousand to its members by immersion during the last >ear. 
Crom the difierent accounts we have from all parts of the United 
6utes, from the different sects of Baptists; and from that sect 
called by themselves "the Christian Church," and by their oppO'» 
oentsthe JV«« Lights, (a name, by the bye, that several sects have

EDITOR. 

RESTORATION OF THE ANCIENT ORDER OF THINGS. 

JVO XXII. 
PSALMS, hymns, and Apirtual songs, embrace the praises of 
ohristians. Psalms are historic compositions, or poetic narratives. 
Hyrnn^ are odes of praise directly address' ng the object of worsliip, 
and declaring his exc«'lenctes and glorious works. Spiritual songs 
are such compo^tions as declare the sentiments derived from the 
revelations of God, and such as are adapted to communicate to 
.others the views and feelings which God's revelations suggest. 
Thus we define them. The reasons of this distribution are not 
obvious to all, nor is it needful to g^ into a labored criticism to 
establish it, as the end will be gained much better by an attention 
lo the classification we have made in this new selection of psalms, 
hymns, and spiritual songs, than by any antique independent of 
such a specimen. Our hymn books are, in general, a collection of 
every thing under the sun in the form of religious rhyme. Not 
one in ten, or, perhaps, in twenty, of any selection* are nstially 
sung by any individual from choice or approbation. And, indeed* 
the religious communities seem to be destitute of any fixed stand- 
ard by which to judge of what i« comely and suitable subject 
matter of social praise. As was said, the greater p.irt conceive 
they ought to sing every notion, speculation, or opinion, which 
they can imagine to be orthodox; not apprehending that tikc object 
of sacred song is to raise and exalt our spirits by divine contem^^lo^ 
lions to the sublime in the worship of out «LdoY«>a\<i ^q^ vxv^^^^^'tx^ 
Jfy admiring md extolling facta extT\ivs\c. <i^ w« cwC^^o^xa^-s. ^^

notions about them. But this is not all: every heretical or schi» 
matical dogma is sunpf, as well as preached; and instead of pratsinff 
Ciod, we are often acoldinif men who dift'er from us. For even 
prayer has been abused to this end. Often have I seen a prayer to 
be dictated by the presence of some one in the con^^regation; and 
thus all the congregation were doing homage to the zeal of the 
preacher, who was praying in relation to some influential errorist 
as he conceived. 1 knew a preacher who got into a violent con- 
troversy with another, because of aii insult he gave him in prayer. 
And Tiot long since a preacher has been called to order by the 
legislature of the first state in the union in point of population, for 
an insult to the nation while praying as chaplain for the legislature. 
This spirit, which on many other occasions manifests itself in 
prayer, is equally at work in the department of religious praise. 
So that all our contests about religion get into our prayers and 
songs. 

Let us analyze a few more specimens. There has been a con- 
troversy of long standing about faith. One hymn extols faith in 
the following words:— 

•Taith — 'tis a precious grace 

Where'er it is bestow'd! 
It boasts of a celestial birtb^ 

And is the gift of God. 

Jesus it owns a King, 

An all-atoning Priest; 
It claims no merit of its own^ 

But looks for all in Christ, 

To him it leads the soul 

When liird with deep distress^ 
Flies to the fotmtain of his blood> 

And trusts his righteousness. 

Since 'tis thy work alone. 

And that divinely free, 
Lord, send the spirit of thy Son 

To work this faith in roe," 

'Vaving any discussion upon the propriety of singing praises tft 

fiiith instead of the Lord, I proceed to observe that in s'.nging the 

above verses we are boasting against those who are supposed to 

maintain ti)at faith is not of a celestial birth, and not the gifV of 

God. In the conclusion the singer is made to act a singular 

part — first to declare that he believes that Jesus is a King, an 

all-atoning Priest; that faith leads the soul to him, flies to the 

fountain of his blood, and trusts his righteousness; and yet, after 

having sung all this, he represents himself as destitute of such a 

faith as he has been singing, and prays for the spirit of Jesus Christ 

to Tcovk this faith in him!!! How the same person can sing the three 

J9/vt reraes and the last one in this hymn I know not, unless they

that faith Is » precious grace, the gift of God, of cekttnl origiiu 
I believe that Jesus is King and an all-atoning Priest; that bis 
righteousness is worthy of my trust, and bis blood purges me from

i^hese verses, as well as the general scope of this song, are not 
Accordant with the spirit of the christian religion. The Holy 
Spirit Is always represented as the author of all goodness in us^ and 
is not to be addressed by men as though they, without it, could say 
that Jesus is Lord, or, without it, breathe forth a spiritual desire. 
Bat here dead ••cold hearts" are represented as panting after the 
Holy Spirit. — But not only does the nature of the christian religion, 
which represents the Father as the terminating end of all christian 
worship, the Son as the onlpr mediator between the 'Father and us* 
and the Holy Spirit as the immediate agent or author of all good- 
ness in us. Not only, I say, does the nature of the religion itseli^ 
to those who understand it^ teach the impropriety of^ direct address- 
es to the Holy Spirit; but this species of address is absolutely 
unauthorized by any prophet or apostle, by any oracle of Goo^ 
commandment or precedent in the sacred books — For from the be- 
ginning of Genesis to the end of Revelation, no man, patriarch, 
Jew, nor christian; prophet, priest, nor apostle, ever did address 
the Holy Spirit directly in praj er or praise. They pray for the 
Holy Spirit, but never to it. Thus Paul desired that the love of the 
Father, the grace of the Lord Jesus, and the communion of the 
Holy Spirit, might be with the saints. This hymn, then, is not 
only contrary to the genius of the New Covenant; but uncommand- 
ed and unprecedented in the book of God. This I asserted to an 
association about ten years ago, which caused an old preacher to 
«earch the whole Bible through tn disprove it. In something less 
than a year afterwards he wrote me he had found me in an error— 
for he had found an authority for this hymn. It was, he said, 
in the book of Canticles, where it saith, "A'vake, O North 
wind, and blow thou. South, upon my garden," &c. But the old 
gentleman hath not, to this day, decided whether the Holy Spirit 
was in the North or in the South wind, and therefore, as ye^ 
nothing has been adduced to show the assertion unfounded.

IT was not from inattention, nor a want of impression 
of the importance of the subject of your communication, that tve 
did not at iirst fully reply to you. The union of all who believe in 
the Lord Jesus, is a thing for which we are most deeply interested; 
ftnd the almost total want of it among the churches of Christ, that 
we deem on the whole nearest to the model of the first churches 
Ss a thing that causes to ua the most unfeigned sorrow. If that 
brotherly intercourse, and earnest care for each other, that sub- 
sisted among the churches in the days of the apostles, is not now 
to be found among those who profess to follow their practice, as 
far as it was approved by Jesus, the causes ought to be sought out 
and removed. In our opinion the chief of those causes is not the 
difference of sentiment, great and greatly to be deplored as this is; 
l^ut is owing to the exercise of an authority never conferred on the 
<^hurchesby the Lord Jesus, to refuse or exclude, for difference of 
sentiment, any of those who give evidence that they have been 
Sought by the blood of Jesus Christ. Mot that we deem it a matter 
i>f slight importance that all the disciples of Christ should know 
and practise all his institutions; on the contrary, we hold this a 
inattcr of very great importance, for the attainment of which all 
the churches ought never to cease to plead with their Heavenly 
JpAther, l^norannA of Rny A\y\n^ inalUuUon is an evfl, and must be 
felt as such by a church as far as it exists in any of the body. But 
the question is. What is God's way of getting rid t)f tliis evil? We 
believe, fVom Phil. iii. 15 and numerous passages of scripture tck 
which there is not room to refer in this letter, that it is by forbear* 
ance, affectionate instruction, and prayer. Many, on the contraty» 
have thought that the most effectual way lo make a disciple receive 
an ordinance of Jesus, is to refuse htm fellowship till he has com- 
plied. Notwithstanding all we have heard in fovor of this plan, 
we still deem it the wisdom of man. Accordingly we have found 
that God has made foolish this wisdom. Long has it been tried 
without success; and of late in some parts of Ireland it has been 
carried so far, that some individuals can scarcely find a second to 
unite with them in constant fellowship. By permitting Satan to 
work them up to this phrenzy, it appears to us that God has affixed 
his seal of disapprobation on the sentiment in its lowest degree, 
and to lead sober-minded christians, who have been led away by 
its plausibility, to examine more attentively the ground of their 
opinion. 

You will observe, then, dear brethren, that we do not plead f>)r 
fbrbearance as a useful scheme left to our own discretion, or justify 
it, as some have done, from that pleasing varieiy found among the 
works of God. Such language we hold in utter abliorrence. 
Variety in the works of creation is a beauty; but God is the author 
of that variety. Difference of sentiment upon every thing reveal- 
5^ /y^ pod is an evil, because it is the sinful ignorance of men* 



BAPTIST. ISr 

C!an.God command all his people to know his will, and shall it be 
a perfection to be variously ignorant of this? It detracts, then, 
considerably from the joy with which we should have received 
your letter, that we find no notice taken of this subject; but on 
the contrary, that you seem to make baptism a term ot fellowship^ 
The greater part m our number not only haVe been baptized, but 
we are convinced that views on this subject extensively affect other 
matters in scripture. But we all deem that the man who has been 
received by Jesus, ought not to be rejected by us; and that if he 
feed his people by his ordinances, it would be criminal in us, as 
far as lies in ouf power, to join in confederacy to starve the weak* 
est of them. We think that the man who has been admitted to 
the fellowship of the general assembly and church of the hrst 
born, is undoubtedly worthy of a seat with us. Dear brethren, we 
know what has been objected to our views on this subject, and as 
we have not had time fully to reply to objections, we deem it un- 
necessary to state all the grounds of our opinion. We know that 
there is no command of Jesus but may be plausibly set aside. We 
entreat you to examine this subject, recollecting that if it be sinful 
to receive any that Christ has forbidden, it is also sinful to refuse 
any that he has invited. There is no safe side in error. That Jesus 
will not approve of refusing fellowship to any of his brethren 
known to be such, appears to us to have the irresistible light of 
self-evident truth. 

With respect to the ordinances which you observe on the first 
day of the week, we agree with you in general; but with respect 
to the order of observing these ordinances, we find but little fixed 
in the New Testament. The only thing we can with any confi- 
dence say we have learned on this subject is, that, at whatever 
time a church meets to observe the institutions of the first day of 
the week, the Lord's supper ought to hold a distinguished place. 
But how often we should sing or pray, or whether we should pray 
or sing first, we find nothing fixed. Though we should have no. 
objection with you to commence with prayer, yet we could not say 
that we considered ourselves bound to this order by 1 Tim. ii. 1.' 
Prayer fer our civil governors we consider an important duty; but 
the above passage does not appear to us to determine the time of it. 
^irst o/'a^ appears to us to refer to the order in which the apostle 
brought forward the subjects of exhortation. He had been speak« 
ing of his own deplorably wicked character, and, from the abound- 
ing mercy of €iod to him, he concludes that they ought not to 
despair of any man's salvation. Therefore, prayers, -&c. ought to 
be made even for pagan and persecuting rulers; for the grace that 
saved Saul of Tarsus, was able to save the vilest of them. I exhort, 
then, first of all, that prayers, £:c. The second thing he exhorted 
to was the deportment of women. From this he passed to the 
-qualifications of bishops. This, dear brethren, is our view, which 
we do not obtrude upon you; but, as you have invited our faithfuls 
ness, we suggest to your consideration. Should we, at any time, 
perceive your view to be just, we shall. most promptly adopt it. 
We conceiTC that whatever Christ has not fix«du»»X^^V^\^\n5^

for ever, and that a church, though it may usually pursue the sane

The order in which we observe the ordinances, on the first day 
of the week, is as follows: — Salutation, singing, reading the scrip* 
tures, prayer, singing, admission ur exclusion, if necessary, the 
Lord's supper, singing, fellowship, exhortation, teaching, prayer, 
singing, prayer. But we do not consider ourselves bound to this 
order any further than we find it fixed in the scriptures We have 
no meetings on any day but the first day of the week, nor any meet- 
ings but one on that day. We consider 1 Cor. xiv. 16. to warrant 
us to subjoin an ^men to the prayer. Your view of the kiss of 
charity does not satisfy the most of us. We think the limitation 
arbitrar}', tliough some among ourselves have not yet observed it 
in any sense. The washing of feet we do not consider an ordi- 
nance, but the selection of one of the most humiliating offices, to 
inculcate the practice of all,"^'hen the brethren need them. It 
lias not to us the least appearance of being enjoined to be observed 
in form. A love feast, as an ordinance, we consider as unscriptu- 
ral. The passages that speak of it we view as referring to the 
Lord's supper. Is any feast so much a feast of love as this? tic- 
aides, we are forbid to eat in the churcli for the gratification of 
appetite. But what surprises us most, is the ground on which you 
hold it. You consider it net of strict obligation, and therefore 
sometimes omit it. \Vq think this inconsistent with all your other 
views. If Christ has instituted a love feast, it must be strictly 
obligatory; and if he has not, you will have no praise from him in 
observing it in his name, though ever so seldom. We see nothing 
to prevent the wealthy bretliren from entertaining the church in 
their own liouses, but we should beware of adding to the institu- 
tions of Christ. 

We met as a church in May, 180r. There never was any schis- 
matic separation from us. I5ut on account of convenience, two 
churches have gone out ft*om us; the one meeting at Maghralt, about 
four miles from us, the other at a country place called Cavindaisy, 
distant about six miles. The former of these did not get on well, 
and has returned to us. The latter continues to prosper, and lives 
in the tiimost harmony and confidence with us. Our number i»

a s^ep or two, and made three low nbeisunces. His lordship then 
advanced) and having uncovered the bread, bowed three times as 
before; then laid his hand on the cup, which was full of wine, with 
a cover upon it, which having let go, he stepped back, and bowed 
three times towards it; then came near again, and lifting up the 
cover of the cup, looked into it, and seeing the wine, be let fall 
the cover again, retired back, and bowed as before. Af\er which 
the elements were consecrated* and the bishop^ having first receiv- 
ed, gave it to some principal men in their surplices, hoods, and 
tippets; towards the conclusioa, many prayers 6eing said, the 
solemnity of the consecration ended.

AVHOEVKR reads the sacred pa^^es, wUh an enlightened and 
attentive mind, will discover that the operations of the Spirit of 
<i(k1 are various and manifold. To this wonder- working: Agent are 
ascribt-d creative energy, — miraculous events, — extraordinary qual- 
ifications, — and sanctifying influences on the souls of men.' It is 
only "parts of liis ways" that we can undertake to speak of; or, 
indeed, of which we can have a conception. Those classes of 
divine operations, which appear more immediately to concern the 
salvation of men, are, the miractdovs and the savctifyivg. Of the 
first class of these operations, it is not my intention now to treat: 
and, indeed, any attempt of this sort, on my part, is au.ply and

those uf a sanctifying nature, will furnish the subject for this

shex't and plain. 

The view which I wish to exhibit contains three points: First, 
the reality of a divine influence on the souls of men, in effecting 
the work of salvation; secondly, some of the principal effects 
produced by this operation; and thirdly, the high practical import 
of this truth. To the first only I can attend in the present number. 
And here 1 desire it may be observed, that I do not assume either 
Cahnnian or ArminMan ground, as being either of them exclusively 
necessary to this view. It is on ocriptural ground that I propose 
to proceed: about any other term that mtiy be used, I am not 
solicitous. 

First, then, I lay down this position: 1h:it the influence of the 
Holy Spirit on the souls of men, in effecting ihe work of salva- 
tion, is a scriptural fact. That many have abused this sacred 
truth, by wild and fanciful imaginations^ is ve3i<L\W v:.':i\\r',^^^^-. — '^.~.

These quotations appear to have reference to the case of believ- 
ers; — to their needs and their supplies. If believei^s must have the 
Divine Spirit to enable them to bring forth the fruits of righteous- 
ness, and prepare them for ultimate glorvj then well might we 
opine that the unre^enerate need the influences of that Spirit, to 
bring them into a gracious state: and this accordingly we iind to 
be the fact. Christ assures Nicodemus, John iii. 7. that men **must 
be born again;" and this new birth is said, verse & to be "of the 
Spirit." The Spirit, then, of course, b necessary to the production 
of that change, without which there is no salvation. The Ephe- 
•ians> io reference to their unbelieving, unregenerate state, are 
sepresented as having been <*dead in trespasses and sins;" £ph. ii. 
1. — in verse 5 the apostle includes himself, as in the same condition; 
and in both places ascribes to God the quickening (or life-giving) 
influence which they had experienced. In verse 10 the figure is 
ohanged; but the same idea is presented of a divine energy in their 
conversion to God: *<For we are bis workmanship, created in Christ 
Jesus unto good works." I am aware, indeed, that the figures 
employed to express this important change, have often been abused; 
and that divine truth has thus been misrepresented by an extrava* 
gant zeal to establish some particular system: but surely there is an 
analogy which justifies the use of such fignres;— there is a strong 
meaning intended to be conveyed;— a meaning which goes obvi- 
ously to shew our natural alienation from Ciod— our destitution of 
the principle of holiness'-and the necessity of an influence from 
the Divine Spirit, to restore us to a meetness for thei heavenly in- 
heritance. Let one more particular reference suflice. In Titus iii. 
5. salvation is ascribed, not to works of righteousness performed 
by us, but to divine mercy, "by the washing of regeneration and 
renewing of the Holy Spirit." Comment here seems unnecessary; 
as I cannot conceive bow language could more explicitly represent 
the agency of the Divine Spirit hi the work of conversion. 

I have mentioned above our natural alienation from God, and our 
destitution of the principle of holiness: and I here take occu&ion to 
repeat what I have before »:ud— >that this stale of human nature^ 
(which is so plainly held out in various parts of the sacred writings,) 
appears to be the ground of that necessity which exists, for a super- 
natural, regenerating influence from the Holy Spirit. But here il 
may possibly be objected that, allowirg such to be the state ol* 
man, the Holy Spirit has so fitted the vord of truth to our cpiuliticn, 
— has so adapted the means to the end, that no farther divine agency 
than what was employed in prBduein^ this word of tiuth, should be 
requisite in effecting the desired end. To such an argument I 
would replyi first, that a fair construction of the passages quoted, 
and of others that might be quoted, will not allow of such an ideb-. 
and secondly, that the fitness of the word to the condition of mar. 
/# 770 Argument that regeneration and sanctiiication will follow, 
without a divine influence accompanying the truth:— no more, I 
^»^ a valid argfumcnt^ in this case, wioiu \.\. wovsXCk, Vi^V'i «:'c^'^\^vv«i. 



BAPTIST. 146 

thai because seeds are adapted to vegetationi we may therefore 
expect a crop without the influence of sun or rain. That there is 
a happy, a beautiful adaptation of the word of truth to the conditioii 
of man, I readily admit: indeed it is one of my favorite ideas: this, 
however, does by no means supersede the necessity of a divine, 
spiritual influence, to g'ive eflect to the truth revealed. 

But possibly it may be farther suggested, that the same effects 
are, in many cases, ascribed to the ivord^ which are also ascribed to 
the Spirit, This too is admitted; and I may add, the same effects . 
are, in some instances, ascribed to the preacher, as the dispenser of 
the word. Thus, we are enlightened by the Spiritt **Open thou 
mine eyes, that 1 may behold wondrous things out of thy law;" — 
and we are enlightened by the vford: "The entrance of thy word 
giveth light.** We are born again of the Spirit: **So is every one 
that is born of the Spirit;*' — and we are born again by the vord: 
''Being born again, not of corruptible seed, but of incorruptible, by 
the word of God," &c« M'e are sanctifled by the Spirit.- "But ye 
are washed, but ye are sanctified," &c. **by the Spirit of our God;" 
— and we are sanctified by the word: **Sanctify them through thy 
truth: thy word is truth.'* It belon^fs to God to open the eyes and 
to turn the sinner;— and Pmil was sent to the Gentiles "to open 
their e} es, and to turn them from darkness to light." These in- 
stances are sufficient to illustrate the fact which has been admit- 
ed; — that the same effects are, in some instances, ascribed to the 
Holy Spirii— to the word of truth — and to the preacher or pub- 
lishtr of the gospel. It remains for us to see how this matter is 
to be understood. 

Briefly, I remark, that the same things are ascribed to different 
objects, prttty much in the way in which the same effect is ascribed 
lo the agent and to the instrument. My pen, the instrument, being 
adapted to the purpose of writing, forms these letters; and I, the 
agent, giving my pen direction, form these letters. The seed and 
tl»e earth produce vegetation: the sun and the rain produce vege- 
tation; and, in a certain sense, the man who sows the seeds and 
cultivates the earth, may be said to produce veg^etation. I know, 
indeed, that such figures cannot adequately represent spiritual and 
moral objects. They are introduced only by way of illustration; 
and I do by no means intend, by the use of them, to reduce men to 
mere machines, or the operations of the Divine Spirit to mere 
phjsical energy. When Paul says to the Corinthians, "Ye are 
manifestly declared to be the epistle of Christ ministered by us, 
written not with ink, but with the Spirit of the Living God," — 
thei'c is a beautiful analogy which justifies the use of the figure; 
and we see in it the agency of Christ, the instrumentality of the 
preacher, and the influence of the Spirit. But he who should 
undertake to disprove the moral agency of man, would, it is pre- 
sumed, pervert the truth by the abuse of a metaphor. If, however, 
on the other hand, one should be disposed to attribute to the effi- 
ciency of the instrument, what belongs to the efficiency of the 
agent, the apostle would certainly correct his error, by saying **WK<\ 
Jhen is Paul, and who is Apollos, but iu\i\*vs\ti^\j'^ >i\\Q^>j^\\^\^'S^^

The sum of these remarks on the effects ascribed to the Spirit 
and the word is this: that the word of truth is (iod's gront 
instrumei¥t in effecting our salvation. By this, or with this, liis 
spirit operates to the renewing and sanctifying of the soul; while 
under its influence, the soul itself becomes active in holy exercises: 
and thus, with Peter, we may say to believers, ''Ye have purified 
your souls, in obeying the truth, through the Spirit." ICow God 
may otherwise work, I know not; though I would by no means 
<^limit the floly One of Israel,'' as to his designs or operations, in 
any respect whatever. 

i now dismiss the first position— the reality of divine influence 
on the souls of men, in efl'ecting the work of salvation. This was 
my leading object in the present undertaking. The other two 
points proposed will probabl)' he treated on with more brevity. 
they must be reserved, however, for another number.

Ihem, or, in icripturc language, keep them in memory, hew 
thankful ought we to be for this ordinance, each first day, which 
brings to our minds and memories (Christ's dying for our sins, that 
we might iiot perish, but have everlasting life. Away, then, v.itli 
monthly, quarterly, half yearly communions, as limes fixed on by 
men, and not by God, periods which have been and still arc oppo- 
sed to the interests of the saints, and let the will of the Lord Jesus 
in his ordinance, as expressed by the example of the first churches, 
be our only rule, and by thus walking by his word, we will be jus* 
tified whatever men may say. Whereas, dept nding on distance of 
time and change of circumstances, for justifying our departure 
from any part of the word of God, is at best but a sandy foundation. 
The mass of the great body called christians, practise and justify 
this departure; but we will esteem their applause buthttle, and the 
having had it will be but poor consolation to us, if we have been 
found opposing the truth when called upon to appear before the 
judgment seat of Christ.

To conclude a disquisition which already has far transcended'its 
due merits, I will just state and briefly illustrate a single position 
of some importance in this controversy. It is this: These heretics, 
called A nans, Socinians, U nivt rsalists, Cntholics, Protestants, &c. 
&c. are kepjt in existence and their numbers augmented by the 
reasonings of such philosophers as Dr. S. M. Noel. Destroy the 
Sucinians' creed, and where will you find the Socinians? Let all 
his glosses, interpretations, and dogmas, be a dead letter, neither 
printed nor read, neither spoken nor heard, and where would the 
sect be in a generation or two? It would be so of sill other sects. 
Consequently, these creeds keep up all these sects. Now each 
one contends for his creed as better than all others, and thus justi- 
fies himself to himself. But let him remember that this creed 
derives all its consequence and superiority in his own estimation, 
from a comparison of it with others. If there were none with 
which to compare it, its excellencies would be all invisible. If, 
instead of making inquisition for opinions; if, instead of condemn- 
ing a person for his interpretations, glosses, or dogmas, we, as 
worshipping congregations, turned our ^WexvCx^xv V.^ \\\^ Xi^^-^Nx^^v

immorality', and indevotion — the church and the world would very 
soon exhibit a quite different aspect. If a man speak of any 
christian topic in any other languag-e than did the apostles, hear 
him patiently. If he will force his opinions upon us in terms cov- 
traryto those furnished by the Holy Spiiit, le* us admonish him. 
But if he will not be admonished, and still aims at imposing* his 
opiniont ot terms upon us, he then becomes what the new Translation 
calls *^a factionist;** and the Common, **a heretic;" and after a 
second admonition we are commanded to rejt ct such a person. 
Not because of his opinions, even at last, but because of his dog- 
matical and faction-making spirit. If a man speaks of God oui' 
Father, and of Jesus Christ our Lord, in all the sentences anA 
Words of the Holy Oracles; if to this unexceptionable style he adds 
a holy, devout, moral, and unexceptionable life, the church of Jesus 
Christ has nothing more to inquire of him, or require from him. 
If he should be called a Socinian, a Universalist, a Presb} terian, 
or a Methodist, it matters not; if he speak in Bible terms oiily, no 
man can justly impeach him, and by his works he must stand ot 
f^W in the estimation of all christians, as by these he must stand or 
fall in the day of the Lord Jesus. Now in this course it is easy to 
proceed without synod, or council, or creed. For if any man call 
Jesus Christ a **mere man" op a **mere creaturey** or an "eternat 
Sojiy" and will insist upon our calling him so; we open the Bible, 
and call for the express warrant. None can be produced. I'hrs 
being easily decided, the person is admonished; if he will stiM 
insist upon it, we admonish hnn a second time; and if still unre- 
claimed, we cast him out, not because his opinion differed from 
ours, but because he would lord it over our faith and conscience, 
and aimed at heading a faction. This most unquestionably is the 
meaning of all that is said upon this subject in the Bible. 

^ow to test the correctness and utihty of the course advised, we 
have only to consider how it would operate if universally adopted. 
Then if the result be obviously salutary on a general scale, no man 
can object to it either in principle or practice, if when only adopted 
by a few it does not do as much as if adopted by ail. And this is 
precisely the logic and drift of all that the creed advocates, whether 
Catholic, Presbyterian, or Baptist, urge against our logic in this 
controversy. They say they must have a creed because other 
sects have a creed; and that although they should adopt the scrip- 
tures only, still sects wotdd exist, and controversies would not 
universally cease. If there is any propriety in their logic, these 
sects or heresies, creeds and divisions, must, in defiance of the 
hopes of a Millenium, continue to destroy the peace and happiness 
of society for ever, or while time endures.

Brother CampbeV, 

A PERSON situated as you are, must submit to be assaulted 
on all sides, both by the wise and ignorant, and sometimes by 
persons of whom you have never heard, (which I apprehend is 
the case with myself.) Without further preface I shall proceed to 
inform you that I am one of that class of people called ** Christians," 
and that I have for a number of years been received as a teacher 
or **preacher" among them. Ten years ago I believed in Jesus, 
and entered the field as a soldier under the banners of the King 
of Heaven. I earnestly desired the salvation of sinners, and could 
not enjoy comfort whilst I refused to invite them to come. I had 
from my infancy been taught that God by his Spirit, abstract from 
his word, did, in every age of the christian dispensation, call 
certain characters to preach the gospel; and that these charactei'S, 
8o called, acted under the same commission as the twelve apostles. 
This tradition, the feelings of my soul, and the opinion of those to 
whom I looked up as teachers in Israel, confirmed me in the belief 
that I was one of *the called of God." Accordingly I went to 
work, and for about eighteen months labored in tde word and 
doctrine, and some in tradition, (as I appropriated every thing said 
of the apostles to myself, and firmly believed myself an ambassador 
of Christ; that to me was committed the word of reconciliation, and 
that I had this treasure in an earthen Vessel,) kc At the end of 
this time an old brother ambassador took in hand to have me 
••ordained." Accordingly inquiry was made of the church to 
which I belonged if they had any objections to the measure. They

ki answer to a certain question, you say that the work of an 
evangelist is preaching the gospel to those who have never heard 
it. Paul left Timothy in Macedonia on a certain occasion; he then 
wn)te certain things to him that he might know how to behave 
himself in the house of God, which is the church of the living 
God; and both the epistles shew that Timothy's business was 
chiefly among believers; and Paul concludes the whole matter by 
exhorting Timothy to do the work of an evangelist, and make full 
proof of his ministry. This consideration has induced me to 
request that you find both time and room for the proof of your 
assertion. 

In your last number of the Christian Baptist you say, **The 
Mahoning Association did one good work at their last meeting: 
they agreed to support one active, spiritually-minded, and able 
brother, as a messenger of the churches, &c. Brother Walter 
Scott accepted of the appointment," Sec, Now, brother Campbell, 
I confess [ ara at a loss to understand you; and as 1 consider the 
matter an important one, I wish information on the subject. No 
man under the heavens has said more against the divine right of 
associations, synods, councils, conferences, &c. than A Campbell, 
and no man has spoken to better purpose on the subject. I now 
ask, If they have no divine right, by what authority did they act 
when they made the above appointment, and how are you justifia- 
ble in styling the person so appointed the Messenger of the 
Churches? A person that so ably advocates the restoration of a 
pure speech ought to be careful not to use tiie language of Ashdod. 
You know, accurding to the best historical account we can get, 
that for more than a century after C^irist the churches were per- 
fectly independent of each other, neither were they joinea to- 
gether by association, confederacy, or any other bond than charity: 
and I know that I need not tell you that your association is an un- 
scriptural institution, and how can an unscriptural association act 
according to the gospel? I would have thought it a good work if 
they had made their will, and voluntarily agreed to die, and ap- 
pointed brother Scott to preach their funeraL But, to be serious, 
I see as great an incongruity between your messenger and the 
messengers of the churches mentioned in the New Testament, as 
you can see between the present order of preachers and a New 
Testament bishop. The messengers mentioned in 1st and 2d 
Corinthians were brethren appointed to travel with the apostles to 
carry the liberality of the Gentile churches to the poor saints at 
Jerusalem. Your messenger is appointed by the Association, in 
the name, and likely by the authority, of the churches who com- 
' pose the Association. But where do the churches find any ac- 
count of the ancient churches sending messengers to such an 
assembly, with the power to act for them there? I am certain 
they do not find it in the New Testament. 

There are seven messengers mentioned in the first chapter ot 
llevelations, and, according to your view of their office, if it be 
proper to call it an office, they were men appointed to visit the 
f>!{l apostle in his banishment, and administer to bis wants. This I 



164 THE CHRISTIAN 

baye no dpubt is the fact. But does not the circumstance of (he 
Son of God walkings in the midst of the seven candlesticks and 
holding^ the seven stars in his rig-ht hand, indicate that their busi- 
ness was something more than what you have stated? Is there not 
room for a stronif presumption that they were men appointed, not 
only to administer to the wants of the apostle, but to teach that 
faith to others which the churches had professed, and that each 
church had one such messenger? 

What I have written I submit without any apology to you as to a 
friend, a brother in Christ, and request the favor of a private 
answer to what I have written, as I have no desire to appear in 
public in my present unlettered situation, hoping that you will not 
cast my uncouth production to ttie moles and to the bats, without 
giving me a christian answer; and wishing you every blessing you 
desire for time and eternity, I subscribe myself your brother in the 
bonds of the gospel. W.

Isr. I believe, that, aside from fashion or •ustom, there is but

lodged from its body, and that it is very unsuitable, undignified, 
and even detestable to apply it to Deity. 

4th. I believe. That if ever there was a being in the universe of 
God, that might, with propriety, be called the Holy Ghost, it was 
the human soul of Jesus Christ, while in its three diys of a disem- 
bodied state, (t. e. in hell, or rather hades.) Rut /do not so call it; 
neither have 1 heard of any one that does. (See Psalm xvi. 10 —

THE DYING MOTHER. 

She made a f ign ^ 

I'o bring her babe— 'twas brought, and by her plac'd; 
She look'd upon its face, that neither srnil'dt 
Nor wept, nor knev who gazed upon 't, and laid 
Her hand upon its little breast, and sought 
For it, with look that seem'd to penetrate 
The heaven— unutterable blessings, such 
As God to dying parents only granted 
For infants left behind them in the world. 
"God keep my child," we heard her say, and heard 
No more; the Angel of the Covenant 
Was eome; and faithful to his promise, stood 
i Prepared to walk with her thro' death's dark vale, 

^ And now her eyes grew bright, and brighter still* 

Too bright for ours to look upon, suffused 
With many tears, and clos'd without a cloud. 
They^iet as sets the morning star, which goes 
Not down, behind tlie darken'd west, nor hides 
Obscured among the tempests of the sky, 
But melts away into the light of Heaven.

LACONIC SERMON. 

.Job i. ^h-'^'Wahed came lout of my mother's vtomB, and naked sftall 

J return thither,** 
IN discoursing from these words, I shall observe the three 
following thing^: — Firtt, Man's ingress into the world. SecontO^, 
His progress through the world. And, thirdly^ his egress out of 
the world. To return: Firtt^ Man's ingress into the world is

trouble and care. And, thirdly, his egress out of the world is 
nobody knows where. To conclude: If we do well here, we shall 
be well there; and I could tell you no more were I to preach a 
whole year.

Stephen -^treet^ Dublin* 
Beloved Brethren^ 

YOUR desire of cominunicatih|^ with the various societies 
throu^chout the world, (united in ttie bunds and profession of the 
faith of God and his Son Jesus Christ,) for the purpose of payin? 
obedience to the connmands of his written word, is worthy of imi- 
tation, and calls for the cheerful correspondence of all that have in 
view the same object. Should the Lord ever restore those enviable 
days, when the multitude of his disciples were of one heart and 
mind, it is probable he will employ sUch means as those uow re- 
sorted to by you. Alas! to human si^ht those days appear to be 
far distant. For thougii, by a comparison of our times with those 
that immediately preceded us, it would appear that Christ's ordi- 
nances have been brought to li^ht as though a copy of the laws of 
his kingdom had lately been discovered buried in the ruins and 
hidden under the rubbish of antiquity; yet does it seem that an 
adequate understanding of his laws is a discovery still wanted, and 
the spirit of interpretation rather to be desired than presumed. 
Whilst most disciples profess and lament this their ignorance, few 
are deficient in confidence: and in our impatience at the dulness of 
others, we all seem to forget the tarliness of our own progress. 
You will probably, in reply to your letter, receive communications 
from many bodies of disciples in this country, some of whom, 
though meeting in the same city, are well known to each other as 
brethren in Christ Jesus. Man^ members of these diifereni bodies 
were once united in association; but discussions having arisen 
among them, not concerning the principle of obedience to God's 
word and the apostolic authority, but concerning the meaning and 
force of certain precepts which perhaps for years were the subject 
of their doubts, they settled at length, each in the confidence of 
his own interpretation, and esteeming all difference to be disunion, 
they divided. On the contrary, we who now address yuu hold 
that difference is not necessarily disunion, and therefore it is that 
we deem disciples of this duy deficient in the spirit of the apostolic 
practice. That the apostles demanded implicit obedience to- a. 1 
their precepts without exception, we doubt not; aud all dibobc d.- 
ence must have been esteemed equivaleul to ql y^^^^Vw^w \»\ 

15 V. 5. 



vvxv:. 



trv THE CHRISTIAN 

niidiority deputed to them by Christ. But we cannot perecitre 
tliut ever}' misconstruction of an apostle's precept amounts to such 
:i rejection. The servant who knows not his master's will may be 
rliMinpfuished from the servant who knows^ and disregaitls it; it is 
clirilcult, in many instances, to ascertain the diiTerence, but surHy 
the maxim is acknowledged by the Lord liimself, and was acted on 
h} him towards his disciples. That errors can be imagined wl>ich 
the I.oi'd would not have borne with, we deny not; but that he did 
bear with errors, even at\et the plainest declaratior s, is most 
manifest. We doubt not, brethren, that all blindness, as to apos- 
lulic precepts, is chargeable on the folly and slowness of our 
hearts. The same folly and slowness of heart prevented the apos- 
lies from receiving many truths at the mouth of Jesus, but as their 
tally and slowness of heart was not indicative of a rejection of 
i^hiist^ so neither in these days do wc apprehend that the folly and 
slowness of professors to receive many truths in the apostolic re- 
cords, is in all cases indicative of a rejection of their authority; 
'A\\(\ as the Lnr<l bore with the apostles, we see not but his example 
v'.iii recorded for our imitation. 

In the apostolic days all mistakes concerning their precepts must 
speedily have been brought t« an issue, for their own explanation 
was not hard to be obtained; and after reference to them it would 
soon apoear who were disobedient and who acknowledged their 
yijthority. . Moreover, the practice of the various churches being 
then recently instituted, derived immediately from the apostles, 
and recorded in every pluce where disciples associattd, there was 
not equal room for mistake among them as in these days. Will any 
man assert, that now, after the lapse of centuries of darkness, 
(hiring which time antichrist has perplexed the meaning, perverted 
the language, mimicked the institutions, and obliterated the cus- 
toms of the first churches; during which time the records of the 
apostles have slept for near 2,000 years, and the very language of 
those records become almost hieroglyphic, and open to the access 
only of the learned; will any man, we say, consider this, and assert 
that diversity of opinion argues in all cases the same spirit of dis- 
obedience now as formerh ? Do they who carry the principle of 
U'liformity to the utmost degree of non-forbearance, do they doubt 
l!»at a cj-iminal blindness of heart obscures yet from their eyes much 
of the h<)rd*s will cojicerning his church? And do they conceive 
that their present attainments are the measure of universal pro- 
gress, and the criterion of faithfulness among all God's people? 
Therefore it is, brethren, that we of Stephen-street, in this ciiy, 
hold it to be very possible for a faithful disciple of Christ to deny 
or to acknowledge the doctrine of baptism— the kiss of charity—

1. THAT the apostles addressed christians as having their sins

In this we had advanced so far as to state that when Peter, to whom

The laws of grace are as sure in their operation, and as certain in 
their effects, as the laws of nature. When I put my finger into the 
lire, by a law of nature, it is burned: and just as certainly am I for- 
given of all my trespasses, by a law of grace, when ' in faith I am 
immersed in water into the name of the Lord Jesus. This is the 
apostles' doctrine, and to all believers in revelation, tliis being

brother Semple^ 

Dear Sir — THE 1o%'e 1 bear to all good men constrains me to 
address you as acuristiun brother, although you may scarcely deem 
ne worthj of such a compellation. But your being my christian 
brother depends not upon the Viil\ of the flesh, nor upon the will 
of man, but upon a higher and more exalted consideration. If you 
are a member of the ftmily of God, (and few doubt it,] and if I 
■hould be recognized by the Great and Good Master as of the 
household of faith, our brotherhood is fixed as firm as the founda- 
tion of the earth, although it may not be indisputably evident to 
all who are acquainted with our standing upon the earth. But 
antil I am proscribed by some new statute, I will claim my rigblt 
under an old statute of the reign of grace 49. 

1 love all christians, of whatever name; and if there Is any diver« 
city in my affection, it is predicated upon, or n&ther graduated by, 
the scale of their comparative conformity to the will of my Sover- 
eign. I do profess, before heaven and earth, to be a christian. I 
will claim this title, and defend it by that course of behavior which 
I think my Master requipes. This is the sole cause of my departure 
from the customs of my Baptist brethren in those items, whenever 
they have departed from the customs of those elder brethren, the 
primitive christians. If I could have been satished in my conaci- 
ence with that course which the populars pursue, 1 would have 
greatly preferred it; but I cannot. For I remember that the Judge 
once taught, **Wbosoever shall break one of the least of these 
oemmandments, and shall teach men so, shall be called ihe least in 
the kingdom of Heaven; but whosoever shall do and teach them* 
shall be called great in the kin^^dom of Heaven." 

You and some of my other brethren represent my views aa 
**chimeras.** Well, then, I, and thousands say. Prove them to be 
such. Nay; you say this is not necessary; it is enough to call them 
^chimeras ** Such is the meaning of your conduct. Now, brother 
Semple, yourself and some two or three other men of high staniK

Nothing which I have yet seen can induce me to doubt of the 
ultimate tvitiniph of the disting^iishing sentiments expressed in 
this work. All wise and good men expect a millenium, or a period 
of ^reat happinesa upon earth. They all argue that greater light 



BAPTIST* 183 

than that hitherto possessed will be universally enjoyed. They do 
not merely expect a universal subjugation of ail nations, kindreds, 
and tongues, to the Lord Jesus: they do not merely expect a state 
of harmony, perfect peace, and union amongst all the citizens of 
heaven; but they look for a vast accumulation of light and know- 
ledge, religious, moral, and political. They do not, however, 
expect a new Bible, or any new revelation of the Spirit, but only a 
more clear and comprehensive knowledge of the sacred writings 
which we now enjoy. This belief and expectation of all wise and 
good men, is unequivocally declaratire of the conviction that the 
scriptures are not now generally understood, and that there are yet 
new discoveries of the true and genuine meaning of these sacred 
records yet to be made. The misfortune is, that while all sects 
and sectaries make similar acknowledgments, no one supposes that 
himself or his people have any advances to make. The Baptist 
thinks that all the world will be Baptists in the Millenium, and 
therefore supposes that the Paido-Baptist sects will make great 
advances in knowledge before they can come up to the light which 
he enjoys. The Presbyterian expects that all the world will be 
Presbyterians in the Millenium, and consequently that many ad- 
vances by all the sects must be made in the science of church 
g^ovemment. The Metlipdist supposes that in the Millenium all 
the world will be Methodists, and anticipates the day when one 
great conference of profound radicaU will deliberate for the four 
quarters of the world. Thus it is that all hold fast their errors, 
never suspecting that they have any thing to oast away; and cal- 
culating that all others must come up to their standard, if ever they 
enjoy the millenial felicity. Thus every sect puts reformation and 
reformers from among them. Like some good hearers in our 
polite congregations, each suspects the parson means some lady or 
gentleman other than himself—and kinclly and politely thanks the 
parson for his benevolence, and invites his neighbor to take a slice. 
Amongst those who admit that they have somethings which they 
ought not to have, there seems to be a reluctance to begin to cast any 
thing off, lest the other sects would gain, or themselves lose some- 
thing thereby. Many cannot humble themselves so far as to admit 
that any thing they have held or taught has been wrong. And so 
it comes to pass, that if we are not getting farther from the com- 
mandments and institutions of the Lord and Saviour, we are not 
getting any nigher. The greatest paradox to my mind in my 
moral horizon is this: How any man can love our I^ord and Saviour 
Jesus Christ supremely, (and if he does not he is to be anathema 
maranatha,) and yet be indifferent about knowing or doing any 
thing he has commanded. For my part I must confess that if my 
orbit has been eccentric — if my career hitherto has been marked 
with any eccentricities, the cause is, my fear •f9ffending the great 
JLa-wgiver, and my detire to do hit xvilL la this I am now, as I have 
been for years, immoveable; and although I should be opposed^U 
the days of my life by some of the wise and good« as well %& b;? 
the ignorant and evil, I must perseNete. M^vj* «^^^ Vi ^'^'vl

SOLILOQUY— JVV. /. 

MEDITATION is a species of soliloquy, inasmuch as when we 
meditate or reflect, especially on serious subjects, we rather con- 
verse with ourselves. Some persons,' when deeply engaged in 
meditation, talk aloud, and not unfrequently argue and debate with 
themselves. Thus I have, when alone, frequently found myself 
carrying on a dialogoe betwixt myself and some penoiufiAd opi- 
nion, virtue, vice, or opposing interest. Some of these soliloquys 
I could have wished to have bad written down immediately on 
coming to a close, for in them I have sometimes had the best views 
of things, and heard the strongest arguments, pro and cos, on some 
subject of importance. A few 1 have penned down and may now 
and then submit one to the examination of my readera. The fol- 
lowing brief soliloquy originated from a temptation to be on the 
strong side: — 

How happy are they who sail with wind and tide down the stream 
of popular esteem, having the banks of the stream on which they 
are embarked lined with admiring crowds, waving their hats and 
bowing their heads in sign of approbation and admiration. How 
tranquilly they glide along. M'hen the sun shines and all is calm» 
how easy and happy their voyage. When storms arise, they betake 
themselves to the shore, and find themselves safe and happy in the 
caresses of admiring thousands. How enviable theyll Who would 
not desire and seek their happy lot. Contrast it with that of yonder 
amali company in a little bark, toiling against wind and current, 
ascending the rapid stream of vulgar applause. Ilow impercep- 
tible their advances. Afer whole nights and days of loilsonte 
rowing, they appear not to have distanced the shadow of a man of 
tall stature. No cheers nor congratulations from the spectators 
vho chance to cast an eye upon them trom the bank, except now 
Mud then a Boiituty **God speed'' from auii\e*Qbscure one perched 
upon some rock or island^ who has Yv\tosq\? Vi^^iv bvi^^v^^ \t\NJ^ 
Adrdships, 



BAPTIST. ^ ns 

Soch was the prospect before me while I Tiewed the landscape 
with the wrone^ end of the telescope next my eye; but all of a 
sudden 1 turned the other end, and strange indeed was the change 
in the scenery. I now could read the inscription on the colors of 
, the descending barg^ and that on the ascending skiff. I could see 
all deToted to present happiness, and those too who sought happi- 
ness in both worlds, on the side of those descending, but not one 
t)f the admirers of their course, nor of those crinbarked on that 
voyage, had yet died. I looked up the stream, and foundj from the 
inscription and other hieroglyphics upon the skiff, that their des- 
tiny was not to any port on earth, and that their eye was fixed upon 
some invisible and distant good, of such charms as to make them 
sing and triumph at every pull they gave the oar. A small compa- 
ny of the living and all that had ever died looked upon them either 
with perfect complacency — with a wishful, or an envious eye. In 
presenting the two rival courses of the whole human race thus to 
the eye of my mind, I could better appreciate the wisdom and 
happiness which distinguish the respective courses of the sons of 
men. But am I not, said 1, thus confounding my own reflections 
with a descriptive and s}mbo1ic representation of things addressed 
to the consideration of others? True, it appears so. But if I gain 
my end this way more readily, what is (he diH'erence? 

O my soul, dost thou not know that every good intention of 
thine, and every good effort of thine, were it only to subdue ci.e 
evil inclination, is witnessed with a<Imiration by all the excellent 
that ever lived. Dost thou not remember that the Saviour said 
there is foy in heaven over one reforming sinner, and even too 
amongst the angels of God, and canst thou think, that one good 
deed of thine is viewed with indifference by any of the exalted 
dip;nitaries of the heavens! When thoti makest one righteous 
effort to promote goodness in thyself, or in any human being, 
know that every good man on earth approves thy course, and is 
upon thy side; yes, and all the spirits of the dead. The wicked 
spirits know that thou art wise, and cannot but approve thy way; 
and all the holy and happy from righteous Abel, look down upon 
thee with delight, and congratulate thee on every advance thou 
canst make in goodness. Stronger and more numeious are those 
upon thy side than they that are on the side of thy opposers — 
AVhcn thou art tempted to consult thy reputation and thy worldly 
advancement amongst men> O reflect how little they can do for thee, 
and b( w much against thy happiness. Can they soothe thy troubles, 
can they heal thy woarfds, can they remove thy fears, or tranqullize 
thy agitations? No, no— full well thou mightest know, from thy 
past experience, how little they can do for thee. AVhen they 
once smiled upon thee and congratulated thee, were not thine acts 
foolish, and did not the very deeds for which they praised thee 
give thee pain? Hast thou not found thyself distressed beyond the 
reach of mortal power and earttiborn remedies to relieve; and 
wilt thou now. when God has smiled upon tht-e, pay thine homage 
to human adulation, and seek to please th6 proud and tt\e. x^\w V^v^ 
can not bless thee? No, my aou\, \Y\ou c^tv%\. w^\^\»iYev'iJ^T\'^

thine own felicity. "Will it not be more than a reward fof all pri-

have toiled with thee, and now approbate thy i^rogress; and when
