TO BISHOP R. B. SEMPLE i,ettebiv. 

Brother Scm/sle, 

YOU say that "the Bible is the charter of the corporation 
called the churchy and that this charter contemplates such regula- 
tions to be made by the church as answer t j the bye laws of cor- 
porate bodies.** This vs precisely, as I understand your epistles 
and your langu.ige, your views of the system of church govern- 
ment. To express this idea fully, you allow that "the charter is 
not enough** for the government of th« members of a church, but 
absolutely requires the church to make bye-laws. And you aver 
that when the church is making *'the bye-laws** she is to take care 
only of one thing, that she "does not violate the word or spirit" 
of the charter. This, then, is a very luminous and clear view of 
your plan of dhurch government. I am phased with its cleayness 
and intelligibility, though 1 have some formidable objections t« 
Guch a representation of the matter. I must always commend 
perspicuity and precision in dcfiaitions, althoupfh the definition 
when given may be every way objectionihle. That our readers 
may fully understand your definition, I will state the matter more 
fully; and, believe me, brother Semple, it will give me no little 
. pleasure to receive from you either a retraction or counter expo- 
sition of the matter. When a legislature grants a charter to a 
bank, a borough, or a manufacturing" conpany, it incorporates 
them into a separate independent body, with full power to manage 
their own concerns; and they may appoint and must appoint either 
directors, a council, or managers, who have full power to make us 
iTiny bye-laws as they please for the government of their body, 
provided thsse bye-laws are sanctioned by the charter; but the 
charier itself contains no particular or special laws for their govern- 
ment. It merely erects them into a body known in law, and grants 
tliem the privilege of legislation, and full power to enforce their 
own regulations and bye-laws; that is, the same power which the 
legislature itself pos&crsscs. 

The following are some of the more pro ninent objections to 
vour plan of church government: — 

1. 1 object to considering the Bible merely as a chatter ^^v^\.^*^ 
^v a legislature or civil goveriimen^jbecww^vL vV-i^Jix^X^ ^vi^'s. '^'^'^' 



242 THE CHRISTIAN 

than eredt congregations, or constitute religious bodies inTesteH 
with peculiar privileges. It gives them many laws for their general 
and particular behavior. It authorizes the existence of congre- 
gations, or, as you call them, "corporate bodies;" but it does 
more than any cbarttrever granted by any legislature ever did. 
It prescribes to the members in particular every requisite rule of 
behavior, for their thoughts, words, and actions. In fact it tran- 
scends any charter on earth in every respect: for if it was like 
other charters it ought to have left every thing, but the definition 
of the powers and privileges granted, to the management of the 
individuals incorpuruted. Now all the apostolic writings are filled 
with matter and laws entirely ^bversive of such a repiesentaiion 
4if the matter. The apostles taught christians a thousand times 
more than any charter teaches; and while the constitution of the 
christian church is laid down most fully in these writings, every 
important item of christian duty requiring the attention of chris- 
tians, either in public or private capacity, is also laid down. In 
representing the Bible, then, only as the chait^r of the church, 
injustice is done to it as great as 1 can conceive of. And the book 
IS divested of all its utility as regulating the conduct of individuals. 
For you know, brother Semple, that charters regulate public 
bodies, and not individual persons; whereas almost the whole 
New Testament is engrossed with the regulations, and rules, alid 
precepts which are to govern individuals. I am therefore con- 
strained to differ essentially from you in this part of your plan of 
church government. But 1 hope, when you more maturely reflect 
upon this matter, you will diB'er from yourself as far as I differ 
from you; and indeed 1 must say, that 1 think you will agree with 
this view of the matter, and that your public lectures to congre- 
gations are at variance with your whole theory. 

A second capital objection to your scheme of church govern- 
ment is, that it terminates in the same systems with those tiashion- 
able in Home, Constantinople, and Edinburgh. In giving to the 
church the incorporated powers oi legislation, even upon the 
subject of bye-laws, the question is, Do the whole church, male 
and female, old and young— or do the rulers in the church make 
these laws? Or do you use the word church in the classic sense of, 
Presbyterians, or the New Testament sense of a single congrega- 
tion? As a Baptist, I suppose you use it in the latter sense. Well, 
then, the congregation in Washington city, for example, is char- 
tered by the Bible, and authorized to make its own bye-laws or 
particular laws for the government of its members. The whole 
congregation must, then, make these laws, or their rulers. Now, 
to say nothing of the principles involved on either hypothesis, 
where do the sacred writings authorize or give directions for 
either? What command, law, or precedent, sa}s. You may make 
your own bye-laws or regulations? 1 must candidly say, 1 know of 
not one. If you know of any such, do, for the sake of the churches, 
declare it. The Presbyterians and Episcopalians, when pressed 
on this subject, have universally failed. The command, **Let al) 
things be done dec&ntly and in otdex," Yi«Ab««i\ o^^^ressed until 



BAPTIST. 343 

it has refused to carry one pound of bye-law3. For "the decency 
and order** are declared in the rolume. The 15th of the Acts 
absolutely refused to aid any of these copncils unless they could 
say that their decisions were infallible and sug^gested by the Holy 
Spirit. But if you will have a church representative of churches* 
in the popular sense, then you are off the ground on which the 
Baptists in former times always stood, and in union with the modern 
hierarchites. 

But a third objection to this platform is, that if the charter 
authorizes a congregation to legislate in matters of faith or prac- 
tice, it authorizes it to enforce, by proper sanctions, every act of 
disobedience or infraction of its bye-laws. What then are the, 
penalties? If no penalties, it all goes for nothing. And if the 
sanctions are enforced, then the decrees of the church are tanta- 
mount to the cou.mandments of the Head of the Church. Divine 
institutes and human enactments are therefore at par. But I only . 
giance at the incongruities of the scheme. 1 

As I do not think you were aware of what was involvad in this 
sentence, I will pursue it no farther and state no other objections 
to it until 1 learn that ybu arc disposed to defend it. These three 
arc, in my opinlSn, invincible. 

What the Sandemanians or Haldanians say or do, it matters not 
to me. I defend them not. I am not answerabie for their impro- 
prieties. 1 contend that the constitution of the church and its 
laws are found explicitly declared in the New Testament. And 
that in all matters of faith and christian practice it requires not one 
bye-Jiiw \Q am«Q4 or gdapt \X \9 any christian society. Aud if yovi 
call the appointment ot one bishop to four churches, a bye-law, or 
the annual meeting of delegates to regulate the internal polity of 
congregations, or the system of text preaching, monthly commu- 
nion, &c. &c. I say, if you call these bye laws, I protest against 
them as papistical and as antiscriptural as any of the dogmas or 
sacraments of the Roman hierarchy. 

I will finish my replies to your first letter in my next; and while 
I am discharging what to me appears an imperious duty, I beseech 
you, brother Semple, not to consider me in any other light than 
as faithfully and affectionately remonstrating against sentiments 
which I am convinced are of very injurious tendency and sub\'er« 
sive of the grand characters of the Divine Volume. This I do 
without one unkihd feeling for your person; and my reluctance to 
undertake this work was altogether owing to my high esteem for 
you as a good and great man, and a desire to have your co-opent« 
tion in a cause which is triumphing and must be triumphant as 
certainly as the promises of God are all Yea and Amen in Jesus our 
Lord. 

The public mind is aroused from its sitimbers. The day is past 
when old usages and loose declamations can be passed cur 
rent as the Oracles of God, or the decisions of reason. A' 
thorough, a radical, a mighty revolution is not now to begin. It 
has actually commenced. And it ia a& nwiv \a ^V«TOk\X\a Oo^-Ot^^ 
progreas as to forbid the appear^v^e ol\Ai-mQXtw«*^'^»W8^> '\:\«.

good and the wise do not wish to limit the expansion of the huissK 
mind; to retard the advancement of that happy period which }ou 
and I and millions more, every day pray for. I would rather be 
found rolling the stumbling blocks out of the way when the King 
Eternal calls me home, than to be called from the chair of the most 
magnificent establishment which the East or the "West ever saw. 

O Lord! hasten thou the glorious day, when the light of thy

had retracted the accusation and had made apologies to 
the person alluded to for the wound inflicted, and had 
fully exonerated him from the charge of being trouble- 
some to the churches. Having these documents and 
assurances I- thought itwas unnecessary and inexpedient 
to give publicity to a sentiment, which at the time I pub- 
lished these letters was disavowed by Bishop Semple ^- 
Desirous of promoting peace and good will among these 
brethren I did, then, order my compositor to expunge 
this obnoxious sentence from the close of Bishop Scra- 
pie's letter of the Sd of September. 

Yours affectionately. EDITOR.

IMMERSION. 
IN wpiting so much upon immersion under the head of the 
aMicient gospel, I am not to be understood as identifying^ christian 
immersion wiith the ancient gospel. Immersion we have before 
said is the gospel in loaten or the gospel exhibited in symbols the 
most significant and impressive. The truth to be believed is one 
ching, and the belief of the truth another. Both are prerequisites 
to immersion. The truth must be known and believed before we 
can be benefited by it And one item of this truth is, that the 
blood of Jesus Christ, God's only Son, cleanses us from all sin. 
Yet €od has made it accessible to us through water, as certainly 
as Jesus came by water and by blood. The virtue that cured all the 
blind, the halt and the maimed: the virtue that raised to life the 
dead, d\kelt in the. person of Jesus Christ; but something was 
necessary to elicit this virtue.. The will of Jesus was the only 
absolute requisite. But he was pleased to institute certain media 
through which this virtue was to pass from him into the frame of 
the dead or the diseased. The media through which this virtue was 
communicated was various, but universally se.nsible. A word to 
the ear, a look to the eye, or a touch addressed to the sense of 
feeling', are equally sensible, and were occasionally employed in 
the impartation of divine restoratives to the soqs and dai^ters of 
distress. As the electricity is drawn from the cloud at a. c^\V^vv 
'moment of time, luid by an e8UbVvaV\ed\BL'w vxv^\s^^xKa^.waV«^^^'^- 



246 THE CHRISTIAN 

80 the Kfiftorinf^ virtue in the person of Jesus was elicited &Tift 
eomraunicared at a certain instant of time by a law in the spiritu&l 
system^ aa firmly established as any law of nature. So it is in the 
impartation of the blessings of salvation to the souls of men. 
There is an instant of time, and a media through tvhich the Jhr^ive- 
mess of sins is imparted as -welt as the other blessing's ffro^inff out of 
adoption into tlie family of God, This point is worthy of much 
investigation, and capable of the clearest demonstration. That 
there is a definite instant of time in which all former sins are ab- 
solved, is generally admitted; but that there is any sensible means 
ordained b\ which this blessing is conveyed, is not so generally 
apprehended. When Peter and John were addressed by the 
cripple at the beautiful gate of the temple, (Acts iii.) Peter said, 
*^ Silver and gold have I none, but such as I h:ve give I thee: in 
the name of Jesus the Nazarene, rice up and walk." The virtue 
which was reposed in the person of Peter was not imparted in the 
pronunciation of the words, "Silver and gold have Inone," nor in 
the pronunciation of the words, **Siich as I have I give thee;" but 
in saying, **In the name of Jesus the J^azareng, rise up and -aalk" 
and at the instant he took him by the hand, the healing virtue was 
communicated. But why select particular ca^es, when it was 
universally the case since the time when God put the rod of wonders 
into the hands of Moses, down to the imposition of the apostle's 
hands, that at a certain instant of time, and by a sensible media, the 
powers called ** super naturuV cr "tniraculous" were exhibited. Even 
tjbe brazen serpent imparted no healing powers unless looked at 
by the stung Israelite. In respect to the remission of sins also in 
the rehgion of types, there was a definite moment, and an instituted 
way in which the conscience of a guilty Israelite was released. It 
would then be an anomaly in the history of the divine government, 
•a defect to which there is nothing analogous in the natural or moral 
systems, should it have happened that there is no time fixed, nor 
sensible means appointed for the remission of sins in the new econ- 
omy. Faith, indeed, is the grand medium ttirough which forgive- 
ness is accessible, but something more is necessary to the actual 
enjoyment of the blessing than a conviction that it is derived 
through the blood of Jesus. Hence those who had obtained this 
belief were commanded to be immersed for the remission of their 
«nff, or to arise and be immersed and wash atoay thi^sius, invoking 
the name of the Lord. The miracles wrought by Moses, by Jesus 
and the apostles, the sacrifices under the law, and the doctrine 
and commandments of the apostles, all eoncur in teaching us that 
there is a fixed time and instituted means in which all difine 
iuvorsare communicated. 

Prom the time when Moses ^'as shewn the glory of God, down 
to the close of the Jewish ages, it was know^n that the God of 
heaven was merciful and gracious, abundant in goodness and 
•ompdssion. But until Peter the Apostle opened the kingdom of 
"heaven, and announced the coronation of Jesus as Universal Lord, 
the mehDs by which this mercy was exhibited in the actual remis- 
sion ofsiasaa communicated to, wvl et\jo^ed ^B^y^wLn^xaittv, ^%a. 



BAPTIST- ' .247 

not clearly and fully developed. And one of the better promises 
on which the new economy is established, one of the superior' 
excellencies of the New Covenant, is, that under it the forgive- 
ness of sins is imparted, and the conscience perfected in and by 
means addressed to our senses, and of the easiest access to every 
believer of the philanthropy of God. So that the instant of time, 
and the means by which, the formal remission is granted, is an 
object of sense, and a proper subject of remembrance. Hence 
those who apostatized from the faith are said to have **forgotten 
that they were purjced from their old or former sins;*' i. e. sins 
committed before immersion. From which it is as clear asdemon- 
tration itself, that the forgiveness of sins was through some sensible 
means, or it could not have been a proper subject of remembrance. 

But the documents which the scriptures afford for the demon- 
stration of this most important fact, are as extensive as they are 
luminous and convincing. We shall attend to another illustration 
in the present essay. It is this: Jesus represents himself as the 
bridegroom; his people are compared to a bride; and their union 
is explained under the simihtude of a marriage. Now, we know, 
that if the relation between christians and their Lord be at all 
analogous to that of a husband and wifie, it must follow that some- 
ihing analogous to a marriage must be celebrated between them. 
This must be done at some definite period, and in some formal 
way. Hence persons are said to **put on Christ" as a woman puts 
on the name of her husband. We christians ar^ftliid to be married 
to him; and in consequence of this marriage we are invested with 
tin indefeasible right to all the honors, emoluments, and felicities 
originating from such an alliance. The property that christians 
derive from (his alliance is thus described by the Apostle Paul: 
««A11 things are yours, whether Paul, or Apollos, or Peter, or the 
vorld, or life, or death, or things present, or things to comej all 
are yours; and you are Christ's, and Christ is God's." Because 
we are Christ's, we have all things. So reads the inventory of the 
christian's estate. Amongst these "all things" we can easily find 
tlie forgiveness of our sins. This, then, becomes ours when we 
become Christ's; and if we formally and actually become Christ's 
the moment we are immersed into his name, it is as clear as day 
that the moment a believer is immersed into the name of Christ, 
he obtains the forgiveness of his sins as actually and as formally aa 
he puts him on in immersion. But as no woman is legally or in 
fact her husband's property, nor his property hers, until the marri- 
age covenant is ratified and confirmed according to law; so no 
person can legally claim the blessings of pardon and acceptance 
who has not been according to law espoused te Jesus Christy But 
so soon as the marriage is consummated, that moment the right is 
established and the blessings secured. And as nothing but a legal 
divorce can disannul the marriage covenant, so nothing but apos- 
tacy from Jesus Christ can alienate us from the rights and immu- 
nities guaranteed in immersion. 

Some persons have thought that because they did not unAei%<as^d, 
ihe import of christiiin immersion 9X l\ie Wme o1 >2s\«« Sskss«s»s8«^ 



)i48 THE CHRISTIAN 

they ouj^ht to be iminersed again in order to enjoy the blessings 
resulting from this institution; but as reasonably niight a womari 
seek to be married a second, a third, or a fourth time, to her hus- 
band, because at the expiration of the second^ third, an^ fourth 
years after her marriage, she discovered new advantages and 
blessings resulting from her alliance with her husband, of which 
she was ignorant at the time of her marriage. It is true she may 
regret that she lived so long in that state without enjoying the 
privileges belonging to her; but her having the rites of matrimony 
celebrated ten times, or once for every new discovery she makes, 
would give her no better right to these enjoyments than she pos- 
sessed through her first marriage. Nor will her repetition of the 
nuptial rites cause her to enjoy more fully the comforts of which 
she was deprived during the past years of her ignorance, than the 
mere consciousness that she now enjoys them. But of this more 
hereafter. We shall thank any of our intelligent readers for any 
objections they can offer to these essays on immersion so soon as 
we have brought them to a close.

ADDRESS 

TO THE READERS OF THE CHRISTIAN BAPTIST. 
THIS is one of the most momentous and eventful periods of the 
history of Christianity since the commencement of our recollection 
of the religious world, and, we think, from the commencement 
of the present century. All religious denominations are shaking. 
Caristians in all parties are looking with inquisitive eyes into the 
sacred books, and examining the platforms of their respective 
schismatical establishments. Many run to and fro, and knowledge 
is increasing. What religious sect is not at this moment waking 
from its slumberis.' Even the establishments of Rome, of England, 
of Scotland, fed and feasted as they are with political pntronage, 
and bolstered up with their charming antiquity, are not likely long 
lo retuin their place in the veneration of their own children. The 
peaceful Quaker and the dogmatical Presbyterian, the zealous 
Methodist and the orthodox Baptist, together with the little hosts 
of more recent origin, are all on the tiptoe of expectation, and the 
cry of "Uefbrm!" is now the loudest and longest which falls upon 
the ear from all the winds of heaven. Light mental, as light 
natural, is one of the most insinuating powers, and the most irre- 
sistible and rapid in its progress, we know any thing of. Its 
•*swi ft- winged arrows" pierce the deep recesses of human hearts, 
and carry down the true images of things to the retina of the 
hunaan soul. The Bible, the fountain of religious light, is more 
fCenerally distributed and more generally read now than at- any 
ibrnner period. Even the measures often designed to uphold 
religious sects, are becoming battering' rams to oreak down the 
walls of separation. Every day's report brings to our cars some 

*Jf the Rev, Mr, Smith, cf Kientueky, will produce the papert, er 
only one of them, loJdch hat ever proved me a liar in any instance, I 
will en^a^e to give Mm all my salary received fir preaehng dwiijj 
the last 15 years of my lifes and^ moreover, I will jrg security for 
some of his brethren in the United States, that they will pay him at 
least 1000 dollars for such a document, F^dx^'CH 



2iz I'HE CHRISTiAN 

n«w triumph of light over darkness — of truth over error-^and u 
liberal minds over the enslaved and enslaving genius of sectariin 
despotism. The very efforts and measures of the abettors oi 
sectarian schemes demonstf ate not merely the imbecility of hunr.in 
skill when warring against the light of heaven, but open to the 
slowest apprehension the corruptions which have secretly crept 
into the bosom of every sect. 

But of all the means which can be employed to promote peace 
on earth and good will among men» which have any influence \o 
desTroy sectarianism, or whicu are at all adapted to introduce the 
Millenium, there is none to compare with the simple proclamation 
of the ancient goipei. It was the proclamation of this which broke 
down Judaism and paganism, at first, and amalgamated men of nil 
religions in a noly brotherhood throu};hout the east and the west 
of tne Roman empire. This was mighty through God to the sub- 
version of all the strong holds of prejudice, error, and iniquity, 
which opposed the subjugation of the heathen to the obedience 
of faith. It was the substitution of human dogmas and specula- 
lions in room of this, which brought on the dark ages of papistical 
domination, and which to this day keeps up a sectarian spirit, and 
caters to the appetites of the demons of discord which have found 
resting places ready, swept, and garnished, in the inner temples cf 
every religious sect. As all the religions existing in the days of 
the Cesars, in the countries where the ancient gospel was pro- 
claimed, finally gave place to its purifying and associating influ- 
ences, so all the sects now in Christendom must g^ve place to that 
holy spirit which the ancient gospel inspires. The proclamation 
of the ancient gospel from the data before us, from the experi- 
ments already made, proves its perfect adequacy to this important 
end, and shews itself to be perfectly adapted to reconcile men to 
God and to each other. 

Of j»ll the religious e:^c;tcments which have been called Hevivalf^ 
of which we have heard, there is nothing that it is exactly similar 
to the influences which attend the proclamation of the ancient 
gospel. 1 do not particularly refer to the great ingatherings 
mentioned in a former number, in Kentucky, amongst Uie friends 
and proclaimers of the ancient gospel and the ancient order of 
things, because I am not so well acquainted with all the circum- 
stances attendant thereupon; but to what has been done in Ohio 
during the last few months, and what is still doing in sundry 
sections of that state. Many hundreds have received the ancient 
gospel within a few months, and have been immersed for the re- 
mission of sins, and have been filled with joy and peace in believ- 
ing. Some of all religious parties embrace it and turn unto the 
Lord, and it has wrought eflcctually in the hearts of all to produce 
the same benign and cheering influences. 

I would not, however, test the true merits of any scheme solely 
by its efliects on any partial experiments. Though this may be, 
and most generally is, the best proof of its true character. If we 
had always the fullest data which the nature of the case affords 
.submitted to our examination, we might then be fully able to de- 



BAPIIST. 253 

cide upon the merits of any scbeme by its actual success upon 
experiment. But this, from our limited information, is seldom, if 
ever, practicable. Reasoning, then, upon the nature of the mcaits 
employed, in addition to the trials made, and sometimes in the 
absence of experiments, is necessary to the formation of right 
conclusions. When, therefore, the obvious nature and tendency 
t>f any scheme, and the experiments made, concur in demonstrating 
its adaptation to the ends or objects in view, we are then in the 
possession of the best attainable means of deciding upon its real 
value. 

The proclamation of the ancient gospel, we all know, was the 
grand scheme of Heaven, to bring to nought all the false religion 
in the world. This is the highest commendation any thing can 
have. When God, the omniscient, and the all-wise, selects any 
means for any end, reason must humbly bow to it as the best in 
the universe. Now this is the feet, as all the intellrgent declare. 
The proclamation of Chris, crucified was both the wisdpm and the 
power of God to salvation, and to bring to the dust all the boasted 
wisdom of Jew and Greek in ameliorating the moral condition of 
the world. It is now the only thing requisite to usher in the 
M.Uenium, or the reign of peace and good will among men. 
In other words, the clear apprehension and general diffusion of 
the ancient gospel, is all that is necessary not to unite all sects/ 
FOR THIS Heaven uesions hot to do; but to grind to powder all 
sects and to destroy all sectarian feeling throughout the dominions 
of the Prince of righteousness and peace. 

Do you not see, my christian readers, that in all Revivals, as 
they are culled, the work of making christians is the al1-engros.sing 
work, and after the fiame is extinct, (and sometimes it is extin- 
guished by it,) then the siruggle to make sectaries. The go&pel 
xnakes the christian, and the schismatical theories make the sects. 
The preaching of the ancient gospel makes the christian; but the 
thetiieory of Calvin or of Wesley makes th* Presbyterian or the 
Methodist. In the language of one of our pious Presbyterian bro- 
thers, they must bring the new converts on **by degrees" to the 
spirit of the sect. Whatever real good is now done in the world is 
done by the simple narration of God's love of men, and all the 
mischief is done by the dogmas of human speculation or the reg- 
ulations of schismatical establishments. If t lie former is universally 
attended to and the latter abandoned, all christians would be one in 
name, in affection, in faith and hope. 

Why, then, contend for shibboleths.' Why fight about sp/cula- 
tions and schismatical sentiments, when their tendency is, and 
must necessarily be, to procrastinate the approach of the events 
for which we pray and ardently hope? *Tis surpassing stningc 
that we can believe ourselves sincei'e in praying for any tiling 
which we are not using the means to obtain; nay, often using 
means to prevent. Should we see a nation prepanngfor war, ur.d 
praying for peace, we would be led to suspect their .sincen*}. 
When then we see a people making new divisions and keepir>g 
up old ones, while praying for the MUkT».w\vim w NJcv^t vtwwws^ ^

lng« Some of all religious parties embrace it and turn unto the 
Lord, and it has wrought efl'ectually in the hearts of all to produce 
the same benign and cheering influences. 

I would not, however, test the true merits of any scheme solely 

by its effects on any partial experiments. Though this may be, 

and most generally is, the best proof of its true character. If we 

had aiirays the fullest data which the nature of the case aflfordi 

submitted io our examination, vie m\^ht.lhen be (ully able to de- 



BAPIIST, 253 

cide upon the merits of any scheme by its actual success upon 
experiment. But this, from our limited information, is seldom, if 
ever, practicable. Reasoning, then, upon the nature of the means 
employed, in addition to the trials made, and sometimes in the 
absence of experiments, is necessary to the formation of right 
conclusions. When, therefore, the obvious nature and tendency 
tof any scheme, and the experiments made, concur in demonstrating 
its adaptation to the ends or objects in view, we are then in the 
possession of the best attainable means of deciding upon its real 
value. 

The proclamation of the ancient gospel, we all know, was the 
grand scheme of Heaven, to bring to nought ull the false religion 
in the world. Ttiis is the highest commendation any thing can 
have. When God, the omniscient, and the all-wise, selects any 
means for any end, reason must humbly bow to it as the best in 
the universe. Now this is the fact, as all the intellrgent declare. 
The proclamation of Chris', crucified was both the wisdpm and the 
power of God to salvHtion, and to bring to the dust all the boasted 
wisdom of Jew and Greek in ameliorating the moral condition of 
the world. It is now the only thing requisite to usher in the 
M.ilenium, or the reign of peace and good will ttmong men. 
In other words, the clear apprehension and general diffusion of 
the ancient gospel, is all that is necessary not to unite all sects; 
FOR THIS Heaven designs wot to do; but to grind to powder all 
sects and to destroy all sectarian feeling throughout the dominions 
of the Prince of righteousness and peace. 

Do you not see, my christian readers, that in all Revivals, as 
they are called, the work of making^ christians is the all -engrossing 
work, and after the fiame is extinct, (and sometimes it is extin- 
guished by it,) then the struggle to make sectaries. The go&pel 
makes the christian, and the schismatical theories make the sects. 
The preaching of the ancient gospel n^akes the christian; but the 
thetiieory of Calvin or of Wesley makes the Presbyterian or the 
Methodist. In the language of one of our pious Presbyterian bro- 
thers, they must bring the new converts on **by degrees" to the 
spirit of tlie sect. Whatever real good is now done in the world is 
done by the simple narration of God's love of men, and all the 
mischief is done by the dogmas of huihun speculation or the reg- 
ulations of schismatical establishments. If t lie former is universally 
attended to and the latter abandoned, all cbribtians would be one in 
name, in affection, in faith and hope. 

Why, then, contend for shibboleths? Why fight about specula- 
tions and schismatical sentiments, when their tendency is, and 
must necessarily be, to procrastinate the approach of the events 
for which we pray and ardently hope? 'Tis surpassing strange 
that we Can believe ourselves sincei^e in praying for any thing 
which we are not using the means to obtain; nay, often u.siiig 
means to prevent. Should we see a nation preparing for war, ur.d 
praying for peace, we would be led to suspect their sinceri'j . 
When then we see a people making new divisions and keeping 
up old ones, while praying for the Mvl\ttT?.vuvrrev «t nJ«v^ vtwwsvs^ *^

of the Father, in his Will and Testament, when this 
state of things should cease had actually arrived; and 
now they were raised from the rank of slaves to the 
standing of sons 

So soon as a person has terminated his non-age, or 
minority, and becomes a full grown man, he is no longer 
treated as a child or servant. He is allowed to have a 
judgment of his own, and to exercise it. This similitude 
the Apostle uses to represent the difference between the 
people of God under the old economy, and the people 
of God under th€ new. Under the latter they are per- 
mitted to exercise their reason, aind to act from the 
principles infused into their miads from the develope- 
ment of the divme philanthropy. Hence the New Tes- 
tament, after stating the ordinances and statutes of the 
kingdom of Jesus, prescribes no ritual or liturgy, but 
leaves the worshippers to act from that holy spirit which 
the gospel inspires. Being adopted into the family of 
God, they are to be treated as sons of God, and are to 
act as the children of God. Hence none of the circum- 
stantials of the christian worship are laid down in the 
New Testament, as were all the circumstantials of the 
Jewish worship in the Old Testament. Take, for in- 
stance, the Lord's supper. The weekly and joint par- 
ticipation of the loaf and of the cup are clearly pro- 
pounded and commanded, in commemoration of the 
Lord's death. But no rules are appended thereunto 
regulating the sitting, standing, kneeling, or reclining 
of the members; no time of the day set apart; no par- 
ticular form of a table or the furniture thereof; no ar- 
rangement of the seats; no collocation of the disciples; 
no prescriptions concerning the quantity of either ele- 
ment to be used, nor adv'ices concerning what remains, 
&c. &c. All of thO'Se items would have merited atten- 
tion udder the old economy; but for the reasons assigned 
would be incompatible with the genius of the new. 
These or similar observations might be made concerning 
every item of the christian worship; but this sufiicientlj 
illustrates our meaning, and demonstrates the weakness 
of those who would lay down rules binding upon indivi- 
dualSf prescribing forms on these points which are left 
fo the discretion of chrisUans. ¥*\t\7 ^^^^>rev\^^ \\\^\^.-. 



BAPTIST, 2$7 

frire, OQ the part of any christian society to institute 
forms or models of the circumstantials, and to bind 
these upon individuals, or to require them in other so- 
cieties before they can fraternize with them, is an at- 
tempt to judaize, or, what is the same thing in this con- 
nexion of ideas, to bring into bondage to the spirit of the 
elements of the world. 

An attempt to find a liturgy in the New Testament, 
under the terms of '^express precept or precedent for 
every thing," is what subjected those called Sande- 
manians and Haldanians to so much censure from many 
good men. How far they carried this attempt it matters 
not, or whether they deserved so much repcoach on this 
account, is not the question; the principle itself, if at 
all admitted, must lead to a stiff, unnatural, and formal 
profession of the christian religion, and to a spirit and 
temper not exactly in accordance with the spirit of 
adoption, and of high* born sons of God. Most of those 
congregations which commenced their career with a 
good share of this spirit, and with the expectation of 
finding as much precision in the New Testament in 
Ja}ing down express commands or precedents for every 
thing, as was exhibited during the non-age of the religi- 
ons world, have since found their mistake, and have 
accordingly changed their course, and found a difierent 
spirit resulting from a change of sentiment on this im- 
portant point. While they have found all the instituted 
acts of social worship and of the discipline of the church 
clearly laid down, they have found also that the absence 
of that minutia of prescription as to time, place, and 
circumstances, which characterized the Jewish age, has 
left it necessary for them to possess and exhibit a toler- 
ant, forbearing, and condescending spilrit, and to make 
love the bond of perfection. 

In our next we hope to bring this review to a close.

THE TRIUMPHS OF SCEPTICISM. 

WHEN scepticism triumphs in any heart, the hope of 
immortaity is banished. It crowns the tyrant death for- 
ever on his throne, and seals the con\ue«t& <^€^\&Ai-^'«^^- 



258 THE CHRISTIAN 

OYer the whole human race. It wraps the tomb in eter- 
nal darkness and suffers not one particle of the remains 
of the great, the wise, and the good of all ages to see the 
light of eternitj. But consigns by an irreTer&ible doom 
all that was admired, loved, and revered in man to per- 
petual antiihilation. It identifies human existence with 
the vilest reptile and levels man to the grade of the mean- 
est weed whose utility is yet uodiscovered . Man^s ori- 
gin and his destiny are to its ken alike fortuitous, unim- 
portant, and uninteresting. Having robbed him of every 
thing which could make him dear to himself and proud 
of his existence, it murders all his hopes of future being 
and future bliss.. It cuts the cable and casts away the 
golden anchor; it sets man adrift on the mighty, unfath- 
omable, and unexplored ocean of uncertainty, to become 
the sport of the wind and waves of animal passion and 
appetite, until at last in some tremendous gust '^he 
sinks to everlasting ruin.'' Say then, proud reasoner, 
of what utility is your philosophy— what vour boast 

Tou boast that you have made man ignorant of his ori- 
gin and a stranger to himself. You boast that you have 
deprived him of any real superiority over the bee, the 
bat, or the beaver; that you have divested him of the 
highest inducements to a virtuous life by taking away the 
knowledge of God and the hope of heaven. Tou boast 
that you have made death forever triumphant not only 
over the body; but the intellectual dignity of man: and 
that you have buried his soul and body in the grave of an 
eternal sleep never to see the light of life a^ain — O scep- 
ticism! is this thy philosophy, is this thy boasted victory 
over the Bible! And for this extinguishment of light and 
life eternal what dost thou teath, and what bestow! thou 
t^achest us to live according to our appetites, and d<ist 
promise us that in thy Millenium man «hall live in a pa- 
radise of colonies almost as industrious, as independent, 
and as social as the bees. Well then dost thou preach 
with ^eal, and exert thy energies; for thy heaven is wor- 
thy of thy efforts, and the purity of thy life is just adap- 
ted to the high hopes of eternal annihilation. 

THE TRIUxMPHS OF CHRISTIANITY. 
A TRUE believer and practitioner of the christian 
refighn^ i> completely a!nd perfecWy dv^^^^t^d of a guilty

conscience, and of the consequent fear of death. The 
very end and intention of Ood's being manifest in the 
flesh, in the person of Jesus our Saviour, fvas to deliver 
them who, through fear of death, were all their life 
time subject to slavery. Jesus has done this. He has 
abolished death and brought iife and immortality to light. 
He has given strength to his disciples to vanquish death, 
and make them triumph over the grave— *So that a living 
or a dying christian can with truth say, O death, where 
now thy sting! O grave, where now thy victory ! He 
conquered both, and by faith in him we conquer both. 
This is the greatest victory ever was obtained- To see 
a christian conquer him who had for ages conquered all, 
is the sublimest scene ever ivitnessed by human eyes. 
And this may be seen as often as we see a true christian 
die. I know that a perverted system of Christianity 
inspires its votaries with the fear of death, because it 
makes doubts and fears, christian virtues. But this 
religion is not of God. His Son died that we might 
not fear to die, and he went down io the grave to show 
us the path up to life again, and thus to make us victo- 
rious over the king of tyrants, and the tyrant over kings. 
They understand not his religion, who are not trium-^ 
phant over those terrors of guilty man. The guilty 
only can fear, and the guilty are not acquainted with 
the character, mission, and achievements of Jesus our 
life. No one taught of God, can fear these horrors of 
the wicked. Jesus Ohrist made no covenant with death, 
he signed no articles of capitulation with the horrible 
destroyer. He took his armor away; he boand him in 
an invincible chain, and taught him only to open the 
door of immortality to all his friends. 

A christian then must triumph and always rejoice. 
Our gloomy systems say. Rejoice not always, but afflict 
your souls: whereas the apostles say. Rejoice in the 
Lord always, and again, we say, rejoice. The gospel 
as defined by the angels of God, is. Glad Tidingz of 
Great Jtiy; and who can believe glad tidings of great joy^ 
and not rejoice? Deists, Atheists, and the wnole host 
of sceptics may doubt^ for this is their whole system; 
tlys wicked, the guilty, and the vile ipay. fear, for this 
is the natural issue of their Iives\ but l\o^ ^^^^x^^^^ae^

knowlnorthe Lord, believing the promises, and confiding 
in the achievements ©f the Saviour, can doubt op fear 
as respects death or the g^rave. is inconceivable. Thanks 
be to God who gives us the victory! 

Some persons may doubt whether they are christians; 
and some may fear the pam of dying, as they would the 
tooth ache, or a dislocated joint: but that a christian 
should fear either death or the grave is out of character 
altogether. For this is the very drift, scope, and end 
of bis religion. They who are under the mflHence of 
such fears and doubts, have much reason to fear and 
doubt whether ever they have known or believed the 
truth, the gospel of salvation. But a christiaa in fact, 
or one who deserves the name, is made to rejoice and 
triumph in the prospects of death and the gr^ve. And 
why? Because hi!» Lord has gone befoire him — because 
his rest, bis home, his eternal friends and assi^cjates, 
his heaven, his God, all his joys are beyond the grave. 
Not to know this is to be ignorant of the favor of Gtkd^ 
not to believe this is to doubt the philanthropy of Godj 
not to rejoice in this is to reject the gospel, and to judge 
ourselves unworthy of eternal life. But the christian 
religion is not to be reproached because of the ignorance 
or unbelief of those who profess it. All rivers do not 
more naturally run down the declivities and wind their 
courses to the ocean, than the christian religion leads its 
followers to the sure, and certain, and triumphant hopes 
of immortality. EDITOR.

THE following query was sent up to a small Association m this 
state for an answers*^' 

"What must a church do with her preacher who has embraced 
Campbellism?" To which the Association in her wisdom, replied, 
"As we khow not what CampbelUsm is, we cannot tell her what 

to do." 

A correspondent in Kentucky asks me, '*What CampheUiam i»?» 
To which I answer: It is a nickname of reproach invented and 
adopted by those whose views, feelings, and dcairea are all secta- 
rian; who cannot conceive of Christianity in any other hght than 
an wm. riicae limt are now the real reproaches of those 
who adopt them, as they are the intended reproaches of those

are disclaimed, violates the express law of Christ. He speaks 
evil against his I rother, and is accounted as a railer or reviler, and 

g laced along with haters of God and those who have no lot in the 
ingdom of heaven. They who adopt them out of choice disown 
the Christ and insult him; for they give the honor which is due to 
him alone to the creature of the Devil: for all slander and detrac- 
tions are of the creation of the Devil. If christians were wholly 
cast into the mould of the Apostles' doctrine, they would fen 
themselves as much aggrieved and slandered in being called by 
any man's name, as rhev ^ou'd in being called a thief, a fornicatoTa 
or a drunkard. And they who bestow such names are actuated 
either by th«- spirit of foolish jesting, or that vengeful spirit which 
would sacriice the life as well as the reputation of those who de- 
prive them ol the means of self-aggrardizement at the expense of 
the intelligence, liberty, and true happiness of mankind. One 
uninspii td man's name weighs as much as another's when put into 
the scales of the sanctuary, and where good information and moral 
chsracter cxibis it is jus* as honorable: but r.o intelligerit christian 
could be pleased to be named a I'auHte, a Cephke, though either 
of these is a thousand times, ten thousand times more, honorable 
than a Calvinitt or Lutheran. But neither PkuI noi Peter would 
own that man as a consistent disciple of Christ who chooses to call 
himself by Paul. A polios, or Cephas. I have alwvyf^ disclaimed 
every thing sectaiian; srd if tie people of the different feels 
slander me or any of those w):o prcier the scriptures to any human 
creed, and the kingdom of JeMis the Messiah, to any sect; I say, 
if they slander us ^ith the ni>me« and epithets which we disavow, 
they must ans>Aer'to him who judges rightecus.y. But for cur- 
selves we protest agaihsf li.e name, the precepts, the icelings of 
any sect or schism in christend( m.

NEW TESTAMENT— SECOND EDITION. 

IN reply to many inquiries received from various sources con- 
cerning this work, I would inform the friends of this work that 
we are now advanced as far as the 80th page, and are progressing 
with steady pace. We have received many valuable criticisms 
upon the style, from Baptist and Paido-Baptist teachers The 
learned of all denominations, from whom we have received critiques 
concur in recommending thv. expurgation of all foreign words and 
obsolete terms and phrases, and advise the carrying out sliil farther 
in the second edition than in the first, the recommendations of 
Campbell and M*Knight, even in those instances where they did 
not carry out their own criticisms to their ultimate issue. We still 
solicit a continuation of their favors; and as it is by the intelligent 
and liberal of different parties perceived and admitted that this 
New Testament is the only one that can make any reasonable 
pretensions to being divested of a sectarian character, we hope 
that men of all sects will contribute their mite towards the render- 
ing of the work every way accepta^e to the devout and honest 
of all parties. 

We have been asked whether those yet subscribing will be 
entitled to the work on the same terms as those who have already 
subscribed. In answer to which we will inform them, that if the 
whole edition is not taken up before the 1st of August, we will 
allow all those whose names or orders are returned before that day, 
the work on the terms proposed to subscribers. But after that 
time, orders received will have to be filled at the price proposed

The mischief I have done, namely, that of creating a distrust ia

instructors, of making the laity read with more hopes of under-

CHRISTIAN IMMERSION. 

90ME say that we substitute -isatcr for the blood of Christ. Thifr 
'{% so far from fact, that we give no efficacy to water, but through 
tlie blood of the Saviour. Had he not shed his blood, all the 
waters which once deluged the world would be unavailing. They 
who say that faiih x» necessary to salvation, include neither infant's 
nor those who U'^ver heard of the Savi )ur. and argue that faitl* 
would be as unavailing as water, were it not for the blood of the 
Messiah. Vet they make faith neces:ary. Why then censure us 
/br making immersion necossary to out <iiv^o^me.T^x.^toc\B^>i«A^ 



BAPTIST. g69 

We, like tbem, neither include infiints nor those who hear not of 
the Saviour; and like them we make immersion nothing indepen- 
dent of the blood of the g^at sacrifice, and of faith in that blood. 
But we make immersion as necessary to forgiveness as they and 
'- ^ we make faith, or as necessary to our being entitled to the bless- 
' ings that are contained in the New Covenant, as they make sprink- 
ling or immersion necessary to admission into the church They 
will not (I mean Baptists and Paidobaptists) receive into the 
church unbaptized persons. We say that baptism or immersion is 
just as necessary to our obtaining the forgiveness of our sins, as 
they make it, to admission into the church. And if th^y will allow 
that there Is a possibility of salvation without faith, baptism, or 
admission into the church, why should they object to our remarks 
upon immersion, which are not more exclusive than their own» 
seein.^ they can take so much latitude after laying so much empha- 
sis upon faith, baptism, and admission into the church as to admit 
the possibility of salvation to infants, ideots, and pagans, remote 
from chriscian privileges. 1 now argue with them upon their 
own principles. 

In fact, I say no more than the Lord Jesus said, "//e that he- 
Kevcth and is immersed shall be saved " And he spoke only of 
them to whom the gospel was preached. 1 make immersion just 
as necessary as they make iaith, or as the Catholics and Protestants 
make sprinkling to admission into the church. The only differ- 
ence is, that 1 ^U'e to immersion with faith the precise import 
which the New Tcs'ament gives it; and they give to immersion 
or sprinkling, -without faiths a significance which it has not. I do 
earnestly contend that God, through the blood of Christ, forgives 
our sins through immersion — through the very act, and in the 
very instant; just as, they say^ God receives infants into the cove- 
nant or church in the very act, and in tlie very instant they are 
sprinkled. Their opinion 1 have lonff since shewn has no founda- 
tion in reason or revelation. We have shewn that the truUi, of 
which their views are a perversion, ij, that when a person believes 
in Jesus, and is immersed, he has obtuined in fac / and /orm, that 
which they ascribe to an unauiliorized tradition, if they have 
become more ashamed of this human invention ^h ui formerly, and 
will not say of it all that their fathers have said, namely, that a 
babe ia the act of sprinkling ^''toas regenerated unto God, and made 
an inheriter of the kingdom of glory;" 'f they have degraded this 
rite to a **mere ceremony;** and if some Baptists have made it mean 
no more than **making a profeesion,-** they ought to remember that 
their ancestors d\d not do so. 

We do most unequivocally connect immersion and the blessinj(S 
of the New Covenant, as explained in our former essays. But we 
object to our objectors, the injustice they do us in representing 
us as ascribing to immersion the efficacy of Christ's blood; seeing 
we declare that it is tr»rough faitti in his blood that we receive 
remiss.on in the act of immersion. Hence faitt and immersion are 
the media throu/h which these blessings are coivv^'^^^k va "^nr^ 
loiiidj of JBea M stated in our Wat. So vYv^x. S^^ ^^Vm\ w^^^vs^k*-

qf forgiveness, acceptance, adoption, and the gift of the Holy 
Spirit, are, by a gracious necessity, made consequent on a be- 
lieving immersion into the name of the Lord Jesus. But this we 
presume was explicitly developed in our last essay. 

With regfard to the promise of the £i ft of the Holy Spirit men- 
tioned in the second of the Acts, we beg the attention of our 
readers. The promise referred to in that discourse of Peter, was 
doubtless the promise quoted from Joel the Prophet, viz. "/ wilt 
pour out of my Spirit upon all Jleah; your sons and your daughters 
shall prophesy^'* Ice. This promise of the ^ft of the Holy Spirilp 
he represents as fulfilled on Pentecost in himselif and his associ- 
ates, who had before known and trusted in the Messiah; and as 
proposed to the present audience when they should believe, and 
be immersed into the name of the Lord Jesus. This is what the 
Apostle proposed to his inquiring audience when he said, **Be 
immersed every one of you into the name of the Lord Jesus for 
the remission of your sins; and you shall receive the gift of the 
Holy Spintf for the promise of this gift is unto you and to your 
children,'' &c. This g\ft of the Holy Spirit was precisely and 
definitely that which was promised by Joel, and not that which 
continued in the church after the age of spiritual or miraculous 
gifts expired. Peter in the house of Cornelius witnessed the 
outpouring of it upon the Gentiles, when he was called to call 
them; thus proving the truth of his own woQJs on Pentecost^ 
when he said tliis gift was not only promised to the Jews and their 
children who received the Messiah, but also to such others (the 
Gentiles) as the Lord the God of the JeWs and Gentiles, should 
call. Hence the Gentiles spake with tongues and glorified God 
before immersion; for this reason, that God designed to predicate 
their ^^o, as well as their rights to christian immersion upon the 
fact that he had bestowed upon them the ssime gifts he had voueb- 
safed upon the Jews, and thus established their claims for admis- 
sion into his family. If, then, we were to suppose that the gift 
of the Holy Spiiit pTomiatd to the converts on Pentecost conse- 
quent upon their immersion for the remission of sins, was the same 
as that now expected, it might with propriety be said that the 
Gentiles were not to be immersed fur the purpose of receiving- 
the gift of the Holy Spirit, inasmuch as God bestowed it upon 
them previous to immersion. But wh^^n we understand the gift 
of the Holy Spirit promiaed on Pentecost, ar.4 that bestowed oix 
the first converts from among the Gentiles, as the words import in 
New Testament usage, we are perfectly exempted from every 
difficulty and from any reasonable objection, in proposing to man- 
kind indiscriminately the remission qf sms and the Uoly Spirit 
through faith and immersion. For so soon as any person, through 
fuith and immersion, is adopted into the family of God, and becomes 
une of the 80718 of God, then he receives the Spirit of Christ; for, as 
says Paul, "Because you are sons, God has sent forth the Spirit of 
JiJa Son into your hearts, causing you to cry Abba, Father." This 
Js the Holy Spiritf which aU who axe wo^ \t«vu\«.wftA. UvcQu^h faith 
is Christ's blood for the remVasM^u q1 ^^ Tt.civi«^ w^^v^ %iK\^tiK\ 



BAPTIST. sri 

ii^ our 5th Essay on ibis subject. It is in this sense only that the 
phrase *^gift of the Holy Spirit" can now be understood. I havo 
always contended for affixing' the same ideas to the words used 
by the Apostles which they affixed to them, and therefore would 
prefer, in this instance, to use the words Holy Spirit or Spirit of 
God, rather than the phrase ^ft of the Holy Spirit, heme aware 
that this latter phrase is, in the New Testament, appropnated to 
what we now call "miraculous gifts," such as the gift of healini^ 
the sick, of speaking foreign languages, and of prophecy, 8tc. 
The phrases '*Spirit of hit &n,'* ••^Vif of Chritt,'' ^•Spirit of 
Holinen,'' "Spirit of God," '^Spirit of Love/' **the Spirit/^ *'Hofy 
Spirit," ** fruit of the Spirit," and **« Holy Spirit," are never used 
as equivalent to the phrase *'£rift of the Holy Spirit," When, then, 
we mean not *<spirituai gifts," but "thefndt of the Spint;" "the 
peace and joy in the Holy Spirit;" •'the Spirit of Christ,'" **thc 
spirit of faith, of meekness, of truth, of a sound mind," we ought to 
use such terms as were by the Apostles used to express those 
ideas, and not those which by them always meant something else. 

The first disciples, when immersed into the name of the Lord 
Jesus for the remission of sins, obtained this blessing. Those on 
Pentecost obtained also the very gifts contained in the promise 
made by Joel; and also all those communications couched in the 
, above expressions. For they not only possessed miraculous gii>s, 
but were filled with peace and joy, with all the fruit of the Spirit* 
of Holiness. 

How gracious this institution! It gives to the convert a oensihlc 
pledge that God, through the blood of Christ, has washed away his 
sins, has adopted him into his family, and made him an heir of all 
things through Christ. Thus, having his heart sprinkled from an 
evil conscience, and his body washed with clean water, he becomes 
a habitation of God through the Holy Spirit. Thus, according to 
the tenor of the New Testament, God dwells in him and he in 
God, by the Spirit which is imparted unto him. Thus he is cou* 
itituteda christian or a disciple of Jesus Christ. 

We are now prepared to consider any objections made to these 
tts^ys on immersion. EDITOR.

<lesire to eKcit truth regardless of all personal eonsiderations; and 
1 hope that all would separate with a very improved knowledge of 
human nature, and in consequence, with real charity and afifection 
fhr each other. • 

That which the friends who think with me wish to hare discuss- 
ed and decided, is— 

1st. Whether all reli^ons are or are not opposed to facts? 

2d. Whether all religions do or or do not virtually destroy all 
charity, except for one sect, in thought, word, and action? 

3d. Whether religion does or does not render it necessary that 
the great mass of mankind, in all countries, should be kept io 
Ignorance and poverty? 

4th. Whether all religions do or de not require that infants and 
children should be taught to think that there is merit in believinf; 
that the doctrines of their own religion are true and that all other 
religions are false; and that there is demerit in believing others 
wise? 

5th. Whether all religions do or do not teach that there is merit 
and demerit in loving and hating, liking and disliking, according 
to their doctrin§tt whether in unison with man's natural feelings 
or in opposition to them? 

6th. Whether almost all bad passions, vices, and moral evils, 
do or do not emanate from the instruction given in infancy and 
childhood, that there is merit and demerit in belief and in liking 
and disliking? 

7tb. And lastly, whether mankind can be trained to become 
more happy, more intelligent, independent, charitable, and kind 
id each other, with or without religion! 
I remain yours,

Sir, 

ALTHOUGH I cannot agree with you, that cliristian*

what shaken.

It is not true that the Christian Baptist "forbids the reading of 
any paper but itself." It is not true that it is read by **very few

CONCLUSION OF VOL. V. 
**A11 things are full of labor" was no new discovery in the 
days of king Solomon. Yet all things are regularly and inces- 
santly tendinis^ to certain ends and beginnings. The ceasless changes 
in ihe face of nature, the varied year, are but the symbols of 
that spiritual and moral variety wVv\cV\ cV\w^cV«-Vyi.^ \,V\«. world of 
minds; and ©very thing in the etnY>'\Te o? \\\oM^Vv\.\a€vOc^<t^\i«^^Ti- 



BAPTIST. 281 

iiiiig or ending some new condition or circumstance in the joys 
or sorrows of human being^. For all the rivers do not more cer- 
tainly run into the ocean than all our actions tend to make us 
happy or miserable either in the present state or in the future. 
Men often do, but nerer should forget that all their actions, which 
are the result of their own volitions, have a tendency necessary 
and unavoidable to promote their own joy or sorrow. And as 
nothing is final on earth, but only tending to eternity, we ought 
to know and bear in mind that we can neither enjoy nor suner 
the full result of our conduct while in the first act, in the mere 
prelude of the great drama of human existence. Our own happi- 
ness Itt misery is so intimately connected with that of others, 
fhAfef^ if any, of our actions terminate wholly upon ourselves. — 
The good or ill of human conduct is seldom or ever individual in 
its character or termination. All these reflections ought to ad- 
monish us of our great responsibilities, and should teach us that 
there is nothing more unworthy of us as rational beings than to 
act without deliberation and proper motives. We are only so far 
rational as we act in subordination to truth; and nothing is truth, 
but what is real. All the actions which are prompted by mere 
appetite, animal passion, or caprice, are purely animal, and be- 
long to us in common with other animals of inferior endow- 
ments. We only act the man when we act under the influence 
of motives drawn from the high relations in which we stand to the 
Creator and our fellow-immortals. Private and public good, men- 
tal and corporal, temporal and eternal* fill up the whole ranee of 
commendable actions. To lose sight of either, is folly— to keep 
the whole before our minds, is wisdom. Efforts designed and 
well directed to promote the more lasting enjoyments of rational 
beings, are of the highest order; and amongst the wise and good, 
are most highly appreciated. But it so happens, that, in conse- 

auence of the common blindness of men to their true interest, 
le imposing influence ofpresent fascinations, and the consequent 
error thereby introduced into the mind, there is nothing more 
generally disparaged than those efforts which are intendea to put 
men in the legitimate course to real enjoyment. Hence the op- 
position with which we have to conflict in attempting to direct the 
public in the acquisition of the true and lasting enjoyment of 
truth. For although truth is opposed to the happiness of no 
man, there are many to oppose truth. But they oppose it through 
mistake^ imagining it to be at variance with their honor, interest, 
or something identified with their happiness. I have long thought 
that truth is recommended merely because it necessarily tends to 
happiness, and 1 do not know any thing in the volume of super- 
natural truth which is not in its very nature promotive of the true 
happiness of all who know and obey it. Being aware of this, and 
being assured that all error ends in misery, we have occupied 
ourselves now for {i\e years in directing thu public attention to 
what we have learned to be the most important truths bearing 
upon the actual condition of our co\.em^\iT«t\«.^. K\A\tv Ovs^iv^^?, 
this fifth volume of our labors^ >xe c^Tvw^XT^ltivcv^^wa.^^'^'^'^^^^ 



282 THE CHRISTIAN 

• * 

our readers tl^nt all their happiness consists in knowing and obev' 
ing truth. Error is the most unprofitable commodity in the whole 
universe— and the sooner it is detected, the better. 
'' We oppose error because, it opposes happiness. We are op- 
posed by the same sort of characters who have always opposed 
reformation; and for the same reasons. There have been millions 
of the human family kept in vassalage, religious, mora), and 
political; and myriads hare fattened upon them» merely through 
the influence of error. And now, even now, if error was de- 
tected, how many who arc lording it over the consciences of men 
and rioting iu insolent euse, would be divested of their influence 
and living^, and would sink down to their proper level in.aMiety. 
How many useful persons would arise and difluse the bletifngt of 
light and liberty far and wide. But so long as the popular errors 
of the day arc patronized and triumphant, both the proper deve- 
lopemcnt of the human mind and the enlargement of human 
happiness, must continue impracticable. And until men's ears 
are turned from theological fables to the oracles of God, we 
cannot expect better times or a greater augmentation of human 
enjoyment. 

We intend continuing our exertions, with increased energy, in 
this cause; and hope never to be leas deserving of public patron- 
age than we have hitherto been. EDITOil.

PERSONS at a distance, living in the vicinities where sub* 
scriptions for the 2d edition of the New Testament have been 
taken, who wish to have complete sets either of the Sd edition 
of this work, or of the whole five volumes now published, neatly 
bound and lettered, could, by an early application, have them 
bound and forwarded with the New Testaments next fall. Those 
who have already ordered complete sets for their family libraries, 
are informed that they will be sent on with the Testaments. 
There are a number who, at diiFerent times, have applied for 
bound vohimes to be sent by mail, but who did not receive them, 
because they could not be sent in that way. If they will now 
order the work, they shall be supplied as above proposed. The 
friends and patrons of this work would do well to have their 
volumes bound at the close of every year in their respective vicini- 
ties. Those who are desirous of possessing complete sets of the 
whole work, or of the 2d e:dition of the three first volumes, either 
bound or in sheets, would find it to their advantage to apply soon, 
as we cannot, lu all probabihty, for a long time undertake a new

"BLOW AT THE ROOT OF POPERY." 

A PAMPHLET was some weeks since forwarded from Ken- 
tucky to this office, written by the Rev. Mr. Steel, of Winchester, 
Ky. on the subject of Infant Sprinkling. A request to review it 
accompanied it; but as I have studiously kept that obsolete con- 
troversy out of this work, I could not trouble my readers with the 
old tale about circumcision. Before I had leisure to read the 
pamphlet all through, I was favored with a reply to it, a copy of 
which I have carefully read. It is titled as above; and it is really 
an efficient **liloiu «♦ the i*oot of popery^* and a perfect refutation 
of Mr. Steel's pamphlet. Those interested in such matters, will 
find themselves remunerated in perusing the said pamphlet. I 
understand they are for tale in the priaci^al tQ\v\v& v\ !sw<!i'Cv^>^Oc\ .

284 THE CHRISTIAN BAPTIST. 

Extract of a letter to the Editor, 

"ONE thing, however, I was more than a little surprised at— U> ' 
hear so many, and of so many, talking about the church coming 
out of Babylon, and the restoration of primitive Christianity and 
order; and to see and near of so little exertion made to bring it 
about; and even its warmest advocates stating that it would not do 
to emerge suddenly. It brought fresh into my mind a circum- 
stance that transpired when I was a little boy, that 1 have often 
thought of w ben viewing the christian church reecding from the 
right rule (ihn Scriptures) in measuring themselves by themselves: 
An old uncle of mine, who had use for a number of wooden pins, 
set me to sawing up tough rails into blocks for that purpose. He 
sawed off one as a measure for me to saw by and w^nt away. I 
commenced. I put the measure on for the first block. After 
starting the saw 1 threw down the measure, and held the block I 
I was sawing wiih one hand till 1 cut it off; then made use of it to 
n.easure the next, and so on until I had sawed a great many. My 
uncle returned and told me I was spoiiirg his timber. I told him 
I did not know how that could be. Wiiere is the measure I gave 
you.' he said. 1 replied, 1 measured ihe first block by it, the next 
by the last one, and so on, and when I touka good look, I found I 
had about as many lengths as blocks. He recommended the pro- 
priety of hurting up the first block that he gave me, and reqtiest* 
ed me to preberve it as a measure." N. P.

ONE Methodist and two Universalist teachers, or "ministers," 
as they are called, in Ohio, of good standing and respectable at- 
tainments, have recently renounced their favorite isms, and have 
been immersed into the belief of the ancient gospel. 

(jjfSEVERAL communications intended for this number, have 
been laid over for the next volume; among which is one on some 
supposed discrepancies between Matthew and I.uke.
